



Web Authorization Protocol                                 K. McGuinness
Internet-Draft                                               Independent
Intended status: Standards Track                         30 October 2026
Expires: 3 May 2027


               Mission-Bound Authorization for OAuth 2.0
                 draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-latest

Abstract

   OAuth 2.0 has authorization grants and access tokens but no standard
   object that binds an approved task, such as one an AI agent performs
   on a user's behalf, to the authority approved for it and governs the
   tokens later issued under that authority.  This document defines that
   object for OAuth 2.0: a Mission.

   A client submits, through Pushed Authorization Requests, a Mission
   Intent describing the task it will perform.  The authorization server
   derives Rich Authorization Requests authorization details from it,
   binds the approved task and that authority to the Approver's consent
   through integrity anchors, and records a durable Mission.  Every
   access token issued under the Mission carries a subset of the
   approved authority and a "mission" claim, and every issuance is gated
   on the Mission's lifecycle state.  Revoking a Mission stops further
   issuance; it does not recall tokens already issued, which a resource
   server that enforces from the token alone can continue to honor until
   they expire.

   Optional capabilities represent delegation among agents with the
   OAuth Actor Profile and, as specified by a companion, let a single
   Mission be honored across trust domains.  Runtime enforcement of each
   action is a separate, optional layer.

1.  Introduction

   This document defines a *Mission*: a durable authorization object
   that binds a disclosed task to an explicitly approved Authority Set
   and governs the lifecycle of the authority derived from that
   approval.

   Agent-identity work such as [I-D.draft-ietf-wimse-aims] establishes
   how an AI agent authenticates and how a user delegates authority to
   it.  It leaves out of scope how the agent's task is translated into
   authorization, how the user's approval of that task is captured, and
   how later token issuance stays bound to that approval.  OAuth itself
   defines no task record that links approval of a task to the grants
   obtained for it: an authorization_details entry ([RFC9396]) expresses
   authority, but not the task it serves or a lifecycle of its own, so
   withdrawing approval of a task need not stop issuance under its
   grants.

   A Mission fills that gap.  A client submits a Mission Intent
   describing the task, the authorization server (AS) derives the
   Authority Set the task needs, and an Approver consents to that
   Authority Set. The AS records the Mission and binds the resulting
   grant to it.  It then issues tokens under the Mission only while the
   Mission is active, each bounded by the approved Authority Set, and
   resource servers enforce the resulting tokens.  Revoking or expiring
   a Mission stops further issuance and refresh, but does not by itself
   invalidate an already-issued access token (Section 10.2).  Section 2
   shows the protocol flow and an example.

   The sections follow this flow.  Section 4 and Section 5 define what a
   client submits and what the AS derives from it; Section 6 the
   approval, the record, and the grant binding; Section 7 issuance on
   every path; Section 8, an optional capability, delegation within a
   Mission; Section 9 enforcement and introspection; and Section 10 the
   Mission's state and its termination.  Section 11, Section 12,
   Section 13, and Section 14 are reference sections; Section 15 defines
   the conformance roles; and Appendix B lists what a deployment decides
   and how each party learns it.

1.1.  Applicability

   This document targets OAuth deployments where authority serves a
   durable, approved task that spans more than one token, request, or
   audience: an agent pursuing a multi-step objective on a user's
   behalf, or a workflow whose audit must join activity across hops on a
   shared task.

   This document is not intended for single-request user flows and
   short-lived authorizations where the credential's lifetime is the
   task's lifetime, ordinary machine-to-machine service credentials
   among them; those use OAuth unchanged.  The boundary is that lifetime
   equality, not the absence of a human: a workload's durable multi-step
   task is in scope as a service-owned Mission (Section 6.1.7).

   The direct realization creates each Mission at an approval
   interaction on the authorization endpoint, and a Mission is intended
   to cover one concrete task, not an agent's whole lifetime.
   [I-D.draft-mcguinness-mission-architecture] maps the paths where an
   administrator, or a policy a human consented to, decides without that
   interaction (Section "Mission Creation Paths"), and how a deployment
   sizes its Missions (Section "Mission Granularity").

2.  Overview

2.1.  Protocol Flow

   The following figure shows the protocol flow between the agent (the
   OAuth client) and the Mission Issuer (the AS); Section 3 defines the
   Subject, the Approver, and the other terms:

    Agent (client)                       Mission Issuer (AS)
         |                                     |
         | 1. PAR: intent + proposal --------->| derive authority
         |<----------- request_uri ------------| (authz_details)
         |                                     |
         | 2. authorization request ---------->| Approver consents
         |                                     |   -> authority_hash
         |<-------------- code ----------------| -> Mission active
         |                                     |   (bound to the grant)
         |                                     |
         | 3. token request ------------------>| gate: active?
         |<----------- access token -----------| + authz_details
         |                                     |   + mission claim
         v

   1.  *Submit.* The client pushes a Mission Intent describing the task,
       optionally with proposed authorization_details (Section 4.2).
       The AS derives the Authority Set, the concrete authority the task
       needs (Section 5).

   2.  *Approve and commit.* The Approver consents to that Authority Set
       itself, not to the task description alone (Section 6.1).  The AS
       records the active Mission, commits the Intent as intent_hash and
       the approved Authority Set as authority_hash (Section 6.4.1), and
       binds the grant the approval produces to the Mission server-side.
       Any grant lineage bound to a Mission resolves to exactly that
       Mission, and no client input chooses it (Section 6.5).

   3.  *Issue.* At each token request the AS checks that the Mission is
       active and derives authority no broader than the approved
       Authority Set (Section 7.1, Section 5.4).  The access token
       carries the derived authorization details and a mission claim
       identifying the Mission (Section 7.8), and a resource server
       enforces statelessly from the token (Section 9.1).

   Each later issuance from that grant lineage, such as a refresh, is a
   derivation under the same checks, and a token derived by Token
   Exchange carries the same mission claim (Section 7.5, Section 8.2).
   Revoking or expiring the Mission stops further issuance and refresh,
   including from any grant lineage bound to it (Section 6.5.3).  An
   already-issued access token can still be honored on a stateless
   enforcement path until it expires (Section 10.2).  A user thus
   approves a task's authority once, and that approval bounds every
   token the agent derives.  Section 2.2 walks through these steps with
   concrete values.

2.2.  Introductory Example

   In the examples in this document, each service's operation contract
   identifies the service's own identifier, such as
   https://erp.example.com, as the operation target
   ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-resource-access]).  Actions and
   constraints determine the permitted operations within that service.

   The following example shows a registered client reading invoices from
   one ERP resource for Alice, who is both the Subject and the Approver;
   the authority source is user-delegated.  The AS and ERP support the
   mission_resource_access type defined by
   [I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-resource-access].  The example
   uses a direct approval, a JWT access token, no delegated token, and
   no submission evidence (the AS's admission policy requires none).
   All times are UTC on the same day.

   1.  *Submit.* The client pushes a Mission Intent naming
       https://erp.example.com and requesting expiry at 13:00, with a
       proposed authorization_details entry of type
       mission_resource_access for that resource and the action
       invoices.read.  It follows the PAR request_uri into the
       authorization interaction (Section 4.2).

   2.  *Approve and commit.* At 12:00 the AS authenticates Alice,
       verifies the authority source and proposed read authority, and
       renders that authority and the effective 13:00 expiry for her
       approval.  On approval it atomically creates the active Mission,
       records its commitments, and binds the authorization code to it
       (Section 6.1.2, Section 6.5.1).

   3.  *Issue.* Still at 12:00, the client redeems the code.  The AS
       resolves and checks the Mission and returns a sender-constrained
       ERP token that expires at 12:05, with the read authority and a
       mission reference.  The response also carries the granted
       authority (Section 7.3), mission_id, and mission_expires_at; the
       access token's five-minute lifetime is distinct from the
       Mission's one-hour lifetime (Section 7.3.1).

   4.  *Use.* The ERP is a Mission-aware Resource Server: it enforces
       the mission_resource_access entry itself, work a plain [RFC9068]
       resource server does not do (Section 15).  It validates the token
       and proof of possession and permits the authorized read; the
       token grants no write authority.  The resource request involves
       no Mission-state lookup or complete-set retrieval (Section 9.1).

   5.  *Stop further issuance.* At 12:02 an authorized revocation makes
       the Mission non-active.  A subsequent refresh, if the AS issued a
       refresh token, is refused with the invalid_grant error code
       (Section 7.1).  The already-issued access token can still be
       honored on this stateless path until 12:05; revocation does not
       undo a completed read (Section 10.2).

   An ERP that instead introspects on every request stops honoring the
   token on its next request after revocation, through the composite
   active result (Section 9.2.2).

   The later message examples use the end-to-end example's separate Q3
   reconciliation Mission (Appendix A), whose approved authority
   includes invoice reads and bounded journal writes.  Its identifiers
   and timeline differ from this introductory example.

3.  Conventions and Terminology

   The key words "MUST", "MUST NOT", "REQUIRED", "SHALL", "SHALL NOT",
   "SHOULD", "SHOULD NOT", "RECOMMENDED", "NOT RECOMMENDED", "MAY", and
   "OPTIONAL" in this document are to be interpreted as described in
   BCP 14 [RFC2119] [RFC8174] when, and only when, they appear in all
   capitals, as shown here.

   All JSON shown in this document is non-normative and illustrative;
   the member definitions in the surrounding text are authoritative.  An
   example line too long for the page is folded with the single
   backslash strategy of [RFC8792].

3.1.  Terminology

   Agent (Client):  The OAuth client acting for the Mission's Subject,
      identified by client_id.  Agent identity is established per
      [I-D.draft-ietf-wimse-aims] or ordinary OAuth client
      authentication.

   Subject:  The user, workload, or organizational principal on whose
      behalf the Mission is approved (Section 6.1.7), identified by an
      (iss, sub) pair and carried in derived tokens' sub claim.

   Approver:  The single accountable principal who approves the Mission:
      at its direct approval event or, under a standing-consent basis,
      through the earlier standing consent (Section 6.2).  Equal to the
      Subject for self-approval; different for administrator or
      delegated approval.  Multi-party approval and the provenance of
      delegated approval authority are out of scope (Section 6.1.9).

   Mission Issuer (authorization server):  The OAuth AS that validates a
      Mission Intent, runs the approval event, records the Mission, and
      derives tokens.  It is the Mission's issuer; this document also
      calls it the "issuer AS" or the "AS".

   Mission Client:  The client conformance role, defined in Section 15.

   Mission-governed client:  A client that the deployment registers as
      Mission-governed ("a client registered as Mission-governed");
      Section 4.3 states the rule that registration triggers.

   Mission-aware Resource Server:  The resource server conformance role,
      defined in Section 15.

   Resource AS:  An authorization server in another trust domain that
      honors a Mission it did not issue, minting its own tokens for its
      resources; it is never the Mission Issuer.  The companion Mission
      Cross-Domain Projection profile specifies cross-domain projection
      ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-cross-domain]).

   Mission Intent:  The structured description of the task the client
      submits (Section 4).

   Mission Intent Submission (Submission envelope):  The object a client
      submits as the mission_intent parameter value: the Mission Intent
      under intent, and Intent Submission Evidence under evidence where
      any is presented (Section 4.2).

   Intent Submission Evidence:  Typed artifacts a client presents in
      support of claims about a submitted Mission Intent (Section 4.4):
      authenticated policy input, not authority.  The term names only
      inbound, client-presented material, not the issuer- or runtime-
      produced evidence this document and companion profiles emit and
      record (a consent-evidence artifact, an audit evidence base).

   Authority Proposal:  The authorization_details array a client submits
      alongside a Mission Intent, a proposal for Authority Set
      derivation and not authority (Section 4.3).

   Authority Set:  The set of authorization_details entries the AS
      derives from a Mission Intent and the Approver approves
      (Section 5).

   Authority Set derivation:  Computing the Authority Set from a Mission
      Intent and any authority proposal, in narrowing or configured-
      mapping mode (Section 5.1).

   Derivation:  One issuance operation the issuer AS performs for a
      single request (Section 7.1): the initial authorization-code
      exchange, a refresh, a Token Exchange, a cross-domain grant
      issuance, or the redemption of a profile-defined grant, such as
      the Template profile's handoff grant.  Distinct from Authority Set
      derivation, which some section titles and fixed phrases, such as
      "derivation policy", also call derivation.

   Mission:  The durable authorization object that binds a disclosed
      task (its Mission Intent) to an explicitly approved Authority Set
      and governs the lifecycle of the authority derived from that
      approval.  It is the record created at the approval event
      (Section 6.3), immutable except for its state and termination
      members, identified by a Mission Identifier (Section 6.3.5) and,
      globally, by the pair (issuer, id).  A Mission is independent of
      any OAuth grant (Section 6.5).  "The approved task" is shorthand
      for this binding: the Approver approves the Authority Set, and the
      task is disclosed and committed beside it.

   Mission grant binding (grant binding):  The AS-controlled, functional
      mapping from one persistent, redeemable grant lineage to exactly
      one Mission (Section 6.5).  Distinct from a Derived token's own
      mission claim, which every Derived token carries without itself
      creating a grant binding.

   Mission-referenced token:  A token that carries a Mission reference
      (for example, a mission_id or a descriptor a companion profile
      defines) without Mission-derived authority or any gating
      guarantee.  An access token of this class does not carry the
      mission claim (Section 7.8.1).

   Derived token (Mission-derived token):  An access token issued under
      a Mission, carrying its Mission-derived authority (the full
      Authority Set or a narrowed subset) as authorization_details and a
      mission claim (Section 7).

   Mission-bound token:  A Mission-derived access token or refresh token
      whose issuance and refresh are gated on the Mission's active state
      and bounded by the subset rule (with refresh tokens bound server-
      side).  Only this class carries the gating guarantee of this
      document; a token that only references or carries Mission data
      without the gates is not Mission-bound (Section 15).

   Applicable entry:  An authorization_details entry that applies, under
      the entry's type, to a request to a resource server: where the
      type defines a resource, the request's operation target matches it
      under that type (Section 7.3).  Section 9.1.1 governs an entry
      whose type the resource server does not implement, or whose type-
      defined enforcement it cannot complete.

   Delegate:  A sub-agent, service, or tool, itself an OAuth client
      authenticated as a distinct actor, that obtains a delegated token
      by Token Exchange (Section 8).

   Delegating party:  The holder of a Mission-bound access token that
      obtains a designation for a delegate: the Mission's approved
      agent, or a delegate delegating further (Section 8.1).

   Designation:  The delegating party's authorization, obtained by Token
      Exchange, for one delegate to obtain delegated tokens
      (Section 8.1).

   Delegated exchange (delegated token):  The Token Exchange in which a
      delegate redeems a designation naming it for a delegated token,
      whose act claim names that delegate as the current actor
      (Section 8.2).

   Self-exchange (no-actor exchange):  A Token Exchange with no actor in
      which an agent presents its own Mission-bound access token to
      obtain a narrowed one; it delegates nothing (Section 7.5).

3.2.  Principal Model

   This document maps principals onto native OAuth constructs:

   *  The *Agent* is the OAuth client, referenced by client_id; its
      identity and credentialing are out of scope
      ([I-D.draft-ietf-wimse-aims]).

   *  The *Subject* and *Approver* are each an (iss, sub) pair, matching
      the access token sub model of [RFC9068].  The Approver is the
      accountable consent principal whose approval created the Mission,
      always equal to approval_basis.consent_principal.  Under a
      standing-consent basis, the Approver remains the human whose
      consent roots the Mission (Section 6.2).

   On a derived token, the iss claim is the AS and the sub claim is the
   AS-local sub to which the AS maps the Subject under the injective
   mapping of Section 6.1.3; adopting the external sub verbatim is the
   common case.  Within the issuing AS's namespace, this (iss, sub) pair
   is the AS-local subject principal, authoritative for the Subject, and
   resource servers authorize against it.

   The Mission separately records the Subject's home issuer and
   identifier as subject.iss and subject.sub: the external subject
   identity, carried as provenance for audit and not on the token.  This
   document defines no runtime lookup of it.

   The record's (subject.iss, subject.sub) and the token's (AS iss, sub)
   identify the same Subject in two issuer namespaces.  Across trust
   domains, the cross-domain companion conveys Subject identity through
   its own subject-resolution claims, not a Mission lookup, and a token
   minted in another domain preserves the mission claim unchanged
   ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-cross-domain]).

   Issuer roles obey two invariants: a Mission has exactly one Mission
   Issuer, its issuer, and a Resource AS never creates or alters a
   Mission.

   Principals are recorded at the approval event and are immutable.  Two
   principals are equal when their iss and sub are byte-equal, so the
   test compares principals only within one issuer namespace and never
   compares the external subject identity with the AS-local subject
   principal.

   Dynamic delegation (the actors an agent delegates to during
   execution) is carried on derived tokens in the act chain
   (Section 8.2), not on the Mission Record.  This document uses only
   (iss, sub) pairs, not the subject identifier formats of [RFC9493].

4.  Mission Intent

   A Mission Intent is a JSON object describing the task.  The client
   submits it as the intent member of the Submission envelope
   (Section 4.2), in place of scope or alongside a narrowed scope.

   Before the approval event (Section 6.1), only the Mission Intent
   exists, as untrusted client input (Section 4.2); after it, only the
   Mission is authoritative.  A Mission Intent therefore has no protocol
   identifier of its own: two submissions of the same Intent produce two
   distinct pending requests, and a Mission acquires its Mission
   Identifier (Section 6.3.5) only at activation.

   The approved Intent carries no authority members.  The task, the
   proposed authority, and the granted authority are committed
   separately (Section 6.4.1):

   *  intent_hash commits the approved Intent, which is recorded on the
      Mission;

   *  proposal_hash commits any authority proposal, which the client
      submits separately, on the standard authorization_details
      parameter (Section 4.3); and

   *  authority_hash commits the granted authority, the derived
      Authority Set (Section 5).

   The client writes every Intent member; how it produces them, for
   example through a Mission Shaper working from a natural-language
   instruction, is out of scope
   ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-mission-shaping]).  The prose members (goal,
   task_bounds) bound authority only through disclosure: the Approver
   refuses authority the words do not support (Section 5).

   An AS that makes no use of an optional member still accepts it and
   records it with the Intent (Section 4.5).  The smallest Intent
   carries only the three required members, as in the Submission
   envelope example (Section 4.2).  The following summary is
   informative; the member definitions after it are authoritative.

4.1.  Intent Members

     +==================+==========+=================================+
     | Member           | Required | What the AS does with it        |
     +==================+==========+=================================+
     | goal             | yes      | Records it and renders it to    |
     |                  |          | the Approver as inert text;     |
     |                  |          | never interprets it             |
     +------------------+----------+---------------------------------+
     | goal_lang        | no       | Checks the language tag and     |
     |                  |          | uses it when rendering          |
     +------------------+----------+---------------------------------+
     | target_resources | yes      | Bounds the resources a derived  |
     |                  |          | entry can name; can key a       |
     |                  |          | configured mapping              |
     +------------------+----------+---------------------------------+
     | task_bounds      | no       | Records it and renders it to    |
     |                  |          | the Approver as inert text;     |
     |                  |          | never interprets it             |
     +------------------+----------+---------------------------------+
     | purpose          | no       | Records it; can key a           |
     |                  |          | configured mapping; in any      |
     |                  |          | other decision can only lead to |
     |                  |          | a refusal or stricter treatment |
     +------------------+----------+---------------------------------+
     | expires_at       | yes      | Caps the Mission's effective    |
     |                  |          | expiry; refuses a malformed or  |
     |                  |          | past value                      |
     +------------------+----------+---------------------------------+

                Table 1: Mission Intent members at a glance

   The Mission Intent members are:

   goal:  REQUIRED.  A string.  A human-readable statement of the task,
      for rendering to the Approver.  Maximum 4096 characters.

      *  It can describe the outcomes that show the task is complete;
         like the rest of the prose, that description carries no machine
         semantics (Section 5).

      *  Prose here persists on the record and can carry personal data
         about third parties (Section 17.4).

   goal_lang:  OPTIONAL.  A string.  A BCP 47 language tag [RFC5646]
      declaring the language of the Intent's human-readable members
      (goal, task_bounds).

      *  It is disclosure metadata for rendering, committed by
         intent_hash like every Intent member, and carries no machine
         semantics (Section 5).

      *  At submission acceptance, the AS MUST refuse a goal_lang that
         is not a well-formed language tag with invalid_request
         (Section 18).

   target_resources:  REQUIRED.  An array of strings.  A client-
      requested Intent ceiling on the task's target resources, each an
      absolute URI identifying a protected resource (an OAuth resource
      per [RFC8707]-style indicators or a protected resource per
      [RFC9728]).

      *  It bounds which resources a derived Authority Set entry may
         name (Section 5) and serves as a configured-mapping lookup key
         (Section 5.1); like purpose, the key alone grants no authority.

      *  It is not RFC 8707 resource carriage: a client separately
         requests the audience of a given token with the standard
         resource parameter (Section 7.3).

   task_bounds:  OPTIONAL.  An array of strings.  Human-readable bounds
      on the task (for example, "read only invoices from 2026").  They
      are disclosure and audit context, rendered to the Approver beside
      the derived Authority Set (Section 6.1.2), and carry no machine
      semantics (Section 5); a machine-enforceable bound enters as
      structure instead.

   purpose:  OPTIONAL.  A string.  A URI identifying the purpose of the
      task, recorded for disclosure and audit.  Its semantics are
      deployment- or registry-defined and opaque to this document.

      *  purpose is an opaque lookup key: a configured mapping can key
         on it to select candidate entries, which the Intent, policy,
         and the approval event bound as in any Authority Set derivation
         (Section 5); the key alone grants no authority.

      *  In any other decision, purpose MAY contribute only to a refusal
         or to stricter treatment: its value, its absence, or a
         substituted value MUST NOT supply, widen, relax, or refresh
         authority, or replace a check that applies independently of it.

      *  After approval, the purpose consulted is the approved
         Mission's, or a validated projection of it.

   expires_at:  REQUIRED.  A string.  An RFC 3339 [RFC3339] date-time:
      the client's requested not-after ceiling for the Mission's
      lifetime.

      *  The submitted Intent is recorded verbatim.

      *  The granted lifetime is the Mission Record's effective
         expires_at, which the AS establishes at Mission creation and
         which is no later than this value (Section 6.1.2, Section 6.3).

      *  At submission acceptance, the AS MUST refuse a malformed or
         already-past value with invalid_request.

      *  Acceptance does not freeze time: Mission creation re-checks the
         effective expiry atomically at the commit (Section 6.1.2).

      *  A request that resolves to an already-committed operation is
         recovery, not a new submission, and returns the stored outcome
         under the applicable idempotency rules even when the ceiling
         has since passed (Section 7.3.1, Section 6.3).

   The Approver's authentication strength for the approval event is
   requested with the standard acr_values and max_age authorization-
   request parameters, not on the Intent (Section 6.1.8).

   The Mission Intent's top level is closed to the members above and to
   those a companion profile the AS implements defines (Section 14,
   Section 4.2).

   The following is an example of a Mission Intent:

   =============== NOTE: '\' line wrapping per RFC 8792 ===============

   {
     "goal": "Reconcile Q3 invoices and post adjustments of at most \
       USD 500 per journal entry.",
     "target_resources": ["https://erp.example.com"],
     "task_bounds": [
       "Read only invoices issued in 2026-Q3.",
       "Post journal entries of at most USD 500 each."
     ],
     "purpose": "urn:example:purpose:reconcile",
     "expires_at": "2026-12-31T23:59:59Z"
   }

4.2.  Submitting a Mission Intent

   A client submits a Mission Intent, with any Intent Submission
   Evidence, in a Submission envelope (Section 4.2.1) sent as
   mission_intent.  On the authorization-code path, the envelope travels
   through a Pushed Authorization Request (Section 4.2.2); a companion
   profile can define another, back-channel carriage (Section 4.2.3).

   A Mission Intent, and any authority proposal submitted alongside it
   (Section 4.3), is untrusted client input; trust enters only when the
   AS validates it and the Approver consents to the rendered result.
   The AS derives and bounds authority by its own policy, whatever the
   client submitted (Section 5).

4.2.1.  The Submission Envelope

   The Submission envelope is a JSON object with exactly these members:

   intent:  REQUIRED.  The Mission Intent object (Section 4).

   evidence:  OPTIONAL.  A non-empty array of Intent Submission Evidence
      entries (Section 4.4); the AS refuses an empty array with the
      invalid_request error code.

   intent_hash commits exactly the intent object, not the Submission
   envelope or its evidence array (Section 6.4.1).

   The following rules govern a submission:

   *  *Structural validity.* An AS that cannot parse mission_intent as a
      JSON object, or that parses it but finds the Submission envelope
      or the Intent structurally invalid against this document's member
      definitions, MUST refuse the request with the invalid_request
      error code.

   *  *Closed top levels.* The AS MUST reject a submission with the
      invalid_request error code if the Submission envelope contains a
      top-level member other than intent and evidence, or if the intent
      contains a top-level member that neither this document nor a
      companion profile the AS implements defines (Section 14).  A bare
      Mission Intent submitted as the parameter value fails this rule,
      since its goal and sibling members are unknown envelope members.

   *  *Bounded size.* The AS MUST bound the Submission envelope's total
      size, the Intent's total size and the lengths of its arrays, and
      the count and per-entry sizes of evidence entries, refusing a
      submission that exceeds the deployment-defined limits with
      invalid_request.  The verification-cost bound of
      [I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-submission-evidence]
      accompanies these.

   The following is an example of a Submission envelope carrying a
   compact Intent and one evidence entry of an illustrative, deployment-
   defined type:

   =============== NOTE: '\' line wrapping per RFC 8792 ===============

   {
     "intent": {
       "goal": "Reconcile Q3 invoices and post adjustments of at most \
         USD 500 per journal entry.",
       "target_resources": ["https://erp.example.com"],
       "expires_at": "2026-12-31T23:59:59Z"
     },
     "evidence": [
       {
         "type": "urn:example:intent-evidence:admission",
         "assertion": "eyJhbGciOiJFUzI1NiIsImtpZCI6ImFkbS0xIn0..."
       }
     ]
   }

4.2.2.  Carriage Through PAR

   On the authorization-code path (Section 6.1), a client MUST submit a
   Mission Intent through a Pushed Authorization Request [RFC9126] using
   the mission_intent request parameter.  The parameter value is the
   UTF-8 JSON [RFC8259] serialization of the Submission envelope, form-
   encoded in the application/x-www-form-urlencoded PAR request body
   like any other OAuth parameter.

   On the authorization-code path, the AS accepts the Intent in one of
   two forms:

   1.  the form-encoded mission_intent parameter of the PAR request
       body; or

   2.  a mission_intent claim inside a signed Request Object ([RFC9101])
       that is itself pushed through PAR.  The claim's value is the
       Submission envelope itself, a JSON object, not its serialization;
       the AS MUST reject with invalid_request a claim whose value is a
       string or any other value that is not a JSON object.

   When the pushed request carries a Request Object (a request parameter
   value), that object is authoritative: a mission_intent submitted
   outside the Request Object in the same push MUST be rejected with
   invalid_request, strengthening Section 6.3 of [RFC9101], under which
   the AS would only ignore such a value.

   A client proposes concrete authority on the standard
   authorization_details parameter in the same push (Section 4.3).  A
   client can also push scope and resource ([RFC8707]) values; the AS
   treats them as a requested subset of the authority the Mission Intent
   yields (Section 5).

   The AS returns a request_uri as usual, which the client uses to start
   authorization.  Pushed parameters are authoritative: on the front-
   channel request that redeems the request_uri, the AS MUST ignore any
   mission_intent, authorization_details, scope, or resource presented.

   The AS MUST reject with invalid_request a mission_intent presented on
   a front-channel authorization request that does not use a PAR-issued
   request_uri.  A client MUST NOT send mission_intent as a plaintext
   front-channel authorization-request parameter, whether or not a
   Request Object is also present.

4.2.3.  Other Carriages

   A Mission Intent travels on no other request unless a companion
   profile explicitly defines that request as a back-channel carriage of
   the Submission envelope.  Support for a grant type or endpoint never
   establishes such a carriage on its own.

   A profile that defines such a carriage carries the envelope and any
   authority proposal in one request that never traverses a user agent,
   and preserves:

   *  the envelope and proposal rules of Section 4.2;

   *  the processing order (Section 4.5);

   *  Authority Set derivation (Section 5);

   *  the approval-event rules for the Mission's approval basis
      (Section 6.1, Section 6.2.1, with a direct approval relocated only
      as Section 14 permits); and

   *  the grant binding (Section 6.5).

   The experimental Mission Device Authorization profile
   ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-device-authorization]) defines
   such a carriage for a client that cannot complete the authorization-
   code path, on the device authorization grant [RFC8628].

4.3.  Authority Proposal

   A client MAY propose concrete authority for the task by submitting
   the standard [RFC9396] authorization_details request parameter, a
   JSON array of authorization_details objects, alongside mission_intent
   in the same pushed request (Section 4.2).  The carriage rules of
   Section 4.2 apply to the proposal.

   The submitted authorization_details is a proposal, not authority
   (Section 4.2); the AS derives and bounds the Authority Set from it
   (Section 5).

4.3.1.  Validating Proposed Entries

   The AS validates each submitted entry per Section 5 of [RFC9396].  It
   refuses a request carrying an entry of a type it does not support
   (Section 5.5, Section 12), or one that fails its type's documented
   definition, with the invalid_authorization_details error code.  It
   never repairs a failure by omitting the entry.  Where a machine-
   readable JSON Schema for the type is advertised (Section 12) or
   established out of band, the entry MUST also validate against that
   schema, and the AS MUST refuse a request carrying an entry that fails
   it with the invalid_authorization_details error code.

   Each proposed entry that carries a resource member MUST have it among
   the Intent's target_resources, compared as Section 5 states; the AS
   refuses a request violating this with the invalid_request error code.

4.3.2.  Deriving From a Proposal

   When a proposal is present, the AS MUST derive each Authority Set
   entry as a subset (Section 5.4) of some proposed entry of the _same
   type_: narrowed under that type's own subset rule where the AS has
   declared narrowing for the type, or carried through unchanged
   otherwise (Section 5.5.1).  With a proposal present, goal and
   task_bounds serve as rendering and bounding context over the proposed
   authority.

   Policy narrowing is distinct from validation (Section 4.3.1).  During
   Authority Set derivation the AS MAY omit a syntactically valid entry
   that policy cannot accept, or narrow it where the AS has declared
   narrowing for its type (Section 5, Section 5.5.1).  The granted
   authorization_details in the token response reflects every narrowing
   and omission (Section 7.3).

   The AS records the proposed array on the Mission exactly as submitted
   and commits it by proposal_hash (Section 6.4.1, Section 6.3),
   separately from the task (intent_hash) and the approved Authority Set
   (authority_hash), so a narrowed approved Authority Set can be audited
   against the proposal that sought it.

   The following is an example of an authority proposal submitted
   alongside the example Intent of Section 4:

   [
     { "type": "mission_resource_access",
       "resource": "https://erp.example.com",
       "actions": ["invoices.*"],
       "constraints": {
         "resource_issued_after": "2026-07-01T00:00:00Z",
         "resource_issued_before": "2026-09-30T23:59:59Z"
       },
       "delegation": {
         "max_depth": 2,
         "allowed_delegates": [{ "sub_profile": "ai_agent" }]
       } },
     { "type": "mission_resource_access",
       "resource": "https://erp.example.com",
       "actions": ["journal-entries.write"],
       "constraints": {
         "max_amount": { "amount": "500.00", "currency": "USD" }
       } }
   ]

   Under a declared narrowing capability, Authority Set derivation
   narrows invoices.* to invoices.read.  It keeps the proposed Q3
   issuance window and write ceiling, which the Intent's task bounds
   support, and carries the proposed delegation policy through
   unchanged.  The result is the example Authority Set of Section 5.

4.3.3.  Mission-Governed Resources and Clients

   Submitting authorization_details without mission_intent is an
   ordinary [RFC9396] request that this document does not govern.  Two
   AS-side rules keep a governed task from being downgraded to such a
   request (Section 12 states the client-side rules and Section 16.1.2
   the threat):

   1.  Where a resource is Mission-governed, its AS MUST NOT issue a
       token for that resource outside a Mission, on any grant type.

   2.  If a client registered as Mission-governed sends an authorization
       request, through PAR or at the authorization endpoint, that
       carries authorization_details but no mission_intent, the AS MUST
       reject the request with the invalid_request error code, so the
       client cannot drop the Intent from a request that still carries
       authorization_details.

   A resource is Mission-governed (a Mission-governed resource) when the
   deployment classifies it so, or when protected resource metadata the
   AS has authenticated ([RFC9728]) declares
   mission_bound_authorization_required as true (Section 13).  Metadata
   that omits the member or sets it to false does not clear a
   deployment's classification, and a less restrictive local
   classification does not override metadata that declares the
   requirement.  This document defines no exception to the first rule;
   an exception requires a concrete scope that a separate specification
   defines, and none applies to a resource whose metadata declares the
   requirement.

   Under the first rule, a token is issued outside a Mission when the
   request has no qualifying Mission binding: no grant bound to a
   Mission (Section 6.5), or to a companion profile's equivalent
   binding, from which the issuance derives under Section 7.8.1.  The
   rule applies to every audience the token would carry, including one
   the AS infers or applies by default when the request names no
   resource: the AS MUST refuse the request if any of them is Mission-
   governed, and MUST NOT issue the token for the others.

   The AS refuses a token request the first rule forbids with the
   invalid_target error code (Section 2 of [RFC8707]) once the request
   is otherwise processable.  On a refresh, the AS MUST decide this
   refusal before it consumes or rotates the refresh token.  Other
   failures keep their own errors:

   *  authentication, syntax, and grant-validation failures keep their
      errors and precedence;

   *  an invalid or expired grant keeps its error; and

   *  a request under a grant bound to a Mission that is not active
      keeps the refusal of Section 7.1.

   Until that request completes, including when the AS resumes it after
   an interaction, the second rule applies to a Mission-governed
   client's authorization request that carries authorization_details but
   no mission_intent.  A request carrying neither authorization_details
   nor mission_intent is an ordinary request that the second rule does
   not reach; the first rule, for a Mission-governed resource, is what
   keeps that resource's tokens inside a Mission.

   A token request under an existing Mission-bound grant, such as a
   narrowing refresh or a self-exchange (Section 7.5), needs no
   mission_intent under the second rule.  This exception opens no
   Mission submission carriage, and every other check still applies to
   the request:

   *  Issuance Gating (Section 7.1);

   *  the subset rule (Section 5.4);

   *  the first rule above; and

   *  the grant's binding to its Mission, client and sender.

4.4.  Intent Submission Evidence

   The Submission envelope's evidence array carries *Intent Submission
   Evidence*: typed artifacts the client presents in support of claims
   about the submitted Intent, such as its originator, an admission or
   consent decision that applies to it, or the presenter authorized to
   submit it.  Each entry is a JSON object whose type member names its
   evidence type.

   Processing is governed by the following rules:

   *  *Reject, do not ignore.* An entry that is not a JSON object, or
      that lacks type, is structurally invalid and refused with the
      invalid_request error code at step 1 of the processing order
      (Section 4.5).  The AS MUST refuse an entry whose type it does not
      support, and an entry that fails its type's validation or
      verification, with the invalid_mission_intent_evidence error code
      (Section 19.2).  A submission is accepted only when every
      presented entry verifies.

   *  *Policy input, not authority.* A verified entry is not copied into
      the Authority Set and does not stand in for the approval event
      (Section 6.1), the sole activation of authority.  Verified
      evidence can serve as authenticated input to admission and
      derivation policy; AS policy decides whether the verified claims
      are acceptable for this request.

   Supporting an evidence type is optional, but these rules are not: an
   AS that supports none still applies them and refuses every presented
   entry (Section 15.1).

   Mission Intent Submission Evidence for OAuth 2.0
   ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-submission-evidence]) specifies
   the evidence framework, including required-evidence resolution and
   the binding of evidence to one Intent and its presenter.

4.5.  Submission Processing Order

   The AS processes a submission in the following order.  Steps 1 to 6
   run when the submission arrives; step 7 can run provisionally then
   and is complete before the approval rendering (Section 5.1); step 8
   belongs to the approval event, and step 9 to activation.
   Section 6.4.3 states how JSON destined for commitment is parsed,
   including the rejection of duplicate member names.

   1.  Parse the Submission envelope, enforce both closed top levels and
       the size bounds (Section 4.2), and check that each evidence entry
       is a JSON object with a type member (Section 4.4).

   2.  Validate the intent object against the Mission Intent member
       definitions (Section 4).

   3.  Validate any authority proposal: refuse an entry of a type the AS
       does not support, or one that fails its type's definition or a
       schema advertised for it or established out of band, with
       invalid_authorization_details, and a proposed entry that carries
       a resource member whose value is not among the Intent's
       target_resources, with invalid_request (Section 4.3).

   4.  Compute the provisional intent_hash over the intent object
       (Section 6.4.1).

   5.  Apply any submission checks that configured admission policy or
       adopted profiles require before evidence verification, including
       when no evidence member is present.  For example, the Submission
       Evidence companion resolves required evidence here and refuses a
       missing required type
       ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-submission-evidence],
       Section "Required Evidence Is Resolved Before Derivation").

   6.  Dispatch each presented entry on its type, and validate and
       verify it under that type's rules (Section 4.4).

   7.  Apply admission policy and derive the Authority Set independently
       (Section 5), including the target_resources membership of each
       entry that carries a resource member (Section 5.1) and, where a
       proposal was submitted, that each derived entry is a same-type
       subset of a proposed entry (Section 4.3).

   8.  Render the Intent, the Authority Set, and the material verified
       provenance for approval; a change to any of them before the
       decision is re-rendered and approved over the changed context
       (Section 6.1).

   9.  At activation, record the approved intent, intent_hash, the
       Authority Set, and the verified evidence facts as
       submission_evidence (Section 6.3).

   The AS MUST refuse a submission that fails more than one check with
   the error of the earliest failing step in this order.  For example:

   *  a malformed proposal together with a missing required evidence
      type is refused at step 3, with invalid_authorization_details;

   *  a proposed entry outside target_resources together with a
      presented evidence entry that fails verification is refused at
      step 3, with invalid_request;

   *  an intent object that fails validation together with a malformed
      proposal is refused at step 2, with invalid_request; and

   *  a structurally invalid evidence entry together with a malformed
      proposal is refused at step 1, with invalid_request.

   The material verified provenance of step 8 is part of the approval
   surface.  Where a deployment commits the rendered approval surface,
   that commitment MUST cover the normalized provenance facts, at least
   as a digest of their canonical submission_evidence representation
   (Section 6.3).  That commitment MAY omit each element's verified_at,
   which the AS generates at each verification, and no other member:
   type, artifact_hash, and facts, including any timestamp within facts,
   stay covered.

5.  Mission Authority

   From the Mission Intent, and from the authority proposal where one
   was submitted (Section 4.3), the AS derives the *Authority Set*: one
   or more [RFC9396] authorization_details entries of an AS-supported
   type (Section 5.5).

5.1.  Derivation Modes

   Derivation is governed by local policy: different authorization
   servers can derive different Authority Sets from the same Mission
   Intent, and interoperability begins at the derived Authority Set and
   its integrity anchors (Section 6.4.1).

   Authority Set derivation is mechanical: one procedure whose candidate
   entries depend on whether an authority proposal was submitted:

   *  *Narrowing mode* (preferred where the client can author
      authorization_details): the client submitted an authority proposal
      (Section 4.3), and the Authority Set is the proposal narrowed to
      policy.  Each derived entry is a same-type subset of a proposed
      entry (Section 4.3).

   *  *Configured-mapping mode*: no authority proposal was submitted,
      and a deployment-configured mapping, keyed on the Intent's purpose
      or target_resources, or on the requested scope values for a scope-
      only target (Section 7.7), yields the candidate entries, which are
      then narrowed to policy.

      The mapping is a lookup, not synthesis, and no key grants
      authority by itself.  Because no authorization_details object was
      submitted, the AS refuses an Intent that matches no configured
      mapping, or whose mapped candidates policy narrows to nothing,
      with access_denied (Section 11).  Its error_description can say
      whether no mapping matched or policy narrowed the candidates to
      nothing.

      Either mode is conforming.  A deployment publishes its mapping
      space as deployment documentation.  Eligibility can be scoped per
      Subject and client, and a mapping keyed on Subject attributes
      resolves at the approval event, where the Subject is established
      (Section 6.1.2), so a refusal at PAR time is best-effort over what
      is checkable without the Subject.

      Every Mission this mode yields takes its own fresh approval;
      standing consent to a pre-approved ceiling is the separate,
      experimental Mission Template profile
      ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-template]), not this mode.

   In either mode, narrowing a candidate entry needs a declared
   narrowing capability for its type; without one, the entry is retained
   unchanged or omitted (Section 5.5.1).

   Authority Set derivation can run provisionally during submission
   processing, where the inputs it needs are available (Section 4.5).
   The candidate Authority Set is complete before the approval rendering
   (Section 6.1.2), and a change that Section 6.1.5 covers is recomputed
   and approved over the changed context.  The approved Authority Set is
   fixed when the Mission Record is created, atomically with the
   approval decision (Section 6.1.2), under the derivation policy in
   force at the approval event.

   In both modes the AS bounds every Authority Set entry by the Mission
   Intent: each entry that carries a resource member MUST have it among
   the Intent's target_resources values, by exact string equality.  This
   membership check applies to the Authority Set, and to a proposal at
   submission (Section 4.3).

   A later token issuance is bounded by the subset rule against the
   Authority Set (Section 5.4), not by the target list again.  A type
   whose subset relation admits a narrower resource (for
   mission_resource_access, a descendant of a prefix entry) can yield a
   token entry that target_resources does not name, and a candidate
   outside every Authority Set entry fails that rule.

   The Mission records the policy version in force as policy_version
   (Section 6.3).

   Generative Authority Set derivation is not one of this document's
   modes.  As a local-policy extension, a model's output can be a
   recorded input to the AS's deterministic derivation policy, together
   with the model's identifier and version.  That input can cause
   refusal or further narrow the candidate authority the selected mode
   and policy otherwise permit.  The AS MUST NOT let it supply or widen
   authority.  Each type's subset rule (Section 5.4), the Intent bounds,
   and the prose boundary below still apply.

   A target_resources entry the deployment does not recognize is, by
   deployment policy, either omitted from the Authority Set or refused
   with the access_denied error code (Section 11).

   When an omission or a narrowing leaves the Authority Set short of
   what was proposed (Section 4.3), Authority Set derivation is partial.
   The token response states what was granted (Section 7.3).

5.2.  Consented Authority Set

   The derived Authority Set, not the Mission Intent, is the authority
   the Approver consents to: the AS renders the Authority Set for
   approval and commits it as authority_hash (Section 6.1.2).  The
   Intent's members describe and bound the task but grant no authority
   by themselves (Section 4): its structured members constrain what the
   AS can derive mechanically, its prose members bound through
   disclosure, and none widens.

   The goal and task_bounds members are human-readable disclosure and
   audit context.  The AS MUST derive the same Authority Set, under the
   same policy, for two submissions that differ only in goal, goal_lang,
   or task_bounds, and MUST NOT gate issuance on those members, whether
   in Authority Set derivation, in an adjudicating policy, or through a
   model input to either; translating a user's words into structure is
   the shaper's job, before admission and outside the trust boundary
   ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-mission-shaping]).

   A client-proposed constraint on an individual Authority Set entry
   enters through the authorization_details proposal: constraints a
   supported type's specification defines (for example, the Common
   Constraints that [I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-resource-access]
   defines for mission_resource_access), and the collision-resistant
   deployment extensions the AS understands (Section 14).

   Authority is further bounded by:

   *  the Intent's structured members (target_resources, expires_at);

   *  the configured mapping, a lookup over structured values, not an
      interpretation of prose; and

   *  local policy and eligibility.

   The approval surface renders the prose beside the derived Authority
   Set (Section 6.1.2) as the human check that the structure matches the
   words, not as a machine enforcement mechanism.

5.3.  Authority Set Example

   The following is an example of an Authority Set. The read entry is
   delegable to depth 2 and bounded to a Q3 issuance window by the
   resource_issued_after and resource_issued_before Common Constraints
   ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-resource-access]); the write
   entry carries no delegation and so is non-delegable, because
   delegation is per entry:

   [
     { "type": "mission_resource_access",
       "resource": "https://erp.example.com",
       "actions": ["invoices.read"],
       "constraints": {
         "resource_issued_after": "2026-07-01T00:00:00Z",
         "resource_issued_before": "2026-09-30T23:59:59Z"
       },
       "delegation": {
         "max_depth": 2,
         "allowed_delegates": [{ "sub_profile": "ai_agent" }]
       } },
     { "type": "mission_resource_access",
       "resource": "https://erp.example.com",
       "actions": ["journal-entries.write"],
       "constraints": {
         "max_amount": { "amount": "500.00", "currency": "USD" }
       } }
   ]

5.4.  Subset Rule

   A derived authorization_details entry is a subset of a reference
   entry when it is no broader under the subset relation the entry's own
   type defines; Section 5.5 states the general rule and each supported
   type defines its own relation (for mission_resource_access,
   [I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-resource-access]).  The AS MUST
   refuse to derive an entry that is not a subset, under its type's
   relation, of some Mission Authority Set entry.

   The AS MUST refuse a request that would widen authority under a
   Mission after the approval event on any dimension (a new resource,
   action, actor, delegation path, longer duration, or constraint
   relaxation); broader authority requires a fresh approval event,
   either a new Mission or a successor
   ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-expansion]).

   The comparison is representational, not semantic.  A candidate that
   compares as no broader can still permit effects the parent's purpose
   never contemplated; semantic narrowing is not a property this rule
   can provide.

   Where the comparison relation cannot decide (an unrecognized member,
   an incomparable value), the posture is conservative refusal, as each
   consuming rule of this document states.  At the token endpoint, where
   the comparison cannot establish that a requested
   authorization_details entry is a subset, the AS MUST refuse the
   request with the invalid_authorization_details error code, as for an
   entry that exceeds the grant (Section 11).

   An entry equal to its reference entry, byte-identical under the
   canonical form of Section 6.4.2, is a subset of it for every type.
   This is the one case the subset rule decides without the type's own
   relation, and it is how an AS that has not declared narrowing for a
   type establishes a subset (Section 5.5).

5.5.  Authorization Details Types

   The Authority Set MAY include any AS-supported [RFC9396]
   authorization_details type an audience consumes; this document
   defines no type itself.  ("Supported" here means the AS recognizes
   and documents the type: it appears in
   authorization_details_types_supported or, where the AS advertises the
   schema endpoint, as a key in its
   authorization_details_types_metadata_endpoint response, then the
   source of truth for the supported set (Section 12).)  The Mission
   apparatus is type-agnostic toward every supported type:

   *  an entry is committed by authority_hash and gated on Mission state
      the same way regardless of type;

   *  narrowing and delegation use the subset and delegation semantics
      the type defines (Section 5.4, Section 8.4), under the
      transformation capabilities the AS declares for the type (below);
      and

   *  evaluating the entry against a concrete request is the
      responsibility of the resource server, under the entry's type
      (Section 9.1.1), or of a composing runtime layer (Section 16.3.1),
      not the AS's.

5.5.1.  Declared Transformation Capabilities

   The subset rule is fully defined only for a type whose specification
   defines it.  For every supported type, the AS declares three
   independent transformation capabilities; understanding one
   establishes none of the others:

   *  *narrowing:* the AS understands the type's subset relation;

   *  *delegation:* the AS understands the type's delegation semantics;
      and

   *  *projection:* the AS establishes a safe scope projection for the
      type (Section 7.7).

   The AS MUST NOT narrow, delegate, or project to scope an entry of a
   type for which it has not declared the corresponding capability; on
   an undeclared capability the entry is carried as approved.

   The narrowing capability also governs Authority Set derivation, and
   approval does not waive the subset proof.  Without narrowing for its
   type, the AS retains a valid candidate entry, whether proposed
   (Section 4.3) or yielded by a configured mapping (Section 5.1),
   unchanged, or omits it where this document permits omission; it never
   modifies the entry under a claimed subset relation.  A malformed
   entry is refused as Section 4.3 states.

   The following rules govern an entry's original approved audience:

   *  The AS relates each entry to the audiences that consume it through
      a trusted entry-to-audience mapping, such as authenticated
      configuration or protected resource metadata ([RFC9728]).  An
      entry's original approved audience is the audience or audiences
      that mapping assigned the entry when the Mission was approved.  An
      entry's resource value is not by itself an audience (Section 7.3).

   *  The AS MUST retain that association with the Mission; a later
      change to the mapping does not redefine it.

   *  A Mission created from earlier authority, such as a Child Mission
      ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-child-delegation]), a
      successor ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-expansion]), or an
      instance of a standing consent (Section 6.2), does not reassign
      the original approved audience of an entry it inherits: the entry
      keeps that of the authority it derives from.  Only a fresh
      approval of the entry, or a transformation under a declared
      capability, establishes a different one.

   *  Where the AS cannot establish an entry's original approved
      audience, the entry has none; the current mapping MUST NOT stand
      in for it.

   Issuing an entry to an audience other than its original approved
   audience, including audience projection to a Resource AS
   ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-cross-domain]), counts as
   narrowing and needs narrowing, not projection.  Without narrowing,
   the AS MUST NOT issue an entry to any audience other than its
   original approved audience, or, except as scope under a declared
   projection, in any form other than exactly as approved, since it
   cannot prove that a transformed copy is still a subset of what was
   approved.

   Section 8.2 and Section 8.1 state how these rules apply to a
   delegated token and to a designation.

   The AS declares the capabilities through these carriers, in order of
   preference:

   1.  a mission_transformation_capabilities member in the type's entry
       in the authorization_details_types_metadata_endpoint response,
       where the AS advertises that endpoint (Section 12);

   2.  an equivalently shaped member of its supported-type
       documentation; or

   3.  deployment documentation naming the type as supported
       (Section 12).

   Deployment documentation is always a sufficient carrier; a machine-
   readable carrier is optional, and the first declaring carrier
   governs.  A mission_transformation_capabilities value is a JSON
   object with three OPTIONAL boolean members, narrowing, delegation,
   and projection; an absent member leaves that capability undeclared by
   this carrier, falling through to the next carrier in the order above.
   A type's specification MAY define another value for one of these
   members; a carrier declaring such a value declares that capability as
   the type's specification defines it, and a reader that does not
   understand the value, or cannot validate it as that specification
   requires, treats the carrier as declaring the capability false.  For
   each capability, the first carrier in that order that declares the
   member governs, whether it declares true or false; a later carrier
   does not override it.  A declared capability permits a transformation
   without requiring it, and waives no check this document requires and
   no local policy under which the AS refuses one.

6.  Mission Creation

   Approval creates a Mission.  Section 6.1 states the direct approval
   event, Section 6.2 the extension point for other approval bases,
   Section 6.3 the record the AS keeps, Section 6.4 the commitments to
   its approved content, and Section 6.5 how the grant the AS issues is
   bound to the Mission.

6.1.  Mission Approval

   An approval event is the atomic transition that creates a Mission.
   Every Mission is created at its own approval event, which the
   record's approval_event_id identifies.

   The approval basis (approval_basis, Section 6.3) and its
   adjudication, not the event alone, determine whether a human decides
   at that event or an earlier standing consent authorizes the instance:

   *  A direct approval event includes the Approver's contemporaneous
      consent: it is a human approval event.

   *  Under a standing-consent basis (Section 6.2), the instance is
      decided under an accountable human's earlier approval, with no
      fresh human approval.

6.1.1.  Authorization Code Protection

   The direct realization runs as an OAuth 2.0 [RFC6749] authorization-
   code flow initiated from the PAR-issued request_uri (Section 4.2).
   Because the authorization code is the artifact the Mission grant
   binds to (Section 6.5.1) and it passes through the front channel, the
   AS MUST bind the code to the requesting client with PKCE ([RFC7636],
   S256 challenge method) or, equivalently, issue a DPoP-bound
   authorization code ([RFC9449]).  Redemption is then verified as
   Section 4.6 of [RFC7636] or Section 10 of [RFC9449] specifies.  This
   prevents authorization-code injection from yielding the Mission
   grant.  Issuance at the code's redemption applies the issuance
   procedure of Section 7.1 as it states for code redemption.

   The AS SHOULD include the iss authorization-response parameter
   ([RFC9207]) on the authorization response, so the client can detect a
   mix-up attack on the consent-bearing redirect leg ([RFC9700]).

6.1.2.  Direct Approval Steps

   At a direct approval event the AS MUST perform steps 1 to 7, in
   order, as one procedure: steps 1 to 5 prepare the approval
   (Section 6.1.2.1), and steps 6 and 7, which continue the same
   procedure, commit the Mission (Section 6.1.2.2).

   The steps of this section define the direct approval basis
   (approval_basis, Section 6.3); a companion profile that relocates a
   direct approval keeps them unchanged (Section 14).

   Section 6.5.1 states the rule that keeps consent reuse, for an
   authorization request that carries no mission_intent, from selecting
   a grant bound to a Mission.

   Refusals follow Section 11.

6.1.2.1.  Preparing the Approval

   1.  *Authenticate the Approver*, subject to the approval-
       authentication floor and any client-requested strength
       (Section 6.1.8).  The AS MUST NOT take the Approver's identity or
       achieved authentication context from unauthenticated client
       input; the authenticated surface that resolved the approval
       establishes both.

   2.  *Establish the Subject*: the principal the task is for, recorded
       as the Mission's subject and mapped to the sub of every derived
       token (Section 7.2).  The Subject can be a workload or
       organizational principal (Section 6.1.7), established and mapped
       the same way.

       *  *Self-approval.* When the Approver is the Subject, the Subject
          is the authenticated Approver.

       *  *Another principal or an external Subject.* The rules of
          Section 6.1.3 apply at this step.

   3.  *Establish the authority source*: whose authority the approval
       draws on, recorded as the Mission's authority_source
       (Section 6.1.7, Section 6.3).  The AS MUST establish it from
       trusted configuration or authenticated governance state, never
       from client assertion.

       The AS then makes two checks:

       *  The AS MUST verify the Approver is authorized under local
          policy to activate the established source.

       *  The AS MUST verify the derived Authority Set lies within that
          source's authority (for organizational, within the governed
          policy identified by authority_source.policy).

       These are distinct checks: an organizational owner may be
       authorized to activate policy without personally holding every
       operational permission.  The AS MUST refuse when either
       relationship cannot be established.  Section 6.3.3 states the
       refusal to approve a Mission whose authority source the AS does
       not recognize.

   4.  *Establish the effective Mission expiry*: the requested
       intent.expires_at ceiling narrowed by applicable AS policy and
       any ceiling an applicable Mission-creating profile defines.  The
       value is bounded as expires_at requires (Section 6.3) and is
       rechecked at the creation commit (step 7).

   5.  *Render for consent* the derived Authority Set in human-
       meaningful terms, with the goal, task_bounds, and the effective
       expires_at (and, when it differs, the requested
       intent.expires_at, so the Approver sees the narrowing) as
       context.

       The consent object is the *derived Authority Set*: what the agent
       may do.  It is not the goal or Mission Intent, because Authority
       Set derivation is local policy and nothing commits that the
       derived authority reflects the goal the Approver read.  An
       approval surface that renders only the goal or Mission Intent
       does not conform.

       These requirements apply to the rendering:

       *  The rendering MUST identify the OAuth client that receives the
          authority, the Agent, from client identity information the AS
          trusts, such as its own registration of the client; display
          text the client supplies does not by itself identify it.

       *  When the Approver is not the Subject, the rendering MUST
          identify the Subject the authority is granted for.

       *  The rendering MUST identify the authority source and, for
          organizational, the governed policy it draws on
          (authority_source.policy).

       *  When the client submitted an authority proposal (Section 4.3),
          the rendering MUST distinguish the entries the client proposed
          from any narrowing or restructuring the AS applied.

       Mission Capability Binding
       ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-mission-capability-binding]) states what
       the rendering presents about the source binding of a catalog-
       sourced action.

6.1.2.2.  Committing the Mission

   The same procedure continues from step 5:

   6.  *Compute the integrity anchors* (Section 6.4.1): authority_hash
       over the consented Authority Set, intent_hash over the approved
       Mission Intent, and, when an authority proposal was submitted
       (Section 4.3), proposal_hash over the submitted
       authorization_details array.

   7.  *Create the Mission Record* (Section 6.3) in the active state
       atomically with the approval decision and issuance of the
       authorization code, or, under a back-channel carriage profile
       (Section 14), with establishment of the Mission grant binding and
       activation of the profile-defined grant for redemption.  The
       commit MUST verify atomically that the effective expiry is
       strictly later than the creation instant: acceptance of the
       submission does not freeze time, and where the requested ceiling
       passed while the approval was pending, completion creates no
       Mission.  Where a direct approval fails this check, the AS MUST
       refuse the request with access_denied (Section 11), issuing no
       authorization code or profile-defined grant.  A deferred or
       relocated approval flow (Section 14) inherits this check at its
       own creation commit, with the completion error each flow defines.

   The authority_hash is the *authority commitment*: it commits, by
   cryptographic digest, exactly the authority the Approver approved.

   Section 6.3.4 states how the AS deduplicates a retried or duplicate
   delivery of the same approval decision on approval_event_id.

6.1.3.  Subject Establishment

   These rules apply at step 2 of the direct approval steps
   (Section 6.1.2).

   *  *Approval for another principal.* When the Approver is a different
      principal (for example, an administrator approving on a user's
      behalf), the AS MUST NOT take the Subject from unauthenticated
      client input, and MUST authorize the Approver to approve for that
      Subject under local policy.  This document defines no wire
      parameter for the Subject; how the AS establishes it
      (administrative selection, a directory, an authenticated
      reference) is a deployment matter.

   *  *External Subject.* When the Subject's home issuer (subject.iss)
      differs from the AS, the AS MUST map the external (subject.iss,
      subject.sub) pair to an AS-local sub under an injective mapping
      (no two distinct external Subjects map to the same local sub).  A
      derived token's (iss, sub) pair then denotes exactly one Subject.
      Adopting the external sub verbatim is valid only where it collides
      with no other principal's sub.

6.1.4.  Rendering Safeguards

   The consent rendering is hardened against client text:

   *  Client-supplied strings (goal, task_bounds) MUST be rendered as
      inert text and MUST NOT be interpreted as markup.

   *  The AS SHOULD mitigate Unicode direction-override and confusable-
      character presentation in them.

   *  The rendering MUST visually distinguish the AS-derived Authority
      Set from client-supplied text and attribute that text to the
      client, identified from client identity information the AS trusts
      (Section 6.1.2.1), so crafted client text cannot pass as derived
      authority or as a statement of the AS.

   The rendering presents goal and task_bounds as recorded, without
   truncation: the Intent is recorded verbatim and committed by
   intent_hash (Section 6.4.1), so a truncated rendering would show the
   Approver less than the record holds.

   Rendering a bound is not the same as enforcing it: a deployment MUST
   NOT present a rendered bound as enforced when no party enforces it.
   Which party enforces each bound, and what holds when that party is
   absent, follows from Enforcement Requirements (Section 9.1.1) and,
   for a resource server that enforces only scope, from Scope Projection
   (Section 7.7).  An AS SHOULD make clear to the Approver which
   rendered bounds its deployment actually enforces, so consent is not
   given to a limit that binds nowhere.

6.1.5.  Changes Before the Decision

   If any of the following changes between approval rendering and the
   approval decision, the AS MUST recompute the affected values and the
   anchors the Mission records, and MUST NOT create the Mission without
   the Approver's consent to the changed context:

   *  the task or the authority proposal;

   *  the derived Authority Set;

   *  the established authority source (Section 6.1.7);

   *  the effective expires_at; or

   *  a policy input establishing any of them.

   Each anchor (intent_hash, authority_hash, and proposal_hash where a
   proposal was submitted) is computed over the approved context.
   Because the proposal is committed separately, a proposal swapped
   between rendering and decision changes proposal_hash even where
   intent_hash is unchanged.

6.1.6.  Approval Comprehension

   The approval event commits the Mission Intent and Authority Set the
   AS records as approved.  Those commitments establish neither that the
   AS rendered them faithfully nor that the Approver understood them
   (Section 16.1.1).  Step 5 of Section 6.1.2 requires human-meaningful
   rendering; comprehension cannot be inferred from an approval or its
   integrity anchors.

   Mission Consent Evidence
   ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-consent-evidence]) defines
   layered rendering of a committed disclosure, including what its first
   layer carries and practices for a large or varied Authority Set, and
   lets the Approver ask the basis for an entry before deciding.

6.1.7.  Authority Sources

   A Mission draws its authority from one of three sources:

   *  *user-delegated*: a delegating person's own authority;

   *  *service-owned*: a workload's own provisioned authority; or

   *  *organizational*: explicitly governed organizational policy with a
      named accountable owner.

   The source names whose authority the approval draws on, recorded
   immutably as the Mission's authority_source; approval_basis records
   how drawing on it was activated (Section 6.3).  The two compose: any
   source may activate under the direct basis or a standing-consent
   basis a companion defines.

   Approval activates only authority the source already holds: the AS
   establishes the source and verifies the derived Authority Set against
   it before approval (Section 6.1.2, Section 6.2.1).

   The subject-representation discipline is the same in every source:

   *  The accountable principal is the record's
      approval_basis.consent_principal in every source; it MUST NOT be
      inferred from the token sub.

   *  sub carries a delegating person only in the user-delegated source.
      A service-owned or organizational Mission MUST record the workload
      or organizational principal as subject and MUST NOT record a human
      principal in its place.  The injective mapping of Section 6.1.3
      applies unchanged: that principal receives its own AS-local sub,
      denotes itself, and impersonates nobody.  It MUST be an
      authorization subject the AS recognizes as a resource owner in its
      own right (the sub model of [RFC9068]), not only the task's
      beneficiary.

   *  The actor model does not vary by source: the Mission Record's
      client_id names the Agent, a token's client_id names the client
      that requested it (Section 7.2), and delegates are carried in the
      act chain (Section 8).

6.1.8.  Approver Authentication Strength

   A deployment MUST publish a statement declaring the minimum approval-
   authentication strength (the approval-authentication floor) it
   enforces for Missions whose derived Authority Set carries high-risk
   authority, and the deployment scope it applies to.  This document
   requires no particular serialization.

   High-risk authority means irreversible, external-commitment, or
   privileged-administration actions under the deployment's
   classification, or a consumption bound
   ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-mission-metering]).

6.1.8.1.  Requested Strength

   For the direct flow, a client MAY additionally request an approval-
   authentication strength on the authorization request using the
   acr_values and max_age parameters defined in Section 3.1.2.1 of
   [OpenID.Core].

   In a Mission approval interaction, these parameters describe the
   requested authentication of the *Approver*, not of the token's
   Subject, who can be a different principal (Section 6.1.3).
   Requesting them implies nothing about the authentication claims of an
   issued token: those claims describe the token's Subject, not the
   Approver (Section 7.6).  When the Approver is the Subject, one
   authentication event can back both.

   The Approver's authentication satisfies acr_values when it matches
   any one listed value under the deployment's own policy mapping.  This
   document defines no global ordering of acr values.  The max_age
   parameter bounds the elapsed time since that authentication.

   Approval authentication for a high-risk Mission MUST satisfy both the
   published floor and, where the client requested one, the acr_values/
