   max_age carriage above; the floor is never relaxed by a narrower or
   absent client request.

6.1.8.2.  OpenID Connect Requests

   This document does not require an AS to support OpenID Connect
   [OpenID.Core].

   An authorization request whose scope includes openid asks for an ID
   Token about the End-User the approval interaction authenticates, who
   is the Approver.  When the Approver is not the Subject established at
   step 2 of Section 6.1.2, the AS MUST refuse such a request, without
   creating the Mission, with the invalid_scope error code
   (Section 4.1.2.1 of [RFC6749]).

   When the Approver is not the Subject, whether or not openid was
   requested, the AS MUST NOT issue an ID Token, serve UserInfo, or
   establish an authentication session for the Subject solely as a
   result of the Approver's authentication or approval.

   When the Approver is the Subject, the openid scope value and the
   acr_values and max_age parameters keep their [OpenID.Core] meaning,
   which then describes the same authentication this document requires.

   In every approval interaction, the prompt, login_hint, and
   id_token_hint parameters concern the Approver.

6.1.8.3.  Achieved Authentication

   The authentication actually achieved for the approval event (acr,
   amr, and auth_time, in the sense [RFC9470] and [OpenID.Core] define
   them) is approval-time provenance, not requested Intent.  This
   document never records it as a Mission Intent member, and an AS MUST
   NOT read it back from a Mission Intent member.

   Where a deployment records Consent Evidence, the achieved values
   belong in its authentication_context member
   ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-consent-evidence]).  This
   document defines no Mission Record member for them.

6.1.9.  Single Accountable Approver

   This document records exactly one accountable principal,
   approval_basis.consent_principal: the principal who approved the
   Mission.  Two richer patterns are out of scope:

   *  *Multi-party approval* (M-of-N or dual control), where more than
      one principal must approve.  The number of approvers does not
      change the authority commitment: principals approving the same
      rendered Authority Set produce the same authority_hash and the
      same derived tokens.  A deployment requiring dual control records
      one accountable Approver; this document does not represent the co-
      approvers.

   *  *Approval-authority provenance*: the standing behind a delegate's
      authority to approve for another principal (for example, whether
      an administrator was entitled to approve on a user's behalf).
      This is governance state about the delegate, not the standing-
      consent basis that approval_basis records (Section 6.3).

   Where a deployment needs either, the Approval Governance Record
   ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-mission-approval-governance]) records it, and
   Consent Evidence can carry a partial presentation of that record
   ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-consent-evidence]).

   The adjudication.governance_record member (Section 6.3) marks an
   approval such a record backs, and
   [I-D.draft-mcguinness-mission-architecture] (Section "Approval Role
   Mapping") shows how the common creation scenarios assign the three
   approval_basis roles.

   The issuer retains the Approval Governance Record, and this document
   defines no surface that discloses it.  An artifact that refers to it
   does so by its record digest
   ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-mission-approval-governance],
   Section "Conventions and Definitions").  The record binds a
   governance evaluation to the Mission's approval event.  It does not,
   by itself, establish a separate business decision for each subsequent
   action.

   Authenticating approval evidence does not, by itself, establish that
   conditions governing later reliance remain satisfied.  Whether
   subsequent changes to an Approver's role, a delegation, or governing
   policy affect an existing Mission depends on the deployment's policy.

6.2.  Standing-Consent Bases

   A companion profile can generalize approval to a named standing-
   consent basis.  Under it, a template or policy activates Mission
   instances against an accountable human's earlier approval, with no
   fresh human approval per instance; each instance is still created at
   its own approval event (Section 6.1).  The companion profile defines
   the basis's approval_basis.type value (Section 6.3.3);
   [I-D.draft-mcguinness-mission-architecture] (Section "Mission
   Invariants") lists the standing-consent bases that companion profiles
   define.  The type values form a specification-defined namespace with
   no registry (Section 14.2): each further basis is defined by the
   companion profile that introduces it.

   For a standing-consent type, activation takes the shape that its
   companion profile defines, and activation_actor names a dispatching
   or requesting party distinct from the consenting human.

   An unrecognized type or adjudication.kind is handled as Section 6.3.3
   states for every record consumer.

   A companion profile defining a standing-consent type MUST make its
   consent_principal and root_commitment trace to an accountable human's
   approval of the named standing consent, with no fresh human approval
   per instance, MUST carry that approval's instant as approved_at, and
   MUST state what the record's approval_event_id carries for an
   instance it activates.  That value identifies this instance's
   approval event, never the standing approval, so a retried activation
   deduplicates and each distinct activation creates its own Mission.

   approved_at:  REQUIRED for every standing-consent type.  An RFC 3339
      date-time: the instant the accountable human approved the exact
      consented root that root_commitment commits (the template version,
      the drawdown policy version), not the instant this Mission
      instance was activated.  The activating issuer MUST verify
      approved_at against its retained, authenticated record of that
      standing consent for that exact version; it MUST NOT accept the
      value as the activating request's own uncorroborated assertion.

   The adjudication member (Section 6.3.3), where present, follows the
   basis.  Where a companion profile defining a standing-consent type
   populates adjudication, kind MUST be policy, naming the identity and
   version of the policy or workflow that adjudicated the instance.  A
   companion profile MUST NOT flatten a policy's or an Approval
   Governance Record's assertion set into a single principal member.

6.2.1.  Standing-Consent Instances

   Under a standing-consent basis (Section 6.2), the standing consent
   and the instance's adjudication supply the human decision that a
   direct approval event makes in Section 6.1.2: the Approver's
   authentication (step 1), the Approver's authorization for the Subject
   and the authority source (steps 2 and 3), and the consent rendering
   (step 5).  The other creation rules still bind the instance's
   approval event wherever they apply:

   *  the Subject is never taken from unauthenticated client input and
      is mapped injectively (step 2, Section 6.1.3);

   *  the authority source and its ceiling (Section 6.1.7);

   *  the effective expiry and its creation-commit check (steps 4 and
      7);

   *  the integrity anchors (step 6); and

   *  atomic creation deduplicated on approval_event_id (Section 6.3).

6.2.2.  Activation Policy Commitment

   Where a standing-consent basis lets a separate policy artifact
   adjudicate activation (a dispatch, drawdown, or child-creation
   policy, rather than the consented object itself), the object the
   accountable human consented to MUST carry that policy as an
   activation policy reference: an object of id (a string), version (a
   string), and digest.  The digest is an integrity anchor
   (Section 6.4.1) whose typ is mission-activation-policy, whose iss is
   the activating issuer, and whose value is an object of content_type,
   the media type of the policy snapshot, and content, the base64url,
   no-padding [RFC4648] encoding of the snapshot's exact bytes.  Before
   each activation, the activating issuer MUST compute digest over the
   exact snapshot it evaluates, and MUST NOT activate the instance under
   that policy when the result differs; the companion profile fixes what
   follows, as it does for an activation the policy does not authorize.
   The issuer MUST retain each snapshot's bytes and media type for the
   audit horizon (Section 6.3.4) of every Mission it activates under
   that snapshot, so an auditor can reproduce the digest.  Where no
   separate policy artifact exists, as when child creation is
   adjudicated against the approved delegation entry itself, this rule
   does not apply.

6.2.3.  Standing-Consent Recency

   A deployment can declare a maximum standing-consent age (a recency
   ceiling), overall or per consequence class.  Where a declared ceiling
   applies, the activating issuer MUST refuse to activate an instance
   whose approved_at is older than the ceiling.  Where ceilings are
   declared:

   *  The evaluation instant is the atomic Mission-creation commit
      (Section 6.1), including the creation commit of a deferred or
      relocated flow.  Recency is issuance-time eligibility only: a
      later change to a ceiling, or the passage of time past one, does
      not terminate an active Mission (Section 10.2).

   *  The activating issuer MUST refuse an approved_at later than the
      evaluation instant by more than the deployment's declared, bounded
      clock-skew allowance.

   *  Where ceilings are declared per consequence class, the issuer MUST
      classify the committed Mission from the derived Authority Set and
      from any consumption bound the Mission Intent carries (for
      example, [I-D.draft-mcguinness-mission-metering]), and MUST apply
      the strictest ceiling across every class present.

   *  The ceilings and the skew allowance belong to the versioned policy
      that policy_version identifies, or to a separately versioned
      declaration retained with it, so an auditor can reproduce the
      eligibility decision.

6.3.  Mission Record

   A Mission is the durable record created at the approval event; a
   Mission Identifier (Section 6.3.5) identifies it.  Its members MUST
   NOT be changed after creation, except state and the termination
   member written once when the Mission terminates (Section 10.1).

   The record is a logical object: this document constrains its members
   and their values and requires no storage form.  Where a surface or
   profile exchanges or signs the record, the record's JSON form, with
   the members this section defines, governs.

   Record member names omit the mission prefix because the record is the
   Mission.  Prefixed names, such as the mission_intent request
   parameter and the mission_id response parameter, belong to surfaces
   that reference a Mission from outside it.

   Member names are spelled out (issuer, expires_at, created_at) and
   spelled identically on every surface.  The compact JWT names (iss,
   exp, iat) describe a signed artifact's own envelope, or identify a
   party in an {iss, sub} object, not the Mission.

   Like the mission claim (Section 7.8), the record is open
   (Section 14): a companion profile of this document MAY record
   additional members set at creation using short names coordinated with
   it (for example, a lineage member linking the Mission to a
   predecessor or parent); any other extension MUST use collision-
   resistant names.

   The following subsections define the record's members.

6.3.1.  Identity and Lifecycle Members

   id:  REQUIRED.  A string.  The canonical Mission Identifier
      (Section 6.3.5).

   issuer:  REQUIRED.  A string.  The issuer URL of the Mission Issuer
      that approved the Mission.  Equals the iss of tokens that AS
      derives; for cross-domain tokens it remains the issuer AS that
      approved the Mission even though the issuing iss differs
      ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-cross-domain]).

   state:  REQUIRED.  A string.  The current lifecycle state: active or
      terminated in this document, or an additional state defined by a
      companion profile, subject to the forward-compatibility rule of
      Section 10.

   termination:  CONDITIONAL.  An object, present exactly when state is
      terminated: the reason and instant the Mission terminated
      (Section 10.1).

6.3.2.  Approved Content and Commitments

   intent:  REQUIRED.  An object.  The approved Mission Intent.

   proposed_authority:  REQUIRED when the client submitted an authority
      proposal (Section 4.3), absent otherwise.  An array: the submitted
      authorization_details array, recorded exactly as submitted.  A
      Mission derived in configured-mapping mode (Section 5) records
      none.

   authority_set:  REQUIRED.  An array.  The consented Authority Set.

   authority_hash:  REQUIRED.  A string.  The authority commitment over
      the Authority Set (Section 6.4.1).  It is not carried on the
      baseline mission claim (Section 7.8).

   intent_hash:  REQUIRED.  A string.  The integrity commitment over the
      approved Mission Intent (Section 6.4.1), making the recorded task
      tamper-evident.

   proposal_hash:  REQUIRED when proposed_authority is present, absent
      otherwise.  A string.  The integrity commitment over the recorded
      proposed_authority (Section 6.4.1).  It is approval-time
      provenance, not enforcement input, and is not carried on the
      mission claim (Section 7.8).

   submission_evidence:  REQUIRED when the approved submission carried
      evidence, absent otherwise.  An array.  The verified Intent
      Submission Evidence facts (Section 4.4), one element per verified
      entry, in the submission's evidence order so the array has one
      canonical form (Section 6.4.2).  Each element carries exactly
      these members:

      type:  The entry's evidence type.

      artifact_hash:  An integrity anchor (Section 6.4.1) with typ
         mission-intent-evidence over the entry exactly as presented.

      verified_at:  An RFC 3339 timestamp of verification.

      facts:  An object holding the verified output facts the type's
         specification selects for recording, nested so type-owned facts
         cannot collide with the common members.

      submission_evidence is provenance, not enforcement input, and is
      not carried on the mission claim (Section 7.8).  Recorded facts,
      such as a verified consent reference, are provenance and policy
      input only; a resource domain validates them under its own current
      policy (Section 17.4).

      No integrity anchor commits submission_evidence: its digests are
      only as trustworthy as the Mission Record that holds them and do
      not independently prove which artifacts were presented at
      admission.  A profile whose threat model requires that proof
      commits normalized provenance under its own anchor typ
      (Section 6.4.1, Section 14).

6.3.3.  Principals and Approval Basis

   subject:  REQUIRED.  An object.  The Subject, an object with iss and
      sub.

   approval_basis:  REQUIRED.  An object.  The approved authorization
      basis this Mission is rooted in, fixed at the approval event and
      immutable thereafter, like subject.  This document defines the
      direct basis in full; a companion profile can define a standing-
      consent basis (Section 6.2).  The members below apply to every
      approval basis, with the presence each one states.  Three of these
      members answer the questions an approval raises: who consented
      (consent_principal), who triggered this instance
      (activation_actor), and what mechanism decided it (adjudication).

      type:  REQUIRED.  A string: direct, or a standing-consent value
         that a companion profile defines (Section 6.2).

      An unrecognized type, and likewise an unrecognized
      adjudication.kind (Section 6.3), is preserved unchanged as opaque
      provenance on an otherwise valid record: a consumer MUST NOT infer
      or fabricate the human, policy, or Approval Governance Record
      standing behind it, and MUST refuse only a profile operation that
      itself requires recognized adjudication semantics to proceed (for
      example, evaluating a policy-approval recency ceiling).  Unlike an
      unrecognized lifecycle state (Section 10), an unrecognized value
      here does not by itself invalidate or deactivate the Mission.

      consent_principal:  REQUIRED.  An object with iss and sub.  The
         accountable human (or human-accountable principal) who
         consented.  MAY equal subject.

      activation:  REQUIRED.  An object naming what activated this
         Mission instance, shaped by type.  For direct:
         approval_event_id, mirroring the record's own approval_event_id
         (below).

      activation_actor:  REQUIRED.  An object with iss and sub.  Who or
         what triggered this instance.  For direct it equals
         consent_principal: the Approver triggers their own approval.

      root_commitment:  REQUIRED.  A string.  The commitment to the
         consented root: an integrity anchor where the root is a
         committed object, otherwise the committed reference that
         identifies it.  For direct, this Mission's own authority_hash.

      adjudication:  OPTIONAL.  A discriminated object naming the
         decision _mechanism_ that adjudicated this instance: distinct
         from activation_actor (who triggered it) and consent_principal
         (who is accountable for it).  Present when a Mission-creating
         profile or deployment chooses to make the mechanism explicit;
         where absent, the mechanism is still fixed by type and the
         construction rules of this document or a companion profile
         (below and Section 6.2).  Members, where present:

         kind:  REQUIRED.  A string: human or policy, naming a decision
            mechanism, not a storage location for supporting evidence; a
            companion profile MUST NOT define a kind value that names a
            record, an evidence store, or the requesting or dispatching
            party.  An unrecognized value is handled under the same rule
            as approval_basis.type (Section 6.3.3).

         governance_record:  OPTIONAL.  A boolean. true when an Approval
            Governance Record is recorded for this approval event
            ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-mission-approval-governance]), joined
            by this Mission's own approval_event_id.

            *  It never changes kind, which follows the basis (below and
               Section 6.2).

            *  Approval Governance aligns the record's accountable
               assertion, where it carries one, with that kind: a
               governed decision still names its mechanism, and the
               record supplies the fuller assertion set behind it,
               including any multi-assertion set, never flattened into
               it.

         For kind: human: no additional kind-specific members; the
         deciding human is consent_principal.

         For kind: policy: policy, a REQUIRED object with id and version
         identifying the deciding policy or workflow.  It decides
         deterministically over recorded inputs, so re-evaluating that
         version over them re-checks the decision.  A model's output can
         be one such input, recorded with the model's identifier and
         version: it can refuse an activation or narrow the authority it
         activates, and the AS MUST NOT let it supply or widen
         authority.  A model is never itself the deciding policy or
         workflow.

      approved_at:  Absent for direct, whose approval event carries its
         own instant (Section 6.1); a standing-consent basis carries it
         (Section 6.2).

      The following rules apply to approval_basis:

      *  Where adjudication is present for direct, kind MUST be human,
         with or without governance_record: a policy decides an instance
         only under a standing-consent basis (Section 6.2).

      *  For direct, activation.approval_event_id MUST identify a human
         approval event (Section 6.1).  Approval Governance states how a
         record backing a direct approval keeps that human decision as
         its accountable assertion
         ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-mission-approval-governance]).

      *  approval_basis is provenance: none of the record's integrity
         anchors, such as intent_hash, authority_hash or proposal_hash,
         covers it (Section 6.4.1); a profile that commits the Mission
         Record itself covers it under that profile's own anchor.  Two
         optional profiles commit it (Section 16.1.1).

      *  Its disclosure through introspection follows Section 9.2.1.

      *  It is not carried on the baseline mission claim (Section 7.8),
         and it MUST NOT be relied on to grant or widen authority
         wherever it does appear.

   authority_source:  REQUIRED.  An object.  The source of the authority
      the approval draws on (Section 6.1.7), established at the approval
      event (Section 6.1) and immutable thereafter, like approval_basis.
      Members:

      type:  REQUIRED.  A string: user_delegated, service_owned, or
         organizational.  An unrecognized value is handled under the
         same rule as an unrecognized approval_basis.type
         (Section 6.3.3): it is preserved unchanged as opaque provenance
         and does not by itself invalidate or deactivate the Mission,
         and only an operation that itself requires recognized source
         semantics refuses (for example, applying the source's authority
         ceiling or its governed policy).  An AS MUST NOT approve a
         Mission whose authority source it does not recognize.

      policy:  REQUIRED for organizational, absent otherwise.  An object
         with id, version, and digest: the stable reference to, and
         commitment over, the governed organizational policy the Mission
         draws on.

         *  digest is a prefixed commitment (Section 6.4.3) whose exact
            committed input and construction the policy's format
            specifies: its species and, for an envelope anchor, its typ,
            iss, and value.

         *  The party that produces the reference computes digest; a
            consumer holding the policy can reproduce it, and one that
            does not compares it as an opaque value.

      authority_source is provenance like approval_basis: recorded
      alongside it, folded into neither intent_hash nor authority_hash,
      and not carried on access tokens; resource servers enforce
      authorization_details and do not consult it.

   client_id:  REQUIRED.  A string.  The Agent (OAuth client) that
      submitted the Mission Intent.

6.3.4.  Provenance and Expiry

   policy_version:  REQUIRED.  A string.  An opaque audit correlator
      naming the derivation policy version in effect at the approval
      event; the policy itself does not travel.

   approval_event_id:  REQUIRED.  A string.  A unique identifier of the
      approval event, used as the approval idempotency key: a retried or
      duplicate delivery of the same approval decision (a replayed
      callback, a double-submitted consent form, a retried standing-
      consent activation) MUST NOT create a second Mission, and the AS
      deduplicates on this identifier.  The identifier names the
      approval event that creates this Mission (Section 6.1); under a
      standing-consent basis (Section 6.2), it identifies this
      instance's creation, not the earlier standing approval.

   created_at:  REQUIRED.  A string.  RFC 3339 timestamp of creation.

   expires_at:  REQUIRED.  A string.  An RFC 3339 date-time: the AS-
      established effective Mission expiry (Section 10, Section 7.1).
      It MUST be later than created_at and MUST NOT be later than
      intent.expires_at, the requested ceiling (Section 4).

      Shortening under applicable AS policy, or under an already-
      approved parent, predecessor, or standing-consent bound a Mission-
      creating profile defines, is ordinary narrowing of the granted
      lifetime, not Authority Set derivation; for direct creation under
      this document the only additional ceiling is applicable AS policy.

   The *audit horizon* is the deployment-declared retention window for
   the Mission Record and its evidence: at least the Mission's lifetime
   plus a declared post-expiry period.  A deployment retains a
   terminated Mission's record for its audit horizon.

6.3.5.  Mission Identifier Format

   A Mission Identifier is an opaque URL-safe ASCII string of [A-Za-
   z0-9_-] characters, with at least 128 bits of entropy, carrying no
   semantic content.  The AS MUST NOT reuse a Mission Identifier.  The
   record and the mission claim carry it as id; a surface that
   references a Mission from outside carries it as mission_id, as in the
   token-response parameter (Section 7.3.1).

6.3.6.  Mission Record Example

   The following is an example of a Mission Record:

   =============== NOTE: '\' line wrapping per RFC 8792 ===============

   {
     "id": "msn_8RfX2Lqv9TqMv4z7sA2bN1k0YpEdHc9-",
     "issuer": "https://as.example.com",
     "state": "active",
     "intent": {
       "goal": "Reconcile Q3 invoices and post adjustments of at most \
         USD 500 per journal entry.",
       "target_resources": ["https://erp.example.com"],
       "task_bounds": [
         "Read only invoices issued in 2026-Q3.",
         "Post journal entries of at most USD 500 each."
       ],
       "purpose": "urn:example:purpose:reconcile",
       "expires_at": "2026-12-31T23:59:59Z" },
     "proposed_authority": [
       { "type": "mission_resource_access",
         "resource": "https://erp.example.com",
         "actions": ["invoices.*"],
         "constraints": {
           "resource_issued_after": "2026-07-01T00:00:00Z",
           "resource_issued_before": "2026-09-30T23:59:59Z"
         },
         "delegation": {
           "max_depth": 2,
           "allowed_delegates": [{ "sub_profile": "ai_agent" }]
         } },
       { "type": "mission_resource_access",
         "resource": "https://erp.example.com",
         "actions": ["journal-entries.write"],
         "constraints": {
           "max_amount": { "amount": "500.00", "currency": "USD" }
         } }
     ],
     "authority_set": [
       { "type": "mission_resource_access",
         "resource": "https://erp.example.com",
         "actions": ["invoices.read"],
         "constraints": {
           "resource_issued_after": "2026-07-01T00:00:00Z",
           "resource_issued_before": "2026-09-30T23:59:59Z"
         },
         "delegation": {
           "max_depth": 2,
           "allowed_delegates": [{ "sub_profile": "ai_agent" }]
         } },
       { "type": "mission_resource_access",
         "resource": "https://erp.example.com",
         "actions": ["journal-entries.write"],
         "constraints": {
           "max_amount": { "amount": "500.00", "currency": "USD" }
         } }
     ],
     "authority_hash":
       "sha-256:hjXlwWBY64-RPs1Z-E_gbyklNcViI5c9jiyMbE4KvFM",
     "intent_hash":
       "sha-256:6T-7s_f-mNW9UpfrrZh3r0HRJmU4My_EsNAf1C1U4r0",
     "proposal_hash":
       "sha-256:p-s-EzdNQTn6RoPHXESkwVtGdk8yL1PxryNyMwroKGo",
     "subject": { "iss": "https://idp.example.com",
       "sub": "user_3p2q8mN1a0kV7tR" },
     "approval_basis": {
       "type": "direct",
       "consent_principal": { "iss": "https://idp.example.com",
         "sub": "user_3p2q8mN1a0kV7tR" },
       "activation": { "approval_event_id": "ape_8K2nP4qV9rL3tY6sB1z" },
       "activation_actor": { "iss": "https://idp.example.com",
         "sub": "user_3p2q8mN1a0kV7tR" },
       "adjudication": { "kind": "human" },
       "root_commitment":
         "sha-256:hjXlwWBY64-RPs1Z-E_gbyklNcViI5c9jiyMbE4KvFM"
     },
     "authority_source": { "type": "user_delegated" },
     "client_id": "s6BhdRkqt3",
     "policy_version": "deploy-policy:v17",
     "approval_event_id": "ape_8K2nP4qV9rL3tY6sB1z",
     "created_at": "2026-11-02T08:00:00Z",
     "expires_at": "2026-12-31T23:59:59Z"
   }

   The following example shows an excerpt, other members omitted, of a
   Mission for an agent acting for its own workload principal: subject
   is the workload principal, client_id is the agent, and
   consent_principal is the administrator who approved it under the
   service_owned source.

   {
     "subject": { "iss": "https://login.example.com",
       "sub": "agt_ledger_reconciler" },
     "client_id": "ledger-reconciler",
     "authority_source": { "type": "service_owned" },
     "approval_basis": {
       "type": "direct",
       "consent_principal": { "iss": "https://login.example.com",
         "sub": "adm_4Hq9Tz" },
       "activation": { "approval_event_id": "ape_2Lw7Kq9Rv4" },
       "activation_actor": { "iss": "https://login.example.com",
         "sub": "adm_4Hq9Tz" },
       "root_commitment":
         "sha-256:R6tY2nD9bM7sX1cF8gH2vJ4kE5pNQl3KvZ4mP5x0wQo"
     }
   }

   The first example's anchors are computed over its displayed intent,
   proposed_authority and authority_set (Section 6.4.1); the excerpt's
   root_commitment is illustrative.  A companion that extends this
   example either reproduces the recorded objects byte-exactly or states
   that its example diverges and its anchors differ.

6.4.  Integrity and Commitments

   This section defines how the anchors the Approver consents to at the
   approval event (Section 6.1) are constructed, canonicalized, and
   bound.  The Mission Record (Section 6.3) records them, and the test
   vectors (Appendix D) pin the construction byte-for-byte.

6.4.1.  Integrity Anchors

   Every anchor is computed the same way over a domain-separated,
   issuer-bound envelope:

   1.  Construct the envelope, where typ selects the committed object
       and value is that object:

      {
        "typ": "<mission-intent | mission-proposed-authority
                | mission-authority-set | mission-activation-policy>",
        "iss": "<the AS issuer URL>",
        "value": <the committed object>
      }

       Each anchor sets typ and value as follows:

       *  For intent_hash, typ is mission-intent and value is the
          approved Mission Intent object: the Submission envelope's
          intent member, not the envelope or its evidence (Section 4.2).

       *  For proposal_hash, typ is mission-proposed-authority and value
          is the submitted authorization_details array exactly as
          recorded (Section 4.3); the anchor is present exactly when a
          proposal was submitted.

       *  For authority_hash, typ is mission-authority-set and value is
          the Authority Set as a JSON array of entries.

       *  For an activation policy reference's digest, typ is mission-
          activation-policy and value is the policy snapshot object that
          Section 6.2.2 defines.

   2.  Canonicalize the envelope with JCS [RFC8785].

   3.  Compute SHA-256 [RFC6234] over the canonical bytes.

   4.  Encode as sha-256: followed by the base64url, no-padding
       [RFC4648] encoding of the digest.

   The typ field domain-separates the anchors so a digest of one object
   cannot be mistaken for another's.  The iss binding prevents a
   committed object from being transplanted across authorization
   servers.  The sha-256: prefix is the algorithm-agility mechanism
   (Section 6.4.3).

   The anchors this section lists, and the Authority Set entry
   commitment below, bind the AS issuer.  The digest of an
   authority_source.policy reference (Section 6.3.3) is not one of them:
   it uses the issuer the policy's format defines, for a governed
   organizational policy its governing authority.  That binding keeps
   the policy's commitment from being transplanted across policy
   authorities, and the same policy commits identically at every AS that
   records it.

   intent_hash and authority_hash are independent commitments to
   independent objects.  That the approved task bounds the derived
   authority is a governance assertion, made by derivation policy and
   auditable through policy_version (Section 5), not a cryptographic
   relation between the anchors: neither anchor proves anything about
   the other's object.

   The typ value space is an extension point (Section 14): additional
   committed objects use this same envelope with a new typ and the
   canonicalization below.

   This document defines no registry of typ values; each committing
   specification defines its own and relies on the typ domain
   separation.  To keep that domain separation safe without a registry,
   a new typ value MUST be a collision-resistant name (for example, a
   short name prefixed within a namespace the defining profile controls,
   following the Collision-Resistant Name guidance of Section 2 of
   [RFC7519]). mission- prefixed values, used by this document and the
   profiles that extend it, form a namespace coordinated through this
   document's change controller.

   This document also defines the *Authority Set entry commitment*, the
   envelope above with typ mission-authority-entry, iss the Mission
   issuer, and value a single Authority Set entry object exactly as
   recorded (Appendix D).  A companion that cites an entry by digest (a
   decision record naming the entry it evaluated, containment or
   completion state keyed to an entry) computes it this way.

   Entries whose canonical commitment envelopes are identical produce
   the same digest, and within one Mission Record every recorded entry
   resolving to the same digest forms one selector equivalence class;
   the class is defined by the canonical bytes, not by pre-canonical
   source text the record does not preserve.

   The commitment is not a globally unique entry identifier: the
   envelope binds the issuer, not the Mission, so a protocol that uses
   it to select or cite an entry MUST bind it to the Mission issuer and
   Mission Identifier whose recorded Authority Set is searched, directly
   or through an enclosing object whose integrity protection binds them.

   authority_hash likewise commits an Authority Set, not a Mission: two
   Missions that approve byte-identical authority share it, and a
   consumer MUST NOT use it as a Mission Identifier or as a replay or
   idempotency key for a Mission.

6.4.2.  Canonicalization Rules

   JCS [RFC8785] alone does not make two implementations agree on every
   byte.  The following rules close the remaining gaps; they apply to
   computing an anchor and to comparing committed values:

   *  The committed value is exactly the object the AS recorded on the
      Mission: the approved intent for intent_hash, the recorded
      proposed_authority for proposal_hash, and the authority_set for
      authority_hash.  An auditor reproduces a digest from the record
      alone.

   *  Duplicate member names are rejected at parse time (Section 6.4.3).

   *  JCS does not reorder array elements, and this document defines no
      element sorting, so array order is significant.  The AS MUST
      present each committed array in its recorded order wherever it
      emits the committed object; that order is part of the canonical
      form.

   *  URI-valued members are compared byte-for-byte, with no
      normalization (Section 12 of [RFC9396]), unless a member's own
      type definition specifies a normalization.  Where a type defines
      one, as mission_resource_access does for its prefix-match resource
      containment test
      ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-resource-access]), it applies
      to that comparison alone: the default resource equality test
      remains an exact match, and anchor computation is always byte-
      exact over the recorded values.

6.4.3.  Commitment Mechanisms

   This document's default prefixed construction commits to bytes in
   three forms, called species, and a specification defining a prefixed
   commitment classifies it as one of them:

   *  *Envelope anchor*: the domain-separated, issuer-bound envelope of
      Section 6.4.1 (intent_hash, proposal_hash, authority_hash, an
      activation policy reference's digest, and commitments produced
      with companion-defined typ values).

   *  *Canonical-object digest*: sha-256: over the JCS serialization of
      a normalized JSON object without the envelope, where protocol
      context already fixes what is committed (for example, a runtime
      parameter digest).

   *  *Raw-octet digest*: sha-256: over an exact, specification-defined
      octet sequence, with no canonicalization: a whole artifact as
      exchanged, or the UTF-8 encoding of a defined scalar value (for
      example, a work-product artifact digest).

   The prefix and agility rules below bind all three species.  The
   I-JSON rule binds the two JSON species.  The envelope and typ
   discipline of Section 6.4.1 binds envelope anchors alone.

   This section instantiates the default commitment construction of
   [I-D.draft-mcguinness-mission-substrate].  A commitment outside this
   construction (a native content address, a member-named digest whose
   member name fixes the algorithm) is permitted; its defining
   specification states its own algorithm identification and agility
   behavior.

   Every committed JSON value and its envelope are I-JSON [RFC7493]
   data, as Section 3.1 of [RFC8785] requires.  Strengthening
   Section 3.1 of [RFC8785], which adapts input to I-JSON, the party
   computing or verifying a commitment MUST reject non-conformant input
   before canonicalization rather than adapt it: externally received
   JSON destined for commitment is parsed by a duplicate-detecting
   parser, and an object carrying duplicate member names is rejected at
   parse time, before the parsed data model exists.

   The commitment is over the parsed I-JSON data value, not the source
   text: JCS does not preserve a source lexeme's spelling or excess
   precision.  A profile whose values need exact decimal or large-
   integer semantics carries them as strings, as Section 3.1 of
   [RFC8785] recommends, or defines a stricter numeric domain, as the
   Mission Resource Access Profile's Common Constraints do for
   constraint values
   ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-resource-access]).

   The security considerations of [RFC8785] apply to every JCS
   computation.

   The algorithm prefix is the agility mechanism. sha-256 is mandatory
   to implement and the only algorithm this document defines.  A new
   algorithm enters only through a new prefix defined by a referencing
   specification, its name drawn from the Named Information Hash
   Algorithm Registry ([RFC6920]); this document defines no negotiation.

   A verifier MUST reject a digest whose algorithm prefix it does not
   recognize, so an algorithm added later cannot be exploited as a
   downgrade.  These rules bind a prefixed digest of any species when
   its defining specification classifies it under this taxonomy and
   imports this section normatively; this document so classifies every
   anchor it defines.

   This document defines no transition mechanism: every commitment a
   current carrier defines is a single prefixed string, and no carrier
   defines a location for a second one.  A specification that introduces
   a new prefix MUST define:

   1.  the carrier and schema of any parallel commitment;

   2.  the binding that proves the old and new values commit to the same
       object;

   3.  producer behavior during the transition;

   4.  verifier selection and downgrade behavior when recognition sets
       differ; and

   5.  the transition procedure itself.

6.5.  Binding the Mission to the Grant

   A Mission is independent of any OAuth grant: it is identified
   globally by the pair (issuer, id) (Section 6.3), and it can be
   approved, tracked, and terminated with no OAuth grant at all.  Where
   this document derives a Mission through OAuth, it relates the Mission
   to OAuth in two distinct ways: the *Mission grant binding*, defined
   below, and the mission claim a derived token carries as its own
   Mission reference (Section 7.8).

   The Mission grant binding is an AS-controlled, functional mapping
   from one persistent, redeemable *grant lineage* to exactly one
   Mission.  The AS alone establishes a binding and resolves it from the
   grant itself; a client never supplies or negotiates one, and no
   client input determines its Mission.

   A grant lineage is state the AS can resolve again on a later request,
   either:

   *  the authorization code issued at approval and, where its
      redemption emits one, the refresh-token family that follows it; or

   *  another profile-defined reusable grant, such as a refresh-token
      family a continuation transport establishes later for the same
      Mission ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-continuation]).

6.5.1.  Establishing and Resolving a Binding

   At a direct approval event the AS binds the Mission to the
   authorization code it issues, or, under a back-channel carriage
   profile, to the grant artifact that profile defines (Section 14).
   The binding is server-side, and it resolves the Mission for
   operations using that binding; a profile-defined grant can instead
   resolve it through its authenticated mission reference.

   An authorization request that carries no mission_intent proposes no
   Mission.  An AS that answers such a request through consent reuse,
   such as with a grant an authentication session holds for the client,
   MUST NOT select a grant bound to a Mission: the request would
   otherwise acquire an earlier Mission's binding with no submission or
   approval of its own.  This rule governs grant selection for a new
   authorization request.  It neither prohibits ordinary consent nor
   changes how a derivation resolves an existing binding.

   The code itself carries no refresh-token family; only a successful
   redemption produces one.  A refresh-token family so produced inherits
   the code's binding atomically with its issuance, extending the same
   Mission grant binding rather than starting a second one.

   At each subsequent derivation the AS resolves the Mission from the
   grant the client presents:

   *  the authorization code at the token endpoint;

   *  the refresh token on refresh;

   *  the Mission-bound subject_token on Token Exchange (a delegate is
      identified by its client authentication, Section 8.2); or

   *  a profile-defined grant, through the authenticated mission
      reference it carries, such as the Template profile's handoff grant
      ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-template]).

   It then applies the gating of Section 7.1.

   A Mission MAY carry zero or more grant bindings.  Beyond any
   authorization-code lineage established at approval, a refresh-token
   family a continuation transport establishes later (above) is a
   further binding, or the Mission's first where it has none, rooted in
   the Mission its own subject_token resolved to.

   The AS MUST resolve a bound grant lineage to the same Mission on
   every derivation performed against it: the mapping is fixed for the
   lineage's lifetime and is never reassigned to a different Mission.
   An AS that finds a lineage resolving to more than one Mission fails
   closed on that lineage.

   Where a profile-defined operation lets a client present a Mission
   Identifier alongside a credential, the identifier is a non-
   authoritative cross-check: the AS MUST verify it against the Mission
   the credential resolves to and refuse a mismatch with the
   invalid_grant error code.  The predecessor parameter of
   [I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-expansion] is an example: it
   selects the Mission a successor is created from and does not reassign
   an existing binding.

   Because the grant, not the identifier, determines the Mission, a
   client does not supply mission_id to obtain a derivation, and an AS
   MUST NOT derive Mission-bound authority from a client-supplied
   mission_id.

6.5.2.  Grant Bindings and Related Mechanisms

   The grant binding relates to other mechanisms as follows:

   *  *Token Exchange.* A Token Exchange derivation ([RFC8693]) that
      returns only an access token establishes no new grant lineage:
      ordinary in-Mission delegation (Section 8) and self-exchange down-
      scoping (Section 7.5) both work this way, and the issued token is
      a derived token (Section 7.8) whose mission claim identifies the
      one Mission its subject_token resolved to.  Where the
      subject_token was issued under a grant lineage, the issued token
      retains that grant provenance, and a later redemption of a
      designation derived from it validates all applicable backing-grant
      state (Section 8.2).  A Token Exchange that instead establishes a
      redeemable grant lineage, such as the continuation profile's
      delegation-family-creating exchange
      ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-continuation]), creates a new
      grant binding, rooted in that same Mission.  Three Token Exchanges
      are covered by the two items below instead: cross-domain
      projection and child creation, which return a JWT authorization
      grant, and expansion, which creates a successor.  Redeeming the
      Template profile's handoff grant
      ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-template]) issues a derived
      token and creates or extends no grant binding.  In the
      continuation profile's Identity Continuation Transport
      ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-continuation]), redeeming a
      root or onward Identity Assertion JWT Authorization Grant (ID-JAG)
      at a Resource AS produces a local derived access token.  A
      Resource AS holds a destination-side grant binding only where it
      separately establishes a redeemable grant lineage mapped to the
      Mission, such as a refresh-token family.

   *  *Cross-domain projection.* A cross-domain projection
      ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-cross-domain]) establishes no
      destination-side grant binding.  A Resource AS consumes the cross-
      domain grant, and the local access token it mints there is a
      derived token: it identifies the one originating Mission
      (mission.id, mission.issuer) but is not backed by a persistent
      local lineage.

   *  *Child Missions and successors.* A Child Mission
      ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-child-delegation]) and an
      expansion successor
      ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-expansion]) are new Missions.
      Any grant binding or derived token they carry is their own under
      the same rules, not an extension of the origin Mission's binding.
      They relate to their origin only by lineage (the child's parent
      member, the successor's predecessor member).

   A decision-time runtime join, such as the Mission Join of
   [I-D.draft-mcguinness-mission-authority-server], in which a policy
   decision point joins an ordinary OAuth credential to a Mission per
   request, is not a Mission grant binding: it does not make the joined
   OAuth grant Mission-bound, and it rests on its own authenticated
   inputs and evidence, never on a Mission grant binding.

6.5.3.  Mission State Across Bindings

   Non-active state gates every future derivation across every Mission
   grant binding (Section 7.1).  It does not itself revoke an OAuth
   grant the same client or subject holds outside any Mission binding,
   and it does not recall an access token already issued before its exp
   absent a runtime state check the token's consumer performs
   (Section 10.2, Section 9.2).

   An issuer that needs to cascade a lifecycle operation, or to
   enumerate a Mission's bound grant lineages and credentials, maintains
   its own issuer-side index from the Mission to each binding; a Mission
   Management companion
   ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-management]) can standardize
   that index's wire surface.

   When the authorization code expires unredeemed, no derivation is
   possible under the Mission regardless of which lifecycle outcome
   follows.  The deployment either revokes the Mission or lets it expire
   at expires_at, and applies one choice consistently; both outcomes
   terminate it (Section 10), so reprocessing the same timeout changes
   nothing.

7.  Mission-Bound Token Issuance

   An access token issued under a Mission takes one of two forms:

   *  a self-contained JWT per [RFC9068], which a resource server can
      enforce from the token alone (Section 9.1) and which this
      document's token-carried enforcement assumes; or

   *  an opaque Mission-bound token, profiled only under the
      introspected consumption mode (Section 9.2.4), where introspection
      or issuer-local resolution carries its claims, with the same
      enforcement obligations.

   Section 7.1 states the issuance procedure for every path.  The
   sections that follow state the token's claims (Section 7.2), its
   audience and the token response (Section 7.3), refresh (Section 7.4),
   self-exchange (Section 7.5), authentication claims and lifetime
   (Section 7.6), scope projection (Section 7.7), and the mission claim
   (Section 7.8).

7.1.  Issuance Gating

   A derivation is one issuance operation the issuer AS performs for a
   single request: the initial authorization-code exchange, a refresh, a
   Token Exchange, a cross-domain grant issuance
   ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-cross-domain]), or the
   redemption of a profile-defined grant, such as the Template profile's
   handoff grant ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-template]).  A
   companion profile bounds the number of derivations under a Mission
   ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-derivation-limits]).

   This document defines five issuance paths:

   *  code redemption, the token response that redeems a newly created
      Mission's authorization code (Section 6.1.1);

   *  a refresh (Section 7.4);

   *  a designation (Section 8.1);

   *  a delegated exchange (Section 8.2); and

   *  a self-exchange (Section 7.5).

   Designation and the delegated exchange belong to the optional
   Delegation capability, and self-exchange is optional too
   (Section 15.2).

   Each of these paths passes every check below.  Each check is stated
   in the sections it cites, with any difference by path, and a path's
   own section states its further rules.  Any other derivation, such as
   one a companion profile defines, passes at least checks 1, 3, 5, and
   7, the exp bound of check 8, and check 10.

   The numbering is presentation order only: it imposes no execution
   order and no error precedence (Section 11 gives the error codes).  An
   order that a rule states still applies, including these:

   *  The Mission-governed refusal applies only to an otherwise
      processable request.  On a refresh, it is decided before the
      refresh token is consumed or rotated (Section 4.3.3).

   *  A refresh establishes any scope projection before the refresh
      token is consumed or rotated, or within the same atomic issuance
      (Section 7.7.6).

   *  A delegated exchange authenticates the client before it refuses an
      actor_token (Section 8.2).

   *  The revocation refusal applies at the AS's final decision to
      release the result (check 10).

   *  A companion profile that sequences a check within a path keeps
      that sequence.

   The checks are:

   1.   Mission resolution from the presented grant (Section 6.5.1): the
        code, the refresh token, or the subject_token, which on a
        delegated exchange is the designation.

   2.   Client and sender binding, as the path's section states for the
        code (Section 6.1.1), the refresh token (Section 7.4), the token
        a designation request presents (Section 8.1), the delegate
        (Section 8.2), and the self-exchanging agent (Section 7.5).

   3.   Mission state: the Mission is active (Section 7.1.1,
        Section 10).  A delegated exchange evaluates each redemption
        afresh (Section 8.2).

   4.   Mission-governed resources (Section 4.3.3).  A refresh, a self-
        exchange, or a designation request needs no mission_intent.

   5.   Subset (Section 5.4): against the Mission Authority Set, and
        also against the presented refresh grant on a refresh, the
        presented token on a designation or a self-exchange, and the
        designation on a delegated exchange; on a designation or a
        delegated exchange, only as Section 8.4 permits.  An entry is
        narrowed, issued to another audience, delegated, or projected
        only under a declared capability (Section 5.5.1).

   6.   Audience (Section 7.2, Section 7.3, Section 5.5.1): by each
        entry's original approved audience.  For a designation, the
        check uses the resource audiences of its entries, not the client
        its aud names.  For a delegated token, the audience is also one
        that processes the act chain (Section 9.1.2).

   7.   Scope projection (Section 7.7), wherever scope is emitted,
        requested, or the only form the target consumes.  On a refresh,
        it applies with that section's protection of the refresh token.

   8.   Claims and lifetime (Section 7.2, Section 7.8, Section 7.8.1,
        Section 7.6).  Each token's exp does not exceed the Mission's
        expires_at.  A designation, a delegated exchange, and a self-
        exchange keep the presented token's mission claim, with the
        differences Section 8.1 and Section 8.2 state.

   9.   Response members: the response echoes the granted
        authorization_details (Section 7.3) and carries mission_id and
        mission_expires_at as Section 7.3.1 states.

   10.  Release: the revocation refusal (Section 7.1.1) applies at the
        AS's final decision to release the result.

7.1.1.  Refusing a Derivation

   Unless the referenced Mission is active, the AS MUST refuse a request
   to derive a token at the token endpoint: a Token Exchange ([RFC8693])
   with the invalid_request error code (Section 2.2.2 of [RFC8693]), and
   any other grant, refresh included, with the invalid_grant error code.
   The AS MUST refuse a derivation request it answers after it has
   acknowledged a revocation of the Mission, with the same error code.

   A Token Exchange takes invalid_request because a presented token
   whose Mission is not active is unacceptable for the exchange, a case
   Section 2.2.2 of [RFC8693] assigns to that code.

   The AS answers a request when it makes its final decision to release
   the result, not when network delivery of the response completes.  A
   result prepared before the acknowledgment and released after it is
   therefore refused.

   The AS acknowledges a revocation when it commits the Mission's
   termination with reason revoked (Section 10.1), whether a revocation
   request or the deployment's policy caused it (Section 10.2).
   Receiving or queuing a revocation request does not acknowledge it,
   and acknowledgment does not wait for an HTTP response to complete or
   an event to be delivered.  The refusal at release re-checks only an
   acknowledged revocation; it is not a general re-check of Mission
   state.

   Where a profile of a Token Exchange that the AS implements assigns
   its own error code to either refusal, such as a continuation
   profile's code for an ended chain, the AS uses that profile's code
   instead; the refusal itself, and the mission_error member
   (Section 7.1.2), still apply.

7.1.2.  Mission Error Member

   The error code alone does not tell a client which gate refused.  On a
   refusal under Section 7.1, the AS SHOULD include, alongside error,
   the mission_error token-error-response member (Section 19.1) with one
   of the values revoked, expired, or superseded (where a companion
   defines supersession), the Mission's termination reason
   (Section 10.1).

   These values are termination reasons.  A companion profile that
   defines either of the following defines any mission_error value for
   it:

   *  another Mission state (Section 10), which Section 7.1 also
      refuses, as Mission Status defines a mission_error value for a
      suspended Mission ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-status],
      Section "Mission Lifecycle Endpoint"); or

   *  a further refusal at issuance, as Derivation Limits defines a
      mission_error value for an exhausted limit
      ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-derivation-limits],
      Section "Enforcement at Issuance").

   A Mission in a state from which it can return to active MUST NOT be
   reported with a termination reason.

   The mission_error member is diagnostic only and optional, whichever
   document defines its value:

   *  it grants nothing;

   *  an unrecognized value is ignored and is not authoritative about
      the Mission's state; and

   *  it is returned only to the authenticated client presenting the
      Mission's grant.

   Each mission_error value this section names reports a terminated
   Mission (Section 10.1): no retry under the same grant cures the
   refusal.  Further work needs a new approval or, for superseded, the
   successor Mission ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-expansion]).
   A new approval is a fresh approval event, not an automatic
   consequence of the refusal.  A credential held outside the Mission is
   no fallback for its task (Section 16.1.2).  The refusal gives no
   assurance that the refresh token remains usable.  This reading is
   keyed on the value, not on the error code, because the same refusal
   covers a Mission in a non-terminal state that a companion profile
   registers (Section 19.7), whose meaning that profile defines.

7.1.3.  Derived Token Lifetime

   Derived tokens SHOULD be short-lived so that a transition to
   terminated takes effect promptly without per-request revocation
   checks.  A resource server that does not check Mission state per
   request can accept a derived token until its exp.  At such a resource
   server, the token's lifetime is therefore the termination latency
   (Section 16.3.1).

   Short-lived means within the termination latency the deployment
   accepts.  This document sets no numerical bound; a deployment profile
   can.  The cap at the Mission's expires_at (Section 7.6) does not by
   itself make a token short-lived.

7.2.  Token Claims

   [RFC9068] fixes the JWT's required claims (including jti), the typ
   header parameter value at+jwt, and resource server validation
   (Section 2.1 of [RFC9068], Section 2.2 of [RFC9068], Section 4 of
   [RFC9068]).  A token whose format a companion profile explicitly
   defines, with its own typ and verification rules, follows that
   profile instead, as the Mission Offline Attenuation root does
   ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-attenuation]).  JWT access-token
   validation remains governed by Section 4 of [RFC9068], including
   acceptance of at+jwt or application/at+jwt.  A companion-format token
   is consumed only under that companion's verification rules.  In
   addition to what [RFC9068] requires, a derived token:

   *  carries the token's Mission-derived authority as
      authorization_details ([RFC9396]); this MAY be the full Authority
      Set or a narrowed subset (Section 5.4);

   *  carries a mission claim (Section 7.8);

   *  sets sub to the AS-local sub the AS maps the Mission's Subject to
      (Section 6.1);

   *  carries client_id with its ordinary meaning (Section 4.3 of
      [RFC8693], Section 2.2 of [RFC9068]): the client that requested
      this particular token;

   *  MUST set aud to identify the resource server(s) authorized to
      consume the carried authorization_details, and MUST NOT include an
      audience unrelated to that carried authority (Section 7.3,
      Paragraph 1), except that a token the AS issues only for
      presentation back to itself at a Token Exchange, such as a
      designation (Section 8.1), names in aud the client that presents
      it;

   *  MAY carry an act claim when the agent has delegated execution
      (Section 8.2);

   *  MAY carry a scope claim, subject to Section 7.7; and

   *  can be sender-constrained, as Section 2.2.1 of [RFC9700]
      recommends, via a cnf claim [RFC7800]: DPoP [RFC9449] (cnf.jkt) or
      mTLS [RFC8705] (cnf.x5t#S256).

   The primary access token may be a bearer token, preserving
   compatibility with bearer-only resource servers while retaining
   Section 2.2.1 of [RFC9700]'s recommendation; the cost is the stolen-
   token exposure (Section 16.4.1).

   A delegated token is sender-constrained to the delegate's own key
   (Section 8.2); the cross-domain companion requires sender-constraint
   for credentials that cross a trust domain
   ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-cross-domain]).

   An AS that issues Mission-bound JWT access tokens for resource
   servers to consume MUST publish its token verification keys (for
   example, at its [RFC8414] jwks_uri, as Section 4 of [RFC9068]
   recommends); rotation retires a key from signing, never from
   resolvability while tokens signed under it remain valid.  A token the
   AS issues only for presentation back to itself, such as a designation
   (Section 8.1), creates no publication duty by itself.

7.3.  Audience and Token Response

   The AS audience-restricts the token per Section 2 of [RFC8707] and
   Section 3 of [RFC9068]; aud names resource server(s), APIs, or
   security domains, not necessarily the entries' resource values.  The
   client obtains a single-audience token (Section 2.3 of [RFC9700])
   with the [RFC8707] resource parameter, and can narrow it further with
   scope; the AS narrows the Authority Set under Section 5.4 to the
   requested resource(s) and sets aud accordingly.

   A token-endpoint resource value outside the Authority Set is an
   invalid resource value in the sense of [RFC8707].

   Three identifiers stay distinct.  An entry's resource, where its type
   defines one, names the protected resource its authority covers, and a
   resource server matches each request's operation target against it
   under the entry's type (for mission_resource_access,
   [I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-resource-access]).  A token
   request's resource parameter ([RFC8707]) selects the audience the
   client asks for.  The token's aud names the recipients that accept
   the token, and the AS relates an entry to them through the entry's
   original approved audience (Section 5.5.1).

   A token issued for an audience carries only the entries whose
   original approved audience includes that audience; an entry approved
   for another audience is not carried, unless a declared narrowing
   capability permits issuing it there.  A token whose aud names several
   audiences is issued only where the same entries are approved for each
   of them; where they differ, the AS refuses the request with
   invalid_target, and the client requests a separate token for each
   audience.  The AS also refuses with invalid_target a request whose
   audience is approved for none of the entries the token would carry,
   and one whose authorization_details (Section 6 of [RFC9396]) names an
   entry not approved for its audience.

   Section 8.1 states how these rules apply to a designation and to the
   delegated token issued at its redemption.

   The AS returns the granted authorization_details in every token
   response, including refresh and Token Exchange responses (Section 7
   of [RFC9396]).  Strengthening Section 7 of [RFC9396], the AS MUST NOT
   omit values from it: the response states exactly the (possibly
   narrowed) set assigned to the issued token, and it is the
   authoritative statement of what was granted, which the client
   compares with its proposal (Section 4.3).  A client MUST NOT assume
   authority absent from the granted set.  An entry the echo narrows
   grants the narrower authority it states, and a client need not reject
   a grant narrower than its proposal.  The mission_id and
   mission_expires_at response parameters carry the Mission reference
   and expiry beside it (Section 7.3.1).

   The following is an example of a refresh request that narrows the
   canonical ERP Mission (the worked example of Section 6.3) to a read-
   only token with the [RFC8707] resource parameter and the
   authorization_details parameter (Section 6 of [RFC9396]).  The ERP
   consumes authorization_details, so a resource scope for it would be
   refused (Section 7.7).  Extra line breaks are for display purposes
   only:

   POST /token HTTP/1.1
   Host: as.example.com
   Content-Type: application/x-www-form-urlencoded
   DPoP: eyJ0eXAiOiJkcG9wK2p3dCIsImFsZyI6IkVTMjU2Iiwi...

   grant_type=refresh_token
   &refresh_token=rt_4mN8qV2xP7sL1tY9zB3k
   &resource=https%3A%2F%2Ferp.example.com
   &authorization_details=%5B%7B%22type%22%3A%22mission_resource_acc...

   The authorization_details value, before form encoding, requests the
   Mission's read entry alone:

   [
     { "type": "mission_resource_access",
       "resource": "https://erp.example.com",
       "actions": ["invoices.read"],
       "constraints": {
         "resource_issued_after": "2026-07-01T00:00:00Z",
         "resource_issued_before": "2026-09-30T23:59:59Z"
       },
       "delegation": {
         "max_depth": 2,
         "allowed_delegates": [{ "sub_profile": "ai_agent" }]
       } }
   ]

   The issuance is a derivation, gated on the Mission being active
   (Section 7.1).  The following example shows the token response.  It
   carries no scope member because none was requested or granted, and it
   echoes the narrowed grant and the mission_id reference
   (Section 7.3.1).  The emitted entry is a subset (Section 5.4) of the
   Mission's read entry, its constraints carried intact:

   {
     "access_token": "eyJhbGciOiJFUzI1NiIsInR5cCI6ImF0K2p3dCJ9...",
     "token_type": "DPoP",
     "expires_in": 300,
     "mission_id": "msn_8RfX2Lqv9TqMv4z7sA2bN1k0YpEdHc9-",
     "mission_expires_at": "2026-12-31T23:59:59Z",
     "authorization_details": [
       { "type": "mission_resource_access",
         "resource": "https://erp.example.com",
         "actions": ["invoices.read"],
         "constraints": {
           "resource_issued_after": "2026-07-01T00:00:00Z",
           "resource_issued_before": "2026-09-30T23:59:59Z"
         },
         "delegation": {
           "max_depth": 2,
           "allowed_delegates": [{ "sub_profile": "ai_agent" }]
         } }
     ]
   }

7.3.1.  Mission Identifier and Expiry in Responses

   This document defines mission_id as a token-endpoint response
   parameter: a string carrying the Mission Identifier, returned
   alongside the issued token.  It is an informational reference only:
   presenting it authorizes nothing (Section 6.5.1), and a client MUST
   NOT derive authority from it.

   Alongside it, this document defines mission_expires_at: the exact RFC
   3339 string recorded as the Mission Record's effective expires_at
   (Section 6.3), the common member of every Mission-creating success
   response, whatever surface completes the creation. expires_in
   describes the access token's lifetime, not the Mission's, and the
   effective expiry can be shorter than the requested intent.expires_at.

   A client learns both values from these members, not from the access
   token, which it treats as opaque (Section 6 of [RFC9068]).  The AS
   returns them as follows:

   *  The success response that first delivers a newly created Mission's
      identifier or credential MUST carry both.  On the authorization-
      code path, the authorization response carrying only the code is
      not that response; the token response redeeming the code is.

   *  A delegated exchange response carries both as Section 8.2
      requires.

   *  Any other Mission-bound token response, including a refresh,
      designation (Section 8.1), or self-exchange (Section 7.5)
      response, SHOULD carry both.

   *  A creation replay deduplicated under the applicable operation
      identifier (approval_event_id for direct approval (Section 6.3),
      or the identifier a Mission-creating profile defines) returns the
      committed effective value unchanged.

   On OAuth token responses mission_expires_at is additionally
   registered as a token-endpoint response parameter (Section 19.1).  It
   is informational in the same way: presenting it authorizes nothing.

7.4.  Refresh Tokens

   Issuance on a refresh applies the issuance procedure of Section 7.1
   as it states for a refresh.  Section 4.3 states a refresh's exemption
   from mission_intent and, for a Mission-governed resource, the
   refusal's precedence and its timing relative to consuming or rotating
   the refresh token.

   A Mission-bound refresh token is bound to the client it was issued
   to.  For a confidential client, client authentication supplies the
   binding: on refresh, the AS authenticates the client and verifies
   that the refresh token was issued to it (Section 6 of [RFC6749]), so
   the token need not also be bound to the DPoP proof key (Section 5 of
   [RFC9449]).  A public client's refresh token MUST be sender-
   constrained or use refresh token rotation (Section 2.2.2 of
   [RFC9700]).

   The AS can rotate either client type's refresh token; rotation
   detects replay of a consumed refresh token (Section 4.14.2 of
   [RFC9700]) and is not a cryptographic binding.

   A refresh request can carry authorization_details to narrow the
   issued token (Section 6 of [RFC9396]).  The AS MUST refuse such a
   request with the invalid_authorization_details error code when a
   requested entry exceeds the presented refresh grant or the Mission
   Authority Set (Section 5.4), and MUST NOT instead issue a narrower
   set than the one requested.

7.5.  Self-Exchange Down-Scoping

   An agent MAY present its own Mission-bound access token as the
   subject_token of a Token Exchange ([RFC8693]) with no actor, to
   obtain a narrowed token (for example, a single-audience one).
   Because it names no actor, such an exchange does not delegate: it re-
   scopes the agent's own authority downward.  A designation
   (Section 8.1) names a delegate, so it is not a self-exchange, and
   these rules do not apply to it.  An exchange whose subject_token is a
   designation is a delegated exchange (Section 8.2), not a no-actor
   exchange, even when it carries no actor_token.  Issuance applies the
   issuance procedure of Section 7.1 as it states for a self-exchange.
   Section 4.3 states a self-exchange request's exemption from
   mission_intent and the checks that still apply to it.  A no-actor
   exchange is subject to the following:

   1.  The AS MUST refuse a no-actor exchange with the invalid_request
       error code (Section 2.2.2 of [RFC8693]) unless the authenticated
       client is the Mission's approved agent (the Mission Record's
       client_id, Section 6.3).  A delegate narrows only through a
       delegated exchange that names it in the act chain.

   2.  The result MUST be a subset (Section 5.4) of the presented
       token's authority; it carries the same mission claim
       (Section 7.8) and adds no act chain.

   Self-exchange is an optional capability (Section 15.2).  An AS that
   does not support it refuses a no-actor exchange: with
   unsupported_grant_type (Section 5.2 of [RFC6749]) if it does not
   support the Token Exchange grant type, and otherwise with
   invalid_request, as for a presented token it does not accept for the
   exchange (Section 2.2.2 of [RFC8693]).  Any other error that applies
   still applies, such as invalid_client for a client that fails
   authentication.

7.6.  Authentication Claims and Lifetime

   A derived token's authority comes from the Mission, not from a fresh
   authentication.  Authentication claims (acr, amr, auth_time) on a
   derived access token follow Section 2.2.1 of [RFC9068]: they describe
   the token's Subject and keep the values of the authentication event
   behind the grant, so derivation does not refresh them.  An AS MUST
   NOT use the authentication of an Approver who is not the token's
   Subject as authentication information about that Subject.

   These claims neither establish approval nor expand Mission authority;
   the Approver's approval-time authentication remains provenance
   (Section 6.1.8).  Where no authentication of the Subject applies, as
   when a human approves a Mission for another principal or for a
   workload, the claims are absent, and their absence is not an
   authentication downgrade.

   The authorization_details claim is the authoritative expression of a
   Mission-bound token's authority; a scope claim (Section 7.7) is a
   compatibility projection that cannot carry per-entry constraints.

   A credential the Mission Issuer derives MUST have an exp that does
   not exceed the Mission's expires_at, so that no credential outlives
   the approved Mission.  The cross-domain companion
   ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-cross-domain]) specifies how
   this bound extends transitively to tokens minted in another trust
   domain.

   Within that bound, a derived token's lifetime is the AS's policy,
   which Section 7.1 recommends keeping short.  Token Exchange does not
   tightly couple an issued token's lifetime to its subject_token's
   (Section 2.1 of [RFC8693]); Section 8.1 states how this applies to a
   designation and to the delegated token issued from it.

7.7.  Scope Projection

   This section applies to every issuance that emits scope on a Mission-
   bound token or whose request names a scope value, and to issuance for
   a target that consumes only scope: such a target receives a safe
   projection or a refusal (projection step 5 below).

7.7.1.  Subset Condition

   For every target audience, the AS MUST establish that the effective
   rights the target's enforcement path grants from the projected scope,
   together with every independently mandatory control on that path, are
   a subset of the rights the token's applicable authorization_details
   grant.

   This is a semantic condition, not a structural one.  Exactly as it
   fails for a relaxed constraint, it fails for a constraints-free entry
   whose scope:

   *  aggregates a broader action;

   *  spans more resource instances or paths than the entry;

   *  carries rights the target's local scope interpretation implies; or

   *  arises from the union of several entries.

7.7.2.  Per-Audience Condition

   For a multi-audience token, the AS establishes the condition
   independently for each audience; a single-audience token
   (Section 7.3) remains preferred.

7.7.3.  Projection Steps

   To emit scope for an entry, the AS:

   1.  determines the target resource server or audience for the token;

   2.  resolves a trusted, versioned scope-projection mapping for that
       target, established through the target's protected resource
       metadata ([RFC9728]) or authenticated out-of-band configuration;

   3.  establishes, under the subset condition above, that the complete
       effective authorization the projected scope grants at that target
       is no broader than the applicable carried entries;

   4.  omits scope for an entry where the target's enforcement path
       consumes authorization_details instead; and

   5.  MUST refuse issuance to a target that is scope-only when no safe
       projection exists for the applicable entries: an issued token no
       enforcement path can safely evaluate is not a usable credential.

   Unknown scope semantics, unknown resource server enforcement
   behavior, or an ambiguous or stale mapping all fail closed under step
   5 (Section 11).  A changed mapping is not by itself stale: the AS
   evaluates each issuance, refresh included, against the target's
   current trusted mapping, and a mapping is stale only when the AS
   cannot establish that it is the current trusted mapping for that
   target.  This rule applies to every issuance path that can emit scope
   on a Mission-bound token: initial issuance, refresh, Token Exchange,
   and any other derived-token path.

   This is the type-agnostic form of the rule; a type's own
   specification states when the mapping in step 3 is safe for that
   type's entries (for mission_resource_access,
   [I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-resource-access]).

   The runtime profile's enforcement-scope declarations
   ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-mission-runtime]) can reference the same
   mapping for the paths it covers; this rule does not depend on that
   profile.

7.7.4.  Requested Scope Values

   Scope projection does not change OAuth response semantics and does
   not make access-token inspection a client requirement.

   Section 3.3 of [RFC6749] requires the token response to carry scope
   whenever the granted scope differs from the requested scope, and its
   grammar defines no empty scope, so an issuance cannot report an
   ungranted requested value by omission.  The AS MUST refuse, with
   invalid_scope (Section 11), a request that explicitly names a scope
   value the issuance cannot grant: a value the target's trusted mapping
   names but no carried entry makes safe (step 3), or any scope value
   the AS associates with a target that consumes authorization_details
   (step 4).

   An unknown, ambiguous, or stale mapping fails projection step 5 and
   yields the invalid_target error code even when the request also names
   a scope value; invalid_scope applies only under a mapping the AS
   trusts.

   Where the AS emits a projected scope, a requested scope narrows it to
   the requested values, and the token response reports the values
   granted (Section 5.1 of [RFC6749]).  A Mission-creating client does
   not request a resource scope for a target that consumes
   authorization_details; for a scope-only target, a requested scope
   selects among the values the projection can grant.  Scope values with
   their own semantics, such as openid ([OpenID.Core]), are unaffected
   and combine with authorization_details as Section 3.1 of [RFC9396]
   permits.

7.7.5.  Scope-Only Targets

   For a scope-only target, the trusted mapping also relates a requested
   scope value back to authority, so a deployment's existing scope
   values can request a Mission in configured-mapping mode
   (Section 5.1), combining scope with the Mission's
   authorization_details as Section 3.1 of [RFC9396] leaves to the AS.

   *  Each requested value stands for the entries that the type's
      specification derives from the mapping (for
      mission_resource_access,
      [I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-resource-access]).

   *  The Authority Set is those entries narrowed to policy, and the
      token for that target carries their projection under the steps
      above.

   *  A requested value the target's trusted mapping does not name
      cannot be granted, and is refused with invalid_scope (Section 11).

7.7.6.  Refresh Token Protection

   A refusal caused solely by failure to establish a safe scope
   projection MUST NOT invalidate an otherwise-valid refresh token or
   its authorization grant.  Independent expiration, revocation, and
   replay-detection rules continue to apply.  An AS meets this by
