   establishing the projection before it consumes or rotates the refresh
   token, or within the same atomic issuance, not by disabling rotation
   or restoring a consumed token.

7.8.  Mission Claim

   The mission claim is a JSON object:

   id:  REQUIRED.  A string.  The Mission Identifier (Section 6.3.5).

   issuer:  REQUIRED.  A string.  The Mission's issuer (Section 6.3).  A
      credential's iss names the party that minted it; mission.issuer
      names the party that approved and serves the Mission, and the two
      differ for tokens minted in another trust domain
      ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-cross-domain]).

   The id and issuer members identify the Mission and carry no authority
   of their own; the token's own signature authenticates the pair, and
   the carried authorization_details remains the token's concrete
   authority.

   expires_at:  OPTIONAL.  A string.  The Mission's expires_at
      (Section 6.3), in RFC 3339 [RFC3339] date-time form.  It commits a
      lifetime bound, not current state: a validator can check that the
      token's exp does not exceed it, but that check does not establish
      that the Mission is still active, the only state that permits
      reliance (Section 10).

   A consumer that relies only on the presented token's own validity
   needs nothing further: the token's exp already bounds it.  A profile
   that mints a further credential downstream of this one, or that
   verifies a Mission's remaining lifetime from retained state rather
   than a live token, MUST require expires_at and MUST treat its absence
   as an error.  The token's own exp bounds only that one credential,
   not every credential the Mission may still yield.

   The baseline mission claim carries neither authority_hash nor
   approval_basis.  Neither is an enforcement input a narrowed-token
   resource server can exercise: authority_hash commits the complete
   Authority Set, which such a resource server does not hold
   (Section 9.1); approval_basis.type is provenance, not authority
   (Section 6.3).  An authorized introspection caller can receive
   authority_hash and approval_basis.type (Section 9.2.1).

   The mission claim is an open object (Section 14): additional members
   MAY appear alongside the members above.  This document defines no
   registry of mission members.  A companion profile of this document
   MAY use short member names coordinated with it; any other extension
   member MUST use a collision-resistant name (for example, a name in a
   namespace the extension controls, per the Collision-Resistant Name
   guidance of Section 4.2 of [RFC7519]) and is defined by the profile
   that introduces it.

   A consumer MUST ignore members it does not understand and MUST NOT
   use any additional member to grant or widen authority; the members
   above remain authoritative.

   The following is an example of a decoded Mission-bound token payload:

   {
     "iss": "https://as.example.com",
     "sub": "user_3p2q8mN1a0kV7tR",
     "aud": "https://erp.example.com",
     "client_id": "s6BhdRkqt3",
     "iat": 1793606405,
     "exp": 1793606705,
     "jti": "at_9Kp2vN7sR1tY8mZ3qX5b",
     "authorization_details": [
       { "type": "mission_resource_access",
         "resource": "https://erp.example.com",
         "actions": ["invoices.read"],
         "constraints": {
           "resource_issued_after": "2026-07-01T00:00:00Z",
           "resource_issued_before": "2026-09-30T23:59:59Z"
         },
         "delegation": {
           "max_depth": 2,
           "allowed_delegates": [{ "sub_profile": "ai_agent" }]
         } },
       { "type": "mission_resource_access",
         "resource": "https://erp.example.com",
         "actions": ["journal-entries.write"],
         "constraints": {
           "max_amount": { "amount": "500.00", "currency": "USD" }
         } }
     ],
     "cnf": { "jkt": "0ZcOCORZNYy-DWpqq30jZyJGHTN0d2HglBV3uiguA4I" },
     "mission": {
       "id": "msn_8RfX2Lqv9TqMv4z7sA2bN1k0YpEdHc9-",
       "issuer": "https://as.example.com"
     }
   }

7.8.1.  Claim Reservation

   The mission claim of an access token, and the mission member that
   introspection returns for an access token (Section 9.2), are reserved
   for issuance that conforms to a Mission-binding profile.  An AS MUST
   NOT issue an access token carrying the mission claim unless the
   token's issuance or derivation conforms to one: this document's
   issuance gating (Section 7.1) and subset rule (Section 5.4) for a
   token the Mission's issuer derives, or a companion profile that
   defines another issuance or derivation path, including a token that a
   Resource AS or a consuming AS issues and a token a holder derives
   under an attenuation profile.

   A profile that defines such a path MUST identify:

   1.  the authenticated grant or parent chain that connects the token's
       authority to the Mission;

   2.  the authority bound the path enforces, and the comparison or
       projection by which it enforces it;

   3.  where Mission state is checked, by which party, and the freshness
       or lifetime bound within which that check is relied on; and

   4.  any check a consumer of the token performs.

   A mission claim means that the token's issuance or derivation
   conformed to its profile.  It does not mean that the party that
   minted the token queried the Mission's current state, and it attests
   issuance, not the Mission's state afterward: a later suspension or
   revocation does not change what the claim attests (Section 10,
   Section 9.2).  The claim establishes no trust by its presence; a
   resource server's issuer authorization and claim validation
   (Section 9.1.1) still apply.

   This reservation concerns access tokens and their introspection
   representation.  An evidence or control artifact that embeds a
   mission descriptor (Section 14.1) is not an access token, and this
   section does not change its meaning.

8.  Delegation Within a Mission

   Delegation is an optional capability (Section 15).  An agent may
   delegate execution to downstream actors (a sub-agent, service, or
   tool that is itself an OAuth client) within a Mission.  Delegation is
   represented with the OAuth Actor Profile
   [I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-actor-profile], which profiles the act
   (actor) claim of Section 4.1 of [RFC8693].

   An intermediary in the agent's own trust domain that presents the
   agent's own tokens, such as a gateway holding the agent's
   credentials, acts as part of the agent and is not a delegate; acting
   as the agent, it can also narrow by self-exchange (Section 7.5),
   which does not delegate.  An intermediary authenticated as a distinct
   actor that obtains a delegated token by Token Exchange is a delegate.

   Delegation takes two kinds of Token Exchange, in this order:

   1.  The delegating party obtains a designation that names the
       delegate (Section 8.1).

   2.  The delegate redeems that designation for a delegated token
       (Section 8.2).

   Delegating party            AS                        Delegate
     | Token Exchange:          |                            |
     | subject_token =          |                            |
     | Mission-bound token,     |                            |
     | audience = delegate ---->|                            |
     |<------ designation ------|                            |
     |---------------------- designation ------------------->|
     |                          |<------ Token Exchange:     |
     |                          |        subject_token =     |
     |                          |        designation         |
     |                          |------- delegated token --->|

8.1.  Designation

   A designation is the delegating party's authorization for one
   delegate to obtain delegated tokens.  The holder of a Mission-bound
   access token obtains it by Token Exchange ([RFC8693]): it presents
   that token as the subject_token, proves possession of the token's
   confirmation key, and names another client's identifier as audience,
   with requested_token_type urn:ietf:params:oauth:token-
   type:access_token.  Only a sender-constrained token can therefore be
   designated.  The holder is the Mission's approved agent for a token
   that carries no act claim, and otherwise the delegate that the AS's
   accounting records as the token's outermost actor (Section 8.4).

   Issuing a designation applies the issuance procedure of Section 7.1
   as it states for a designation.  A designation request is a token
   request under an existing Mission-bound grant (Section 4.3): it needs
   no mission_intent, and every other check that section names still
   applies to it.

   A designation the AS issues:

   *  carries a subset (Section 5.4) of the presented token's authority,
      and the same mission claim (Section 7.8);

   *  has aud equal to the designated client's identifier, and a may_act
      claim (Section 4.4 of [RFC8693]) whose sub is that identifier;

   *  carries no cnf and no act claim: like an authorization code, it is
      bound to the client that redeems it, which authenticates and
      proves its own key in the delegated exchange (Section 8.2), and
      the delegated token's act renders the AS's accounting
      (Section 8.4); and

   *  MUST expire shortly after it is issued, and no later than the
      presented token or the Mission's expires_at.

   A designation's aud names its redeemer and reassigns no entry's
   original approved audience (Section 5.5.1).  For a designation, the
   audience rules of Section 7.3 test the resource audiences associated
   with the entries it carries, never the designated client its aud
   names (Section 7.2).  A designation carries only a permitted subset
   of the presented token's entries (Section 5.4, Section 8.4), each
   keeping its original approved audience (Section 5.5.1).  An entry
   without an original approved audience is not carried, and a
   designation that would carry no entry is refused with invalid_target.
   Its redemption issues an entry only as Section 5.5.1 and Section 8.2
   permit, including their narrowing condition.  The delegated token
   issued at its redemption is subject to every rule of Section 7.3
   unchanged.

   A designation names a delegate and adds none.  Whether one may be
   redeemed more than once within its lifetime is the AS's policy; each
   redemption is a delegated exchange of its own (Section 8.2).  Its
   expiry bounds when it can be redeemed, not the lifetime of a
   delegated token issued from it (Section 7.6).  That delegated token
   can outlive both the designation and the credential the designation
   was derived from.  Revoking the grant state backing that credential
   still refuses every later redemption (Section 8.2).

   A designation authorizes no resource access.  A designated client's
   identifier MUST NOT be an audience that any resource server accepts
   for this AS's access tokens, throughout the designation's lifetime.
   That includes an alias a resource server accepts but the AS never
   issues as an audience.  The AS MUST refuse, with invalid_target
   (Section 2.2.2 of [RFC8693]), a designation request naming a client
   for which it cannot establish that separation.  A resource server
   that validates a JWT access token's audience (Section 4 of [RFC9068])
   therefore rejects a designation, and at introspection (Section 9.2.2)
   the AS MUST return active: false for one, with no mission member.

8.2.  Delegated Exchange

   A delegate obtains a delegated token by Token Exchange ([RFC8693]),
   presenting a designation that the delegating party obtained for it
   (Section 8.1).  Issuance applies the issuance procedure of
   Section 7.1 as it states for a delegated exchange.  The AS issues the
   delegated token subject to all of the following:

   *  *The exchange is explicit.* The subject_token is a designation
      this AS issued naming the authenticated requesting client
      (Section 8.1), with subject_token_type
      urn:ietf:params:oauth:token-type:access_token, and the
      requested_token_type is urn:ietf:params:oauth:token-
      type:access_token.  The AS MUST refuse any other subject_token
      with invalid_request (Section 2.2.2 of [RFC8693]); an access token
      issued for resource access is never sufficient.  The delegate is
      identified by its own client authentication, and the AS asserts
      the actor itself (Section 8.4).

   *  *The delegate authenticates.* The AS MUST authenticate the
      requesting client, whether or not the request carries an
      actor_token, by a method it relies on to identify the client
      (Section 2.3 of [RFC6749]), and MUST refuse a client it does not
      so authenticate with invalid_client (Section 5.2 of [RFC6749]),
      issuing no token.  Once it has so authenticated the client, the AS
      MUST refuse a request that carries an actor_token with
      invalid_request (Section 2.2.2 of [RFC8693]), issuing no token.  A
      claimed client_id, possession of the designation, or proof of an
      independently chosen key, such as a DPoP key, does not
      authenticate a client.  This document does not support a public
      client as a delegate: Section 2.3 of [RFC6749] lets an AS
      establish a client authentication method with a public client but
      not rely on it to identify the client.  Only an extension that
      defines an explicit binding of a designation to its delegate
      admits one; deployment policy alone does not.

   *  *Subject is stable.* sub remains the Mission's Subject.  The
      delegate is an actor, not the Subject.

   *  *client_id keeps its ordinary meaning.* It names the client that
      requested the token (Section 7.2); the originally-approved agent
      stays in the Mission Record (Appendix C.3).

   *  *The act chain identifies the delegates.* The delegated token
      carries an act claim per Section 4.1 of [RFC8693] and the Actor
      Profile [I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-actor-profile]: the outermost
      act is the current delegate, and each earlier delegate is nested
      in the previous actor's act member.  Each actor object carries the
      members that profile defines (for example, sub, iss, and the
      sub_profile actor-type classification, such as ai_agent).

   *  *The audience processes the chain.* The AS issues the delegated
      token only for an audience that meets the issuance rule of
      Section 9.1.2, and refuses any other with invalid_target.

   *  *Authority only narrows.* The delegated token's
      authorization_details MUST be a subset (Section 5.4) of the
      designation's authority, hence of the delegating token's and of
      the Mission Authority Set. A type that declares delegation without
      narrowing (Section 5.5.1) is delegable only unchanged: the AS MUST
      NOT include an entry of such a type in a delegated token unless it
      is equal (Section 5.4) to an entry of the presented delegating
      token, it keeps that entry's approved audience, and the type's
      delegation policy permits the delegate (Section 8.4).  The
      reference is the presented token, not the Mission Authority Set,
      so equality never restores an entry, or any part of one, that the
      presented token omits.

   *  *The mission claim is unchanged.* The delegated token carries the
      delegating token's mission claim (Section 7.8) unchanged, so every
      actor in the chain operates under the one consented authority.

   *  *Each delegate is bound to its own key.* The delegated token MUST
      be sender-constrained (Section 7.2) to the *delegate's own* key:
      its cnf is the delegate's DPoP or mTLS key, not the delegating
      party's, and the delegate proves possession of that key in the
      Token Exchange.  Compromise of a delegate's key therefore does not
      enable use of tokens bound to the agent's or another delegate's
      key.

   *  *Each redemption is gated.* Issuing a delegated token is a
      derivation, refused unless the Mission is active (Section 7.1).
      The AS MUST evaluate every redemption of a designation afresh, as
      a derivation of its own: the credential the designation was
      derived from, and all applicable grant state backing that
      credential, are still valid; the Mission is active; and the
      delegation policy (Section 8.4) and the Mission's current
      authority admit the result.  An earlier redemption establishes
      none of these.  Where that credential was issued under a grant
      lineage, the backing grant state includes that lineage, kept
      through any exchange that established no new grant lineage
      (Section 6.5.2).  A credential issued under none, as on the
      Template profile's Dispatch path before its delegation family
      exists, has no lineage to validate; that never excuses dropping or
      ignoring a lineage that does exist.  The Mission's active state
      never substitutes for backing grant state.

   A delegated exchange response MUST carry mission_id and
   mission_expires_at (Section 7.3.1), because the delegate did not
   receive the response that created the Mission.

   The act chain nests (act.act) for as long as, and only while,
   authority continues under the same approved Mission.  A new approval
   basis, such as a Child Mission
   ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-child-delegation]) or an
   expansion successor ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-expansion]),
   begins its own delegation basis and chain, and no organizational,
   network, or deployment boundary by itself restarts or extends a
   chain.

   The chain is attribution, not authority:

   *  An act entry names who acted, for audit.

   *  The input to the eligibility matching of Section 8.4 is the
      delegate the AS authenticates and asserts at the exchange, not an
      entry read from the chain.

   *  An asserted actor identity grants nothing.

   *  The authorization_details subset relations (Section 5.4), not the
      chain, show that authority narrowed.

8.3.  Instance Context in Delegated Tokens

   Where a deployment authenticates client instances
   ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-client-instance-id], with attesters a
   client endorses under [I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-client-attesters]),
   the delegate named by the outermost act can be an actor that an
   authenticated instance represents.  Instance evidence does not
   establish that actor (Section 5 of
   [I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-client-instance-id]), so the AS
   establishes the delegate's actor identity, and that actor's trusted
   association with the instance, separately.

   Because a delegated token is sender-constrained to the delegate's own
   key (Section 8.2), and the Actor Profile makes the top-level cnf the
   current presenter's key ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-actor-profile]),
   that cnf is a key the instance possesses.

   A deployment MAY convey client_instance in delegated tokens under
   [I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-client-instance-id].  This composition
   selects the authenticated presenting instance for output context.
   The instance specification owns attestation validation, instance-to-
   key association, audience-scoped mapping, and Context Consumer
   validation; this section supplies the Mission-specific authorization
   and context selection.  Deployments configure its use and whether
   instance attribution is required.

   *  When issuing context under this composition, the AS MUST use the
      presenting instance validated under Section 5 of
      [I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-client-instance-id], establish its
      trusted association with the separately authenticated delegate,
      and bind the output token to an instance-unique key whose
      possession it verified in that exchange.  Context is mapped from
      that validated instance into the output audience's Consumer Scope
      under Section 7.1 of
      [I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-client-instance-id].  The AS MUST NOT
      use context copied or remapped from an input token as a substitute
      for the presenting instance's evidence.  For example, when
      instance B is authorized to continue work from instance A, output
      context names B; A's context does not become B's identity by
      remapping it.

   *  The actor authentication, delegation eligibility, subset, and
      lifecycle checks of Section 8.2 and Section 8.4 still authorize
      the exchange; instance evidence satisfies none of them by itself.

   *  Where deployment policy requires instance attribution, the AS MUST
      refuse the exchange if the required instance evidence or its
      association with the delegate and output key cannot be
      established.  Section 11 gives the error codes for these refusals.

   *  If the exchange issues a refresh token, and so establishes a
      redeemable grant lineage (Section 6.5.2), the grant-continuity
      rules of Section 5.1 of
      [I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-client-instance-id] apply alongside
      Section 6.5; instance continuity alone does not authorize
      replacement of the recorded instance or key rebinding.

   *  Consumers establish presenter attribution under Sections 7.3 and
      7.5 of [I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-client-instance-id].  A
      consumer requiring attribution rejects unestablished associations
      under Section 7.6 of
      [I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-client-instance-id].

   *  This composition does not add provenance to client_instance: a
      consumer can use the direct-attestation trust configuration only
      when the issuer meets that configuration's restriction against
      upstream context.

   *  An issuer also conveying upstream context needs a separately
      specified, authenticated provenance mechanism before its context
      can support presenter attribution.

8.4.  Delegation Constraints

   What may be delegated, how far, and to whom is governed per Authority
   Set entry by a type-defined delegation policy (Section 5.5).  For
   mission_resource_access, the policy is the delegation member, whose
   concrete depth and matcher conditions the Mission Resource Access
   Profile defines
   ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-resource-access]).

   Because the policy lives in the entry, authority_hash commits it with
   the rest of the Authority Set, and it is carried with the entries
   wherever they go, including across a cross-domain projection where
   that profile's provenance rule keeps it
   ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-cross-domain]).

   *Delegation depth.* The delegation depth of a token is the number of
   delegations between the approved agent and the token's current actor:
   the agent's own non-delegated token is depth 0, the first delegate is
   depth 1, and each further delegate adds 1.  The depth checked against
   max_depth is that of the token being issued, computed after appending
   the new outermost actor, not the depth of the delegating token.  A
   credential projected across a trust domain carries no act chain and
   enters the target domain at depth 0
   ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-cross-domain]).

   *Depth accounting.* The AS MUST establish delegation depth from
   accounting it maintains for the tokens it issues under the Mission
   and associates with the credential it validates (for example,
   recorded at issuance against the token), not from the nesting of a
   presented act claim.  The act chain is attribution (Section 8.2): it
   renders the accounted chain and is not the source of the count.

   Over the exchanges this document defines, the accounting is complete:

   *  each delegated exchange adds exactly one delegate;

   *  a designation (Section 8.1) and a self-exchange (Section 7.5) add
      none; and

   *  a cross-domain projection is the one defined reset.

   A profile that issues Mission-bound tokens by another path specifies
   how it preserves or reconstructs this accounting and any reset it
   defines.

   Where the AS cannot establish the depth of the token being issued, no
   entry's delegation policy can be evaluated at that depth, so every
   entry narrows out under the per-entry rule below and the exchange is
   refused as an empty result.  A resource server bases no authorization
   decision on a prior actor (Section 4.1 of [RFC8693]).

   *Per-entry enforcement.* When the AS issues a token to a delegate
   (the actor that becomes the outermost act) at delegation depth d, it
   includes a Mission Authority Set entry in the delegated token's
   authorization_details only if both of the following hold:

   1.  The entry's type defines a delegation policy for the entry.  An
       entry carrying no type-defined delegation policy is non-
       delegable, the default.

   2.  That policy, evaluated at depth d, permits this delegate.  The AS
       applies the policy's own eligibility test at every exchange;
       where the policy leaves a matcher unstated, the AS's delegation-
       authorization policy decides, and absence is never a blanket
       grant.

   An entry failing either condition narrows out of the delegated token,
   consistent with the subset rule (Section 5.4).  The delegation policy
   is not part of the subset comparison itself, and a surviving entry
   carries it intact so the next hop is evaluated the same way.

   *Empty result.* If narrowing leaves no entries for the delegate, the
   AS MUST refuse the exchange with the invalid_target error code
   (Section 2.2.2 of [RFC8693]) rather than issue a token with empty
   authority.  The requested delegation has no authority to carry, while
   the subject grant itself remains valid for other exchanges.

   *The resource server enforces none of this.* The AS applies
   delegation constraints at issuance; a resource server sees only the
   already-narrowed authorization_details and enforces those as usual
   (Section 9.1).

8.5.  Delegated Token Example

   In this example, the Mission's Authority Set has two entries on the
   ERP: invoices.read, delegable to ai_agent actors through depth 2; and
   journal-entries.write, which carries no delegation member and is
   therefore non-delegable.  The approved agent s6BhdRkqt3 designates
   sub-agent tool-runner-7, an ai_agent, which redeems the designation
   at depth 1.  The read entry is permitted (depth 1 <= 2, ai_agent
   allowed) and the write entry narrows out.  The following is an
   example of the decoded delegated access token:

   {
     "iss": "https://as.example.com",
     "sub": "user_3p2q8mN1a0kV7tR",
     "aud": "https://erp.example.com",
     "client_id": "tool-runner-7",
     "iat": 1793607005,
     "exp": 1793607305,
     "jti": "at_3qX5bN7sR1tY8mZ9Kp2v",
     "authorization_details": [
       { "type": "mission_resource_access",
         "resource": "https://erp.example.com",
         "actions": ["invoices.read"],
         "constraints": {
           "resource_issued_after": "2026-07-01T00:00:00Z",
           "resource_issued_before": "2026-09-30T23:59:59Z"
         },
         "delegation": {
           "max_depth": 2,
           "allowed_delegates": [{ "sub_profile": "ai_agent" }]
         } }
     ],
     "act": {
       "sub": "tool-runner-7",
       "iss": "https://as.example.com",
       "sub_profile": "ai_agent"
     },
     "cnf": { "jkt": "Inaz7NtW9__w8ZNhk-CB7X_Y1G-XSCRAYT4oBffaS_0" },
     "mission": {
       "id": "msn_8RfX2Lqv9TqMv4z7sA2bN1k0YpEdHc9-",
       "issuer": "https://as.example.com"
     }
   }

   sub is still the user. client_id is tool-runner-7, the delegate that
   authenticated the Token Exchange and requested this token
   (Appendix C.3); it matches the outermost act only because tool-
   runner-7 authenticated the exchange itself. client_id does not name
   s6BhdRkqt3, the originally-approved agent, whose identity remains
   recoverable from the Mission Record via mission_id (Section 6.3).

   The cnf is tool-runner-7's own key, not the agent's, so this token
   cannot be replayed as the agent.  The non-delegable write entry was
   dropped; the read entry survives, carrying its delegation member so a
   further hop can be evaluated: a depth-3 delegate, or a non-ai_agent
   one, would narrow it out too.  The mission claim is unchanged.

9.  Token Consumption

   Section 9.1 states how a resource server enforces a Mission-bound
   token, and Section 9.2 how the issuer reports a token's Mission state
   through token introspection.

9.1.  Resource Server Enforcement

   A resource server enforces a JWT Mission-bound token from the token
   alone; no call to the AS is required.  It validates the token per
   Section 4 of [RFC9068], and any sender-constraint binding (cnf) per
   Section 7.1 of [RFC9449] or Section 3 of [RFC8705], locally, even
   when it introspects (Section 9.2.2).

   An opaque Mission-bound token is enforced from its active
   introspection response or issuer-local resolution instead, under the
   same rules (Section 9.2.4).

9.1.1.  Enforcement Requirements

   A resource server:

   1.  MUST treat authorization_details as the authoritative expression
       of authority and enforce each applicable entry according to that
       entry's own type specification (for mission_resource_access,
       [I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-resource-access]).  Where
       more than one carried entry applies, entries are alternative
       grants of authority, not conjunctive filters, unless the entry's
       type states otherwise.

   2.  MUST treat as providing no authority any applicable entry whose
       type it does not implement, or whose type-defined enforcement it
       cannot complete (an unrecognized member, an unenforceable
       constraint, or an unrecognized matching mode).  It MUST refuse
       the request, with insufficient_scope ([RFC6750]) or the RAR-
       remediation challenge as Section 9.1.4 states, unless another
       applicable entry that it can fully enforce authorizes the whole
       request on its own, and MUST NOT combine parts of several entries
       to authorize one request.  A failure of the token's own
       validation (above) rejects the token whatever its entries carry.

   3.  MUST NOT, when a token also carries scope, grant on the basis of
       a scope value any access broader than the corresponding
       authorization_details entry permits, including access that
       bypasses a constraint carried only in authorization_details.

   4.  MUST NOT infer the Mission's originally-approved agent from
       client_id, which names the client that requested this token, on a
       delegated token or otherwise (Section 4.3 of [RFC8693],
       Appendix C.3); the approved agent is recorded only in the Mission
       Record (Section 6.3) at the issuer.

   5.  MUST, when configured to require the mission claim for a Mission-
       governed resource, reject a token that lacks it with the
       invalid_token error code.  The issuance-side duty that pairs with
       this rejection is stated in Section 4.3, the downgrade it
       prevents in Section 16.1.2, and the metadata that advertises the
       requirement in Section 13.

   6.  MUST accept a mission claim only from an issuer it explicitly
       trusts to issue Mission-bound authorization for the resource;
       trusting the issuer's ordinary access tokens is not enough.  It
       MUST require mission.issuer to equal the token's iss, unless a
       Mission-binding profile it has adopted (Section 7.8.1), such as
       [I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-cross-domain], explicitly
       establishes the permitted relationship between the two.  It MUST
       reject a token that fails either check with the invalid_token
       error code.  For an opaque token, the issuer is the AS whose
       introspection response or issuer-local resolution the resource
       server relies on, and the same checks apply to that response's
       mission member (Section 9.2.4).

   Other sections state how a resource server treats a reported Mission
   state (Section 10), mission claim members (Section 7.8), an entry's
   resource against a request's target (Section 7.3), and a designation
   presented to it (Section 8.1).

   A resource server can also:

   *  require that a token carry an act chain and record it, while
      authorizing only the token's current actor (Section 4.1 of
      [RFC8693]);

   *  log the mission claim's id and the token's jti claim with each
      served request, so its access logs join to Mission evidence; or

   *  where the AS offers it, introspect the token (Section 9.2) to
      observe Mission state per request.

9.1.2.  Mission-Unaware Resource Servers

   A resource server that receives no delegated token need not be
   Mission-aware.  One that authorizes only from scope operates within
   the Mission only to the extent the AS established a safe projection
   for it at issuance (Section 7.7); constrained authority that no safe
   projection carries is enforced only where a resource server, or a
   runtime layer, evaluates authorization_details.

   Two parties keep a delegated Mission-bound token, one carrying an act
   claim, from consumers that do not process the act chain:

   *  *The AS, at issuance.* The AS MUST refuse, with invalid_target
      (Section 11), a request for a delegated Mission-bound token whose
      audience it has not established as a Mission-aware Resource Server
      (Section 9.1.1) that also processes the act chain, authorizing and
      logging the caller from the chain rather than from client_id
      alone.  That the audience parses act does not establish this.

   *  *The deployment, after issuance.* A deployment MUST NOT route a
      delegated Mission-bound token to a Mission-unaware resource
      server, or to logging or audit infrastructure, that authorizes or
      logs the caller on client_id without processing the act chain.
      The AS cannot verify where a token travels beyond its audience or
      which pipelines log its use, so that routing and logging remain
      the deployment's.

   A Mission-unaware [RFC9068] resource server reads client_id as the
   immediate client, which is accurate for that single token.  It cannot
   see the delegation lineage in the act chain or look up the
   originally-approved agent in the Mission Record, so it cannot
   recognize the current actor as a delegate, record that lineage, or
   join a delegate's action to the Mission's approval in its audit
   records.

   A resource that requires Mission-bound tokens can advertise that
   through the mission_bound_authorization_required protected resource
   metadata member (Section 13); a resource server that serves such a
   resource is, by that requirement, Mission-aware.  That requirement
   does not by itself establish that the resource server processes the
   act chain.

9.1.3.  Carried Authority

   An entry whose type-defined enforcement a resource server cannot
   complete fails closed because a type-defined constraint narrows
   authority: treating an unenforceable key or member as absent, or
   reducing it to disclosure-only, would widen the grant.

   The baseline token carries no authority_hash (Section 7.8).  Where a
   deployment discloses it to a resource server (through introspection's
   disclosure privilege, Section 9.2.1, or a companion profile that
   carries its own copy), it is an audit correlator, not an enforcement
   input, and not a cryptographic proof that the carried entries are a
   subset of the approved set.

   That subset relationship is an assertion by the AS, authenticated by
   the token signature.  Mission Approved-Set Verification
   ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-approved-set-verification])
   defines an independent check for a resource server that needs more
   than that assertion.

9.1.4.  Denial Responses

   A resource server denial falls into one of four cases, each using the
   OAuth challenge for its own failure class (Section 11 gives the
   codes):

   1.  *Weak or stale Subject authentication.* Where the deployment
       conveys the authentication of the token's Subject, through the
       claims of Section 7.6 or introspection (Section 6 of [RFC9470]),
       and that authentication does not meet the resource's requirement,
       the resource server challenges with
       insufficient_user_authentication and the acr_values or max_age
       parameters (Section 3 of [RFC9470]).

       Where no Subject authentication applies, the token carries no
       such claims and the challenge offers no remediation, so the
       resource server does not use it.  The Subject's authentication is
       a distinct fact from the Approver's approval-time authentication;
       a requirement on the Approver is an approval floor
       (Section 6.1.8), not a resource server challenge.

   2.  *Sender-constraint or key-binding failure.* The token's proof of
       possession is missing or invalid: the resource server challenges
       with invalid_token, in the DPoP scheme for a DPoP-bound token
       (Section 7.1 of [RFC9449], with use_dpop_nonce per Section 9 of
       [RFC9449]) and in the Bearer scheme for a certificate-bound token
       (Section 3 of [RFC8705]).  This is not a step-up: no fresh user
       authentication repairs a missing or wrong key.

   3.  *Insufficient carried authority.* The action is outside the
       token's carried authority: the resource server challenges with
       insufficient_scope ([RFC6750]), or with the RAR-remediation
       challenge where [I-D.draft-ietf-oauth-rar-metadata-remediation]
       is deployed (Section 9.1.5).

   4.  *Unenforceable constraint.* An applicable entry carries a type-
       defined member or constraint the resource server cannot enforce,
       and the request fails closed under the same base error as case 3.

   Cases 3 and 4 are identical 403 responses to a client, which cannot
   tell from them whether a new approval would help.  A Mission-aware
   Resource Server SHOULD therefore indicate which of the two cases
   applies by including, alongside error ([RFC6750]), the mission_denial
   attribute that this document defines for the WWW-Authenticate
   response header field, with one of two values:

   insufficient_authority:  The action is outside the token's carried
      authority; more requires a new approval or an expansion where that
      companion is deployed.

   constraint_unrecognized:  An applicable entry carries a type-defined
      member or constraint the resource server cannot enforce, and the
      request fails closed.  A client MUST NOT treat this value as
      inviting retry, step-up, or fresh approval: none of those makes a
      resource server enforce a constraint it does not implement.

   A value the client does not recognize is treated as
   insufficient_authority.  A resource server SHOULD include the
   attribute only in a response to a validly signed, audience-correct
   token whose holder its deployment permits to learn the distinction
   (Section 16.3.2).

9.1.5.  Remediation Grains

   A denial can carry independent remediation grains: separate
   remediation mechanisms, each naming a next step without granting
   anything.  There are three: the mission_denial attribute of the WWW-
   Authenticate challenge (Section 9.1.4); the
   insufficient_authorization error code with the
   authorization_remediation parameter
   ([I-D.draft-ietf-oauth-rar-metadata-remediation]), which names what
   mission_denial: insufficient_authority only points at; and the
   requestable denial of [AuthZEN.ARAP], profiled by
   [I-D.draft-mcguinness-mission-authzen], which routes the same denial
   into a governed access request rather than a fresh derivation.  Each
   grain keeps the wire shape and response status its defining document
   gives it.

   A client that decodes authorization_remediation can request the
   carried entries on the standard authorization_details parameter of a
   refresh request (Section 7.3), where they derive under this
   document's ordinary rules (Section 5): of an advertised, schema-valid
   type (Section 12), narrowed same-type (Section 5.4, Section 5.5) like
   any other request.  The AS issues only what both the presented
   refresh grant (Section 6 of [RFC9396]) and the Authority Set contain,
   and refuses a request beyond either (Section 7.4), so a refresh-token
   family narrower than the Mission does not obtain the Mission's wider
   authority.

   For entries outside the Authority Set, more authority requires a new
   approval, proposed as for a new Mission (Section 4.3), or an
   expansion where that companion is deployed
   ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-expansion]).  A client holding
   no refresh grant that covers the entries proposes them as for a new
   Mission.

   The three grains compose: a deployment can offer any subset, and none
   widens authority beyond what Section 5 derives from the same proposal
   unremediated.

9.2.  Mission State via Token Introspection

   A resource server consults the issuer about a Mission-bound token in
   one of three cases, with different obligations:

   *  *JWT state observation.* A resource server that observes Mission
      state per request introspects the JWT Mission-bound token it
      holds, instead of waiting for the token to expire.

   *  *Opaque-token consumption.* For an opaque Mission-bound token,
      introspection is the claims carriage: an active response carries
      what the JWT would (Section 9.2.4).

   *  *Issuer-local resolution.* In place of introspection for an opaque
      Mission-bound token, an AS can serve a qualifying resource server
      by a direct read of the AS's authoritative committed token and
      grant state (Section 9.2.4).

   An AS MAY support OAuth 2.0 Token Introspection [RFC7662] for
   Mission-bound access tokens.  When it does, an active: true response
   for such a token, to a caller authorized to receive Mission data
   (Section 9.2.1), MUST carry, in addition to the standard members, a
   mission member: a JSON object with the following members:

   *  id and issuer: as in the mission claim (Section 7.8).

   *  state: the Mission's current lifecycle state (string).  Its value
      space, including a state a deployed companion profile defines, and
      the consumer's forward-compatibility rule are those of Section 10.

   *  termination: when state is terminated, the Mission's termination
      (Section 10.1) (object).

   *  proposal_hash: when the Mission records an authority proposal, the
      Mission's proposal_hash (Section 6.3) (string).

   *  authority_hash: the Mission's Authority Set commitment
      (Section 6.3) (string).

   *  approval_basis: the Mission's approval_basis (Section 6.3),
      carrying type only.

   *  authority_source: the Mission's authority_source (Section 6.3),
      carrying type and, for organizational, policy with id and version
      only, never the policy digest.

   Only the Mission issuer reports state, termination, proposal_hash,
   authority_hash, approval_basis, and authority_source (Section 9.2.3).
   The last four are audit and correlation signals, not enforcement
   inputs (Section 9.1); each is disclosed only as Section 9.2.1
   permits.

   For a malformed, unknown, individually expired, or otherwise
   unresolvable token, the AS responds per [RFC7662] (active: false)
   with no mission member; it does not reveal Mission state for a token
   it cannot bind to a Mission.  The AS discloses a mission member only
   for a token it has established was issued on a path that conforms to
   Section 7.8.1; a token it cannot establish so is unresolvable in this
   sense.  How the AS establishes this is its own; a record of its own
   issuance is one means.

   The composite-active rule (Section 9.2.2) and the mission member
   apply equally when a Mission-bound refresh token is introspected.

   Freshness is per use.  Strengthening Section 4 of [RFC7662], which
   permits a protected resource to cache the response, this document
   defines no caching semantics for the mission member: a resource
   server that relies on introspection for Mission state treats each
   response as an observation for that decision, not as a cacheable
   state assertion.

   A deployment that needs bounded-staleness caching adopts the Mission
   Status companion, whose signed responses carry explicit freshness
   ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-status]).

9.2.1.  Caller Authorization and Minimization

   Strengthening Section 2.1 of [RFC7662], which requires some form of
   authorization to access the introspection endpoint, the AS:

   *  MUST authenticate the calling party.

   *  MUST return Mission data only to a caller authorized to receive
      it, in particular a resource server that is an audience of the
      token.

   *  MUST audience-filter the response, returning the
      authorization_details entries and Mission data relevant to the
      caller's audience and not disclosing entries addressed to other
      audiences (Section 7).

   These rules apply equally to the mission member of an active: false
   response (Section 9.2.2).

   Disclosure is member-scoped as well as caller-scoped. proposal_hash,
   authority_hash, approval_basis, and authority_source serve audit and
   correlation consumers, not resource server enforcement, and the AS
   MUST disclose each only to a caller the deployment has granted that
   member's disclosure privilege.  By default, an audience-authorized
   resource server receives the audience-filtered enforcement projection
   above, without them.

   A mission member that a companion profile defines for disclosure here
   follows the same member-scoped rule and is not an enforcement input.

9.2.2.  Composite Active State

   The introspection active member reflects the composite authorization,
   not the token in isolation.  Strengthening Section 4 of [RFC7662],
   the AS MUST return active: true only when the access token passes
   that section's checks (valid signature, unexpired, and not
   individually revoked) and the Mission is active.  A designation,
   which authorizes no resource access, is reported inactive with no
   mission member, as Section 8.1 states.

   The AS does not verify the token's sender-constraint (cnf) at
   introspection; the resource server checks proof of possession when
   the token is presented, so active: true is not by itself evidence
   that the caller holds the bound key.

   When the token is otherwise valid but the Mission is terminated, the
   AS MUST return active: false and include mission.state and
   mission.termination, whose reason gives the cause, so a resource
   server can distinguish a dead Mission from a bad token.  A Mission
   transition does not by itself revoke the token as an individual
   credential; introspection reports the composite authorization as
   inactive.  An otherwise valid token is one that passes those checks
   and that the AS has established was issued on a conforming path
   (Section 9.2).

   Reporting the mission member, mission.state included, for an inactive
   token deviates from the SHOULD NOT of Sections 2.2 and 4 of [RFC7662]
   against including additional information about an inactive token.
   The caller authorization and minimization rules (Section 9.2.1)
   govern that deviation.

9.2.3.  Issuer-Only Members

   An AS MUST NOT include mission.state, mission.termination,
   proposal_hash, authority_hash, approval_basis, or authority_source in
   an introspection response unless it holds the Mission, that is,
   unless it is the Mission issuer.  Introspection at a non-issuer
   Resource AS, which returns only the claim-shape members, is specified
   by the cross-domain companion
   ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-cross-domain]).

9.2.4.  Introspected Token Consumption

   The RFC 9068 JWT of Section 7 is this document's self-contained
   carriage.  An AS MAY instead issue a Mission-bound access token as an
   opaque reference token, under this mode and only under it; an opaque
   Mission-bound token outside this mode is not profiled.

   *  In this mode, an AS issuing opaque Mission-bound tokens MUST offer
      introspection for them with the members this mode names, unless
      every resource server that consumes them uses issuer-local
      resolution (below).

   *  An active (active: true) response for such a token MUST carry, as
      introspection response members, the audience-filtered granted
      authorization_details ([RFC9396]), the mission member above, aud,
      cnf where the token is sender-constrained ([RFC8705], [RFC9449]),
      and act where execution was delegated (Section 8): everything
      Section 7 requires the JWT to carry, sourced from the same
      issuance state.

   *  The granted authorization_details and any act chain appear only on
      an active response.  An inactive response carries active: false
      and the mission state facts (Section 9.2.2), never the authority
      itself.

   *  A resource server consuming an opaque Mission-bound token:

      -  MUST resolve it through introspection or issuer-local
         resolution (below) before service;

      -  MUST verify active is true, its own identity in aud, and the
         sender-constraint binding cnf names; and

      -  MUST enforce the response's authorization_details under the
         same rules as the token-carried form (Section 9.1), the fail-
         closed duties included.

   *  A resource server whose introspection response for an opaque
      Mission-bound token is active: false, or whose issuer-local
      resolution resolves it as inactive, MUST refuse the request with
      the invalid_token error code (Section 3.1 of [RFC6750]), in the
      DPoP scheme for a DPoP-bound token (Section 7.1 of [RFC9449]).

   *  A resource server that cannot obtain a valid introspection
      response or issuer-local resolution for an opaque Mission-bound
      token MUST refuse the request rather than serve it from any cached
      or out-of-band belief about the token's authority.  The failure
      establishes nothing about the token: the resource server MUST
      respond with HTTP status code 503 (Service Unavailable)
      (Section 15.6.4 of [RFC9110]), adding a Retry-After header field
      where a retry time is meaningful, and MUST NOT report the token as
      invalid, such as with the invalid_token error code.

   The per-use freshness rule of Section 9.2 covers the whole response
   in this mode: each response is one observation of the token's claims
   and Mission state, not a cacheable authority record.

   An AS MAY serve a resource server through issuer-local resolution: a
   direct read of the AS's authoritative committed token and grant
   state, not a call to an introspection endpoint.  [RFC7662]
   introspection keeps its meaning as a network protocol.  A resource
   server qualifies only when it is inside the issuer's trusted
   implementation boundary and the AS authorizes it to read that state;
   common ownership or a shared network does not by itself qualify it.

   For each resolution, the AS:

   1.  MUST resolve the presented token to that token's own issuer-
       qualified issuance or grant record, and establish from that
       record the token's expiry, revocation and grant validity, its
       intended audience, the composite active state (Section 9.2.2),
       and the token's own authority;

   2.  MUST authorize the resource server and minimize disclosure to it
       as Section 9.2.1 requires for an introspection caller; and

   3.  MUST yield, when the token is active, only what an active
       response for that token carries in this mode, audience-filtered
       for that resource server and with act and cnf as that response
       carries them, and never the Mission's Authority Set or other
       authority the token does not carry.

   Mission state alone, or a lookup by Mission Identifier, never
   establishes a token's validity or authority.  An unknown, invalid,
   expired or revoked token, or a Mission that is not active, resolves
   as inactive and yields no authority; missing or unavailable state
   yields no valid resolution.

   In the resource server duties above, the resolution stands in for the
   introspection response, possession proof and fail-closed refusal
   included, and each resolution is one per-use observation, not a
   cacheable assertion.

9.2.5.  Introspection Response Examples

   The following is an example of an introspection response for the
   canonical ERP token (Section 7.8) while the Mission is active.  The
   issuer AS returns the standard [RFC7662] members and the mission
   member to a caller holding the deployment's audit-and-correlation
   disclosure privilege, so authority_hash and proposal_hash appear; the
   default audience-filtered enforcement projection omits them.

   {
     "active": true,
     "iss": "https://as.example.com",
     "sub": "user_3p2q8mN1a0kV7tR",
     "client_id": "s6BhdRkqt3",
     "aud": "https://erp.example.com",
     "exp": 1793606705,
     "authorization_details": [
       { "type": "mission_resource_access",
         "resource": "https://erp.example.com",
         "actions": ["invoices.read"],
         "constraints": {
           "resource_issued_after": "2026-07-01T00:00:00Z",
           "resource_issued_before": "2026-09-30T23:59:59Z"
         },
         "delegation": {
           "max_depth": 2,
           "allowed_delegates": [{ "sub_profile": "ai_agent" }]
         } },
       { "type": "mission_resource_access",
         "resource": "https://erp.example.com",
         "actions": ["journal-entries.write"],
         "constraints": {
           "max_amount": { "amount": "500.00", "currency": "USD" }
         } }
     ],
     "mission": {
       "id": "msn_8RfX2Lqv9TqMv4z7sA2bN1k0YpEdHc9-",
       "issuer": "https://as.example.com",
       "authority_hash":
         "sha-256:hjXlwWBY64-RPs1Z-E_gbyklNcViI5c9jiyMbE4KvFM",
       "proposal_hash":
         "sha-256:p-s-EzdNQTn6RoPHXESkwVtGdk8yL1PxryNyMwroKGo",
       "state": "active"
     }
   }

   The following is an example of the response, after the Mission is
   revoked, for an unexpired token the AS issued on refresh before the
   revocation (Section 9.2.2):

   {
     "active": false,
     "mission": {
       "id": "msn_8RfX2Lqv9TqMv4z7sA2bN1k0YpEdHc9-",
       "issuer": "https://as.example.com",
       "authority_hash":
         "sha-256:hjXlwWBY64-RPs1Z-E_gbyklNcViI5c9jiyMbE4KvFM",
       "state": "terminated",
       "termination": {
         "reason": "revoked",
         "terminated_at": "2026-11-02T08:53:20Z"
       }
     }
   }

10.  Mission Lifecycle

   A Mission is in one of two states:

   *  active: the only state in which the AS derives tokens.

   *  terminated: a terminal state; the Mission has ended.  Its
      termination member (Section 10.1) gives the reason: revoked when
      it was revoked (Section 10.2), or expired when its expires_at
      passed.

   The transitions are:

          +========+====================+============+=========+
          | From   | Event              | To         | Reason  |
          +========+====================+============+=========+
          | (none) | approval event     | active     |         |
          +--------+--------------------+------------+---------+
          | active | revoke             | terminated | revoked |
          +--------+--------------------+------------+---------+
          | active | expires_at reached | terminated | expired |
          +--------+--------------------+------------+---------+

                  Table 2: Mission lifecycle transitions

   The Mission Lifecycle States registry (Section 19.7) holds these
   states, and the Mission Termination Reasons registry (Section 19.8)
   holds the reasons.  A companion profile MAY register an additional
   state for a lifecycle it introduces (for example, a paused state).  A
   companion profile that defines a further way for a Mission to end
   registers a termination reason, not a state.

   Wherever a Mission state is reported, including the Mission Record
   and the introspection mission member, a consumer MUST treat only the
   exact value active as permitting derivation or continued reliance,
   and MUST treat every other value, including one it does not
   recognize, as non-active and non-deriving (the forward-compatibility
   rule).

   For every state-dependent decision this document defines, the AS MUST
   treat a Mission as active only when its stored state is active and
   the decision time is strictly before expires_at.  From expires_at, a
   Mission whose stored state is still active is terminated with reason
   expired for every such decision, whether or not that transition is
   persisted, including when persisting it fails.

   Persisting that transition, and emitting any lifecycle event a state-
   distribution companion defines, can happen after the decision that
   observed the boundary; persisting it never replaces a termination
   already recorded.

10.1.  Mission Termination

   A Mission whose state is terminated carries a termination object, in
   the Mission Record and wherever its state is reported with it; a
   Mission in any other state carries none.  Its members are:

   reason:  REQUIRED.  A string, a value of the Mission Termination
      Reasons registry (Section 19.8): revoked or expired in this
      document, or a reason a companion profile registers.

   terminated_at:  REQUIRED, except as described below.  A string.  An
      RFC 3339 [RFC3339] date-time: the instant the termination took
      effect.  For expired, it is the Mission's expires_at.

   A companion profile that registers a reason defines that reason's
   further members.  A profile that maintains a state version for the
   Mission adds the version of the transition that committed the
   termination as its audit reference.

   The issuer writes termination once, atomically with the transition to
   terminated, and MUST NOT change it: an expiry, or any later event,
   does not replace the reason or references of a Mission already
   terminated.

   A termination carries every member its reason requires, except as
   follows.

   A termination need not carry a member its reason requires when it was
   recorded before its reason defined that member, or derived from such
   a termination: such a termination exposes only the facts its record
   retained and omits a member it has no retained value for,
   terminated_at included, rather than inventing one.  For expired, the
   retained terminated_at is the Mission's expires_at; for another
   reason, a retained commit time is its terminated_at only where that
   commit was the termination's effect.

   A consumer that receives such a termination, or a reason it does not
   recognize, still treats the Mission as terminated: it stops work
   governed by the Mission, follows no absent reference, and infers no
   cause-specific action.

   For a transition period, a consumer of a report that still carries
   revoked, expired, completed, superseded, or cascaded as a Mission's
   state MAY read it as terminated with that reason (cascaded as
   parent_terminated) and the facts the report retained; an emitter
   never reports those values as a state.  That reading is local: the
   consumer never re-emits or re-signs it, and it verifies a signed
   artifact over its original bytes, which it retains, before reading it
   this way.

10.2.  Revocation

   A Mission is revoked when the AS receives a revocation for it from a
   party the deployment's policy authorizes to revoke it, or when that
   policy itself terminates the Mission; either way, its termination
   reason is revoked.  The Subject, the Approver, and an administrator
   are parties that policy can authorize; none is authorized by its role
   alone.  A Mission Issuer MUST accept, through an authenticated means
   independent of possession of any token, a revocation by mission_id
   from a party the deployment's policy authorizes (so a Mission can be
   stopped even when no refresh token is held).

   The wire shape of that revocation is out of scope for this document.
   A deployment-defined authenticated surface satisfies this
   requirement; Mission Status
   ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-status]) defines an
   interoperable revoke operation.

   When the AS establishes that the principal identified by a Mission's
   recorded subject (Section 6.3.3) has been deprovisioned, it SHOULD
   terminate that Mission with termination reason revoked.  The rule
   reaches every Mission not yet terminated, including one a companion
   profile has suspended, and identifies the Subject by its recorded iss
   and sub.  Temporary disablement, a source outage, or an unavailable
   lookup does not by itself establish deprovisioning.  The departure or
   disablement of an Approver, an accountable owner, or a client
   triggers the rule only when that principal is the recorded Subject;
   an organizational Mission records the organizational principal as its
   Subject (Section 6.1.7), so an approving officer's departure does not
   terminate it under this rule.  A deployment that does not follow this
   rule documents why; that exception cannot bypass a continuing
   dependency the deployment requires or rewrite the recorded Subject.
   Termination stops further issuance (Section 7.1) but does not itself
   recall tokens already issued; an earlier execution cutoff depends on
   the deployment's enforcement mechanism.

   Revoking a token does not by itself revoke a Mission.  Section 2.1 of
   [RFC7009] lets an authorization server's revocation policy extend a
   token's revocation to related tokens and the underlying grant; a
   deployment's revocation policy can likewise treat revoking a
   Mission's refresh token as revoking the Mission; a deployment that
   couples token revocation to Mission revocation documents that
   behavior.

   Token validity and Mission validity are distinct: an issued access
   token remains valid until it expires (Section 7.1).  A deployment
   whose consumers rely on Mission state beyond a token's lifetime
   offers token introspection (Section 9.2) or Mission Status
   ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-status]), so they read current
   state rather than infer it from token validity.

11.  Error and Challenge Mapping

   This document reuses standard OAuth errors and challenges, selected
   by parameter ownership and processing stage.  The following tables
   are the normative statement of the base OAuth error for each failure
   they list.  Every rule elsewhere in this document that names one of
   these codes applies this mapping.

   An AS that performs an applicable check early, at PAR, responds with
   the same error class the check would yield at the authorization or
   token endpoint.  Section 2.3 of [RFC9126] permits an authorization-
   request error at PAR, and returning it there does not change which
   row applies.

   Where a token request fails more than one check, this document
   defines no general precedence among the resulting errors; as under
   [RFC6749], which defines none, the AS returns any applicable one.
   The orders this document does state still apply: a submission's
   processing order (Section 4.5), and the Mission-governed refusal,
   which applies only to an otherwise processable request (Section 4.3).
   The choice relaxes neither client authentication nor any rule that
   limits who receives a detail, such as mission_error (Section 7.1).

   The tables are grouped by surface.  The first table holds each
   failure whose rule is not confined to the surface of one of the other
   three tables (PAR and the authorization endpoint, Token Exchange, or
   a protected resource); each failure appears once.  Token Exchange is
   a token-endpoint operation: a row of the first table that names the
   token endpoint or a token request covers an exchange, alongside the
   exchange rows of the third table.
