   In these tables, which evidence types are required follows
   [I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-submission-evidence], and
   invalid_client_attestation is the error code that Section 5.2 of
   [I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-client-instance-id] specifies.

12.  Authorization Server Metadata

   This document defines the following authorization server metadata
   parameter [RFC8414]:

   mission_bound_authorization_supported:  OPTIONAL boolean.  When true,
      the AS supports the core Mission Issuer surfaces of this document
      (Section 15): the mission_intent authorization request parameter
      through PAR (Section 4), Authority Set derivation yielding
      authorization_details entries of its supported types (Section 5,
      Section 5.5), Mission-bound access tokens (Section 7), and the
      mission JWT claim (Section 7.8).  It asserts Mission Issuer
      support only; it makes no claim about any resource server or about
      the optional capabilities, whose discovery is described below.  If
      omitted, the default value is false.

   A deployment can instead arrange Mission-bound authorization,
   including its supported types and schemas, out of band.  A client
   holding a Mission Intent MUST NOT submit the same authority as bare
   scope or authorization_details to an AS whose Mission support is
   neither advertised nor otherwise established; it surfaces the
   inability instead (Section 16.1.2).  Where the deployment's AS cannot
   change, the standalone Mission Authority Server
   ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-mission-authority-server]) is the governed
   alternative.

   A client performing a task under a Mission MUST NOT present, for that
   task, a credential that is not Mission-bound, such as an ordinary
   access token it already holds, unless a deployment-defined, trusted
   enforcement path establishes that Mission for the request and applies
   the Mission's authority and lifecycle controls to it.  Neither a
   Mission Identifier the client supplies nor documentation alone
   establishes the Mission for a request, and the path confers no
   authority beyond the Mission's.  The rule does not restrict the
   client's workflows unrelated to the Mission (Section 16.1.2).

   An AS that advertises support for this document MUST include at least
   one AS-supported type in its authorization_details_types_supported
   metadata ([RFC9396]): the approved-set commitment a Mission-aware
   client relies on.  Where mission_resource_access is among them, the
   Mission Resource Access Profile
   ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-resource-access]), not out-of-
   band documentation, is that type's normative definition.

   An AS that advertises mission_bound_authorization_supported: true
   MUST also publish pushed_authorization_request_endpoint ([RFC9126]),
   since the authorization-code path accepts a Mission Intent only
   through PAR (Section 4.2).

   An AS that advertises mission_bound_authorization_supported: true
   SHOULD also advertise authorization_details_types_metadata_endpoint
   [I-D.draft-ietf-oauth-rar-metadata-remediation] where it implements
   that endpoint.  Implementing that endpoint is optional, and the rules
   below bind only an AS that advertises it:

   *  its response's key set is the source of truth for which types the
      AS supports;

   *  authorization_details_types_supported mirrors those keys and MUST
      NOT list a type absent from them; and

   *  the AS MUST publish, within that response, an entry for every
      supported type whose schema validates that type's documented
      object shape.  For mission_resource_access, that shape, including
      the Common Constraints structure, is the Mission Resource Access
      Profile's ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-resource-access]).

   The optional capabilities are discovered first through existing OAuth
   metadata ([RFC8414]): introspection_endpoint for introspection, and
   grant_types_supported containing urn:ietf:params:oauth:grant-
   type:token-exchange for self-exchange, for delegation, and for the
   companion's cross-domain grant issuance.  Advertising that grant type
   does not by itself establish support for every exchange variant, such
   as self-exchange or delegation.  Absent such a signal, a capability
   is discovered out of band or by attempt: a Token Exchange, a cross-
   domain grant issuance, or an introspection request fails if the
   issuer does not support it.

13.  Protected Resource Metadata

   This document defines the following protected resource metadata
   parameter [RFC9728]:

   mission_bound_authorization_required:  OPTIONAL boolean.  When true,
      the protected resource accepts only Mission-bound tokens: a token
      that lacks the mission claim (Section 7.8) is rejected
      (Section 9.1).  If omitted, the default value is false.  An AS
      that has authenticated this metadata treats a true value as making
      the resource Mission-governed (Section 4.3); false or omission
      does not clear a deployment's classification.

   A type-defined authorization_details member may define its own
   constraint-discovery surface; mission_resource_access's is defined by
   the Mission Resource Access Profile
   ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-resource-access]).

   A protected resource can list the authorization_details types it
   accepts in the authorization_details_types_supported parameter
   (Section 2 of [RFC9728]), so a client can propose entries of a type
   its target accepts.

14.  Extensibility

   Other specifications can extend this document.  Extensions build
   alongside the stable interface below; they MUST NOT redefine it.  The
   following remain stable across revisions of this document:

   *  the mission claim members id and issuer (Section 7.8);

   *  the authorization_details carriage and its type-agnostic subset
      discipline (Section 5.5, Section 5.4); and

   *  the act delegation chain (Section 8.2).

14.1.  Extension Points

   This document's extension points are:

   *  *Authority types.* The Authority Set is open to any AS-supported
      authorization_details type (Section 5.5); the Mission apparatus
      (commitment, gating, delegation) is type-agnostic, subject to the
      delegation and projection limits in Section 5.5.  A type's
      specification supplies what the apparatus relies on for its
      entries:

      -  the definition a proposed entry is validated against, alongside
         any schema advertised for the type or established out of band
         (Section 4.5);

      -  the enforcement semantics a resource server applies to an entry
         (Section 9.1.1);

      -  the subset relation, which the AS applies only where it
         declares narrowing for the type; otherwise an entry is carried
         as approved (Section 5.4, Section 5.5.1);

      -  the delegation policy, which the AS applies only where it
         declares delegation; an entry with no type-defined policy is
         non-delegable (Section 8.4);

      -  when a scope-projection mapping is safe for the type's entries,
         which the AS relies on only where it declares projection
         (Section 7.7); and

      -  any normalization a comparison of its members uses; without
         one, URI-valued members compare byte for byte (Section 6.4.2).

   *  *Intent Submission Evidence types.* The evidence array of the
      Submission envelope is open to evidence types defined by companion
      profiles: each type is a collision-resistant name whose owning
      specification defines the entry's closed schema, verification, and
      verified output facts
      ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-submission-evidence]), and an
      AS refuses an entry of a type it does not support rather than
      ignoring it (Section 4.4).

   *  *Mission Intent members.* The Mission Intent's top level
      (Section 4) is open to members a companion profile defines, under
      a short name it registers in the Mission Intent Members registry
      (Section 19.9) before use, or under a collision-resistant name.
      The owning specification defines, produces, and enforces each such
      member, and a recognized member never grants or widens authority
      beyond what that specification states.  Closed-top-level
      validation (Section 4.2) refuses any top-level member that neither
      this document nor an implemented companion profile defines, and
      the AS ignores none it recognizes.  Registration settles which
      specification owns a short name; it does not make an AS implement,
      recognize, or trust any member.

   *  *Integrity anchors.* Additional committed objects use the same
      domain-separated, issuer-bound envelope with a new typ
      (Section 6.4.1).  A consent-disclosure commitment, an instruction-
      text attestation, or a delegation receipt can be committed this
      way without changing this document.  A profile that commits an
      evidence or disclosure object MUST commit it with this envelope
      and a typ that meets Section 6.4.1, not by hashing the bare
      object, so the domain separation and issuer binding hold
      uniformly.  A mission descriptor embedded in such an object uses
      the mission claim shape (Section 7.8), optionally extended with
      collision-resistantly named members (for example, an intent_hash
      for audit), and is never authority-bearing on its own.

   *  *The mission claim.* It is an open object: additional, collision-
      resistantly named members can be carried in it (for example, a
      runtime decision reference, a delegation receipt, or an
      attestation reference), under the consumer rules of Section 7.8.

   *  *Mission Record members.* The Mission Record is open to additional
      members set at creation, under short names a companion profile
      coordinates with this document or under collision-resistant names,
      as Section 6.3 states.

   *  *Lifecycle state and termination reason.* The lifecycle state
      space (Section 10) is open to additional states that companion
      profiles register in the Mission Lifecycle States registry
      (Section 19.7) for lifecycles they introduce.  The termination
      reasons (Section 10.1) are likewise open to reasons that companion
      profiles register in the Mission Termination Reasons registry
      (Section 19.8).  Under the forward-compatibility rule of
      Section 10, only active permits issuance.

   *  *Approval-event sequencing.* The approval-event steps, their
      order, and the atomicity of record creation with the approval
      decision are fixed by Section 6.1.2; the coupling of that decision
      to authorization-code issuance belongs to the direct flow.  A
      companion profile MAY relocate the approval event relative to code
      issuance (for example, deferring the decision beyond the
      authorization response), provided the steps and their atomicity
      hold unchanged and no Mission reference exists before the record
      is active; the Mission Deferred Approval companion is such a
      profile ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-approval]).  A back-
      channel carriage profile (Section 4.2) MAY instead couple the
      approval decision to establishing the Mission grant binding and
      activating for redemption a grant the profile defines, such as a
      device code issued while the request was pending, provided the
      steps and their atomicity with the approval decision hold
      unchanged and no Mission reference exists before the record is
      active; the Mission then binds to that profile-defined reusable
      grant (Section 6.5.1).

   This document defines no capability-negotiation mechanism or profile-
   version field; an extension declares its own identifiers and, where
   it needs discovery, its own metadata.  Section 14.2 states the
   general rule these extension points follow.

14.2.  Namespace Taxonomy

   This document's extensible namespaces follow one of three postures:

   *  *Registry-backed.* A namespace whose values determine fail-closed
      behavior and span multiple documents is backed by an IANA
      registry.  This document creates the Mission Lifecycle States
      (Section 19.7), Mission Termination Reasons (Section 19.8), and
      Mission Intent Members (Section 19.9) registries and seeds each
      with the values it defines; every further document that defines a
      value requests that value's registration, carrying any Internet-
      Draft reference as a publication dependency under the registry's
      policy.

   *  *Specification-defined.* A namespace with a defined fail-safe for
      unknown values and no registry, such as the mission claim members
      (Section 7.8), the Mission Record members (Section 6.3) and the
      approval_basis.type values (Section 6.3.3), is specification-
      defined: the defining documents are its value space.

   *  *Collision-resistant.* Deployment-defined names are collision-
      resistant names (Section 4.2 of [RFC7519]) and are never
      registered.

   A typ inside a JCS commitment envelope (Section 6.4.1) names a hash
   domain, not a representation crossing a protocol boundary, and is not
   a media type.

15.  Conformance

   The smallest useful conforming deployment is a Mission Issuer that:

   *  derives in narrowing mode from the client's authority proposal
      (Section 5);

   *  supports one AS-supported authorization_details type and emits
      only that type's specification-defined vocabulary; and

   *  implements none of the optional capabilities.

   A Mission Issuer can instead start from configured-mapping mode
   (Section 5), which is equally conforming.  Neither starting point is
   a conformance class, and neither removes a surface: the Mission
   Issuer still implements every surface its role lists (Section 15.1),
   the evidence dispatch and refusal rules included even when it
   supports no evidence type.

15.1.  Conformance Roles

   An implementation conforms in one of three roles.

   A *Mission Issuer* (the authorization server) implements the core
   issuance surfaces:

   *  submission of a Mission Intent, in the Submission envelope, via
      PAR (Section 4.2), with the Intent Submission Evidence dispatch
      and refusal rules (Section 4.4);

   *  Authority Set derivation yielding authorization_details entries of
      its supported types (Section 5, Section 5.5);

   *  the approval event with its integrity anchors and its recorded
      approval_basis and authority_source (Section 6.1, Section 6.3);

   *  issuance of Mission-bound access tokens carrying the mission claim
      (Section 7), as the RFC 9068 JWT or as an opaque reference token
      under the introspected consumption mode (Section 9.2.4);

   *  the subset rule (Section 5.4);

   *  gating of issuance on Mission state (Section 7.1); and

   *  acceptance of a revocation by mission_id through an authenticated
      means independent of possession of any token (Section 10.2).

   An AS can conform as a Mission Issuer without supporting any Intent
   Submission Evidence type.  Such an AS refuses every presented entry
   under the dispatch and refusal rules (Section 4.4): a structurally
   invalid entry with the invalid_request error code, and any other
   entry, as an unsupported type, with the
   invalid_mission_intent_evidence error code.  It need not implement
   the evidence framework of
   [I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-submission-evidence] for this
   configuration.  The companion defines conformance for ASs supporting
   an evidence type, whether its evidence is optional or required.

   A *Mission-aware Resource Server* implements resource server
   enforcement (Section 9.1).  It enforces a JWT from the token's own
   claims, needing no Mission-state store and no call to the AS.  Under
   the introspected consumption mode, it instead enforces the token from
   the token-specific inputs of its active introspection response or
   issuer-local resolution (Section 9.2.4).

   In either form, it enforces each applicable entry its tokens carry
   under that entry's type, which for mission_resource_access is work a
   plain [RFC9068] resource server does not do.  Conformance in this
   role implies support for no particular type: an entry of a type the
   resource server does not implement provides no authority there
   (Section 9.1.1), so interoperable enforcement needs a type that both
   the AS and the resource server implement.

   A *Mission Client* implements the client surfaces:

   *  submission of the Mission Intent via PAR on the authorization-code
      path (Section 4.2), proposing concrete authority, where it does,
      on the authorization_details parameter pushed alongside
      mission_intent (Section 4.3);

   *  reading its granted authority from the token-response
      authorization_details echo, and assuming none absent from it
      (Section 7.3); and

   *  obtaining mission_id from the mission_id token-response parameter
      (Section 7.3.1), never from the access token, treating it as a
      reference, not a credential.

15.2.  Optional Capabilities

   Beyond these mandatory roles, an implementation can additionally
   claim four OPTIONAL capabilities.  Each is independent, and an
   implementation that supports none of them is still conformant:

   *  *Delegation* (Section 8): issuing designations (Section 8.1) and
      the derived tokens that carry the act delegation chain, and
      consuming those tokens.  Only a sender-constrained token can be
      designated, so Delegation requires sender-constrained primary
      tokens.

   *  *Self-Exchange* (Section 7.5): narrowing an agent's own Mission-
      bound token by a Token Exchange that names no actor.  Supporting
      Delegation does not require Self-Exchange, and supporting Self-
      Exchange does not require Delegation.

   *  *Introspection* (Section 9.2): reporting Mission state through the
      mission token introspection response member.  Required where the
      AS issues opaque Mission-bound tokens to a resource server that
      does not use issuer-local resolution (Section 9.2.4).  Issuer-
      local resolution does not by itself support a claim of this
      capability.

   *  *Cross-Domain*: projecting a Mission so it is honored by an
      authorization server in another trust domain.  An implementation
      claiming this capability preserves, across the hop:

      1.  the Mission reference (mission.id, mission.issuer) and the
          authority_hash the projection carries, intact;

      2.  authority that only narrows (Section 5.4);

      3.  projection performed only by, or under the authorization of,
          the Mission issuer, gated on the Mission's active state
          (Section 10, Section 7.1); and

      4.  projected credential lifetimes capped by the Mission's
          expires_at (Section 7.6).

      This bar is self-contained in this document.  The companion
      Mission Cross-Domain Projection profile
      ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-cross-domain]) specifies the
      interoperable mechanism that satisfies it, and implementations
      that interoperate across the hop implement that companion.

   Mission Approved-Set Verification
   ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-approved-set-verification])
   defines an independent check, by a resource server or policy decision
   point, that a token's carried authority is a subset of the complete
   approved Authority Set; conformance to this document does not require
   it.

15.3.  Claiming Conformance

   A conforming implementation names the optional capabilities it
   supports (for example, "Mission Issuer with Delegation and Cross-
   Domain"); each capability's defining section or document states its
   detailed requirements.

   Emitting the mission claim is not, by itself, conformance: the claim
   is reserved for conforming issuance (Section 7.8.1).  Conformance as
   a Mission Issuer requires the gates: authority derived from the
   approved Intent and committed by the anchors (Section 6.1), issuance
   bounded by the subset rule (Section 5.4), and derivation gated on
   Mission state (Section 7.1).  An implementation that carries Mission
   metadata without these gates conforms to no role in this document and
   does not implement it: in particular, it MUST NOT advertise
   mission_bound_authorization_supported as true (Section 12), the
   machine-checkable form of that claim.

   The Mission Binding Properties vector of
   [I-D.draft-mcguinness-mission-architecture] names what these gates
   supply as its credential-mission-bound property, informatively; these
   gates remain authoritative for conformance to this document
   regardless.

   This document makes no Mission Substrate conformance claim and takes
   no requirement from the substrate contract
   ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-mission-substrate]), which is informative.

16.  Security Considerations

   The security considerations of OAuth 2.0 (Section 10 of [RFC6749]),
   the OAuth 2.0 Security Best Current Practice [RFC9700], Rich
   Authorization Requests (Section 12 of [RFC9396]), Pushed
   Authorization Requests (Section 7 of [RFC9126]), and JWT access
   tokens (Section 5 of [RFC9068]) apply to this document.  Those of
   Token Exchange (Section 5 of [RFC8693]) apply to delegation, and
   those of DPoP (Section 11 of [RFC9449]) and mutual TLS (Section 7 of
   [RFC8705]) apply where a token is sender-constrained.

16.1.  Commitment and Consent Integrity

16.1.1.  Consent Binding

   The security goal of this document is that a user's approval of a
   task bounds every token derived for it.  The Mission records
   authority_hash, which commits the exact Authority Set the Approver
   consented to (Section 6.3).  The goal rests on the AS deriving only
   subsets of that set and signing every token accordingly, and on the
   resource server verifying the AS's token signature and enforcing the
   carried authority, not on every token carrying the commitment
   (Section 7.8).

   A derived token can carry a narrowed subset, so a resource server
   cannot in general recompute authority_hash from the token, and the
   hash supplies no subset proof.  A resource server that does not
   verify against the complete set relies on the signed token as the
   AS's assertion that the carried authority was correctly projected
   (Section 9.1).

   A deployment that needs assurance independent of the token signature
   verifies the carried authority against the complete approved set.
   What that verification provides depends on when the issuer is
   compromised.

   Retrieving the complete set under the same trust root as the token
   adds nothing against an issuer malicious at approval time: that
   issuer can approve and commit arbitrary authority, and no containment
   mechanism changes that.  The same checks do defend against projection
   implementation errors, against corruption of the record after an
   independently anchored approval commitment, and against post-approval
   signing-key compromise where the original commitment is pinned
   outside the issuer.

   intent_hash extends the same protection to the task: it commits the
   approved Mission Intent, so an auditor can detect any later
   alteration of the recorded task.  No anchor substitutes for another.
   authority_hash is computed over the Authority Set alone
   (Section 6.4.1), what a resource server enforces and a cross-domain
   projection carries, so it can be checked without the Intent.
   intent_hash stays tamper-evident audit material even where the
   authority is projected without the Intent.

   The anchors do not commit the *rendered consent disclosure*: none
   binds the locale, disclosure-template version, or material notices
   the Approver was shown.  A buggy or malicious rendering layer could
   therefore show a narrower or different task than the committed
   Authority Set without a committed trace.

   A deployment whose Missions carry high-risk authority can retain
   presentation-level audit evidence from which the disclosure shown can
   be reconstructed and audited.  Mission Consent Evidence
   [I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-consent-evidence] binds a
   consent_rendering_hash over a structured consent-disclosure object on
   the wire; an AS that does not implement it can record equivalent
   evidence out of band.  Such a commitment binds the structured
   disclosure the AS records, not the presentation itself; it narrows
   this gap for audit but does not close it.

   The anchors do not commit the approval basis either: who consented,
   who activated the Mission, and under which basis (Section 6.3).  The
   record's immutability protects it, and only the AS enforces that.  A
   deployment that needs the approval basis checkable outside the AS can
   register the approval event under Mission Audit Transparency
   [I-D.draft-mcguinness-mission-audit], whose registration covers the
   record at creation except its state and termination, or carry the
   Approval Context Commitment of
   [I-D.draft-mcguinness-mission-approval-governance], whose closed
   manifest includes the approval basis, in a signed artifact.

16.1.2.  Downgrade by Omission

   A token bearing equivalent authorization_details but no mission claim
   is governed by no Mission state, revocation, or consent commitment.
   Section 4.3, Section 12, and Section 9.1 address this downgrade at
   issuance, at the client, and at enforcement.

   A held ordinary credential needs no request to the AS, and neither an
   AS nor a resource server can generally tell that a request made with
   one serves a Mission.  An unmediated ordinary workflow at a resource
   that is not Mission-governed therefore remains a residual that only
   the client's own conformance closes; the Mission Security Model
   ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-mission-security-model]) analyzes that path
   and where mediation closes it.

   The integrity of the mission_bound_authorization_supported
   (Section 12) and mission_bound_authorization_required (Section 13)
   metadata rests on the retrieval protections of [RFC8414] and
   [RFC9728], whose security considerations apply.

16.1.3.  Compromised or Over-Broad Derivation

   The AS is trusted to derive authority no broader than the Mission
   Intent.  Both Authority Set derivation modes (Section 5), a proposal
   narrowed to policy and a configured mapping, are mechanical rather
   than free-form inference.  The recorded policy_version makes each
   Authority Set derivation auditable by naming the policy it ran under.

16.2.  Agent-Specific Threats

16.2.1.  Prompt Injection and Data Exfiltration

   This document assumes that an agent reading attacker-influenceable
   content can be prompt-injected.  Authority is fixed at the approval
   event, so injected text cannot expand an approved Mission. purpose
   supplies candidate authority only as a lookup key of the pre-approval
   Authority Set derivation whose result the Approver reads and consents
   to, and otherwise can only refuse or tighten; goal and task_bounds
   bound it only through that disclosure (Section 4, Section 5).

   Within an approved Authority Set, an injected agent can still read
   what the Mission permits and write to a sink the Mission permits: a
   Mission governs actions, not the content that flows within an
   authorized action.  The Mission Security Model
   ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-mission-security-model]) analyzes that
   residual; constraining it is the runtime layer's role
   (Section 16.3.1), and even there it is bounded, not closed:
   preventing misuse of data within the authorized scope needs an
   information-flow layer this document does not define.

16.2.2.  Authority and Propagated Information

   Issuance gating bounds escalation by token acquisition (Section 7.1,
   Section 5.4), and the same bound holds for information: an agent can
   inherit another agent's knowledge, but not its authority.  A work
   product of one Mission is input, not authority, to an agent under
   another Mission, which acts on it only under its own Authority Set.
   Independent Missions communicating through shared state can still
   compose individually acceptable actions into behavior no single
   Mission authorized; the Mission Security Model
   ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-mission-security-model]) analyzes that
   coordination.

16.3.  Enforcement Boundaries

16.3.1.  Issuance Scope and Runtime Enforcement

   This document governs Authority Set derivation and the issuance of
   authority: it bounds what authority a Mission yields, binds it to the
   Approver's consent, and gates every issuance on Mission state.  It
   does not evaluate individual runtime actions.  In particular, it does
   not:

   *  evaluate a request's parameters against the Mission at the point
      of use;

   *  produce runtime enforcement evidence for each consequential
      action;

   *  bind tool or function identities to the Mission; or

   *  re-evaluate at execution time to close the approval-to-execution
      (time-of-check to time-of-use) gap.

   Within a token's lifetime, an active Mission can therefore become
   ambient authority for individual consequential actions.  Short token
   lifetimes and narrow authority bound this exposure but do not
   eliminate it.

   A deployment can use Missions for issuance alone
   ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-mission-architecture], Section "The Issuance-
   Only Deployment").  A runtime layer
   ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-mission-runtime]), out of scope for this
   document, evaluates each consequential action against the Mission,
   with parameter binding, and records evidence for the actions it
   covers.  A deployment adds one for an action class that needs any of
   the following, which issuance-time bounds alone do not provide:

   *  per-action evaluation or evidence;

   *  approval bound to a single action; or

   *  a bound the receiving resource server cannot enforce.

   Where the resource server or a composing runtime layer matches an
   operation target against a prefix entry, the Resource Boundary
   Canonicalization analysis of the Mission Resource Access Profile
   ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-resource-access]) gives the
   single-normalization rule for that match, for a deployment that
   supports that type.

   Short-lived access tokens are this document's issuance-only
   recommendation: with no runtime layer, token lifetime is the
   revocation-latency bound at unmodified resource servers.  Where a
   runtime layer covers the high-consequence classes with an active
   freshness source, the point-of-use decision is the revocation cutoff,
   and lifetimes can be sized by action class without losing that cutoff
   ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-mission-runtime]).

   An extended lifetime is appropriate only for a token whose carried
   entries are all on runtime-gated paths.  Classes attach to entries
   while exp attaches to the token, so a single ungated entry takes the
   extended lifetime as its revocation latency.  Narrowed, single-
   audience tokens (Section 5.4) keep gated and ungated authority from
   sharing one long-lived token.

16.3.2.  Denial Detail Disclosure

   The mission_denial attribute and the [RFC9470]
   insufficient_user_authentication challenge (Section 9.1) each tell a
   caller which path a denial leads into, and so reveal authorization
   shape.  An insufficient_user_authentication challenge confirms to the
   presenting party that the authority exists and only the Subject's
   authentication is weak or stale, while mission_denial:
   insufficient_authority denies that the authority exists.

   Introspection guards the same class of fact behind caller
   authorization (Section 9.2.1), and Section 9.1 limits the attribute
   to a token holder that the deployment permits to learn the
   distinction.  Of the two values, insufficient_authority reveals
   least, and omitting the attribute reveals nothing.

16.3.3.  Revocation Authority

   A deployment's policy can authorize no party to request a Mission's
   revocation (Section 10.2).  Under such a policy no external party can
   request revocation, so containing a Mission depends on its expiry and
   on any termination the deployment's policy drives.  No role, such as
   the Subject, the Approver, or an administrator, gains revocation
   authority by default, and the policy's choice does not remove the
   Mission Issuer's token-independent revocation means.

16.4.  Credentials and Delegation

16.4.1.  Token Theft

   Derived tokens are sender-constrained (DPoP [RFC9449] or mTLS
   [RFC8705]) where Section 7 and Section 8.2 require it.  A designation
   (Section 8.1) carries no confirmation key: like an authorization code
   issued to a client that authenticates, it is bound to the client that
   redeems it, which authenticates to do so (Section 8.2), and it
   authorizes no resource access.  A stolen designation is therefore
   useless without that client's credentials.  A stolen token is bounded
   by the Authority Set and the Mission lifetime regardless, but sender-
   constraint prevents replay by a different party.

16.4.2.  Delegation and Chain Compromise

   Delegation (Section 8) widens the set of parties holding Mission-
   derived authority.  Because authority only narrows down the chain, a
   compromised actor can act only within its narrowed
   authorization_details, for the lifetime of the token it holds.  The
   following limit this exposure:

   *  The per-entry delegation constraints (delegability, max_depth, and
      allowed_delegates, Section 8.4) bound it at approval time.
      max_depth bounds the length of a delegation chain, not its
      breadth: only allowed_delegates bounds fan-out to many distinct
      depth-1 delegates.

   *  Binding each delegated token to the delegate's own key
      (Section 8.2) confines a compromised delegate to its own
      credential.

   *  Short derived-token lifetimes (Section 7.1), and marking delegable
      only the entries that need delegation, keep it small.

   Audience replay into the exchange is a distinct path: a Mission-bound
   token obtained by or issued to one party, presented as a
   subject_token, could be exchanged for a fresh delegated credential
   bound to the presenter.  The following gates bound it:

   *  the exchange is a derivation gated on Mission state;

   *  the AS applies delegation-authorization policy at every exchange
      (Section 8.4);

   *  the result is bound to the authenticated delegate's own key and
      narrowed by the subset rule; and

   *  a no-actor exchange is accepted only from the Mission's approved
      agent (Section 7.5).

   Designation (Section 8.1) closes the remaining gap: a delegated
   exchange accepts only a designation naming the authenticated
   requesting client, which only the token's holder obtains, by proving
   possession of the token's key, so a token obtained from an audience
   is refused there.  Sender-constraining the primary token
   (Section 7.2) likewise keeps a stolen token from being designated or
   self-exchanged.

16.4.3.  Composition and the Effective Ceiling

   Delegation depth (Section 8.4) resets at each cross-domain hop
   ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-cross-domain]) and, where a
   deployment runs the child-delegation profile, at each child
   generation ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-child-delegation]),
   so the authority a Mission's descendants can reach can exceed what a
   single approval appears to bound at consent time.  Where that
   apparent bound must hold in practice, a deployment can impose a
   global cap out of band; the Mission Security Model
   ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-mission-security-model]) analyzes the
   composition.

16.4.4.  Client Attribution and Delegation Lineage

   Because this document keeps the ordinary [RFC9068] meaning of the
   client_id claim (Appendix C.3), a generic [RFC9068] resource server,
   or a logging, SIEM, or audit pipeline, that keys attribution on
   client_id attributes a Mission-derived token, delegated or not, to
   the correct requesting client.  Such a component cannot see the
   delegation lineage in the act chain (Section 8.2) or the Mission's
   originally-approved agent, which is recorded in the Mission Record
   (Section 6.3), not in client_id.

   An existing component that authorizes or logs solely from client_id
   needs review for this gap before it receives delegated Mission-bound
   tokens; Section 9.1 states what a resource server may not infer or
   route on client_id.

16.4.5.  Signing and Key Rotation

   The mission claim and authorization_details are covered by the AS's
   signature on the [RFC9068] JWT, so their integrity reduces to the
   AS's signing key, whose publication and rotation Section 7.2
   specifies.

   Verification for audit outlives validity: keeping a key resolvable
   for the audit horizon (Section 6.3.4) of every Mission whose tokens
   it signed lets an auditor verify those tokens later.  A companion
   that anchors a longer-lived artifact to the same keys (a status
   assertion, a Mandate, registered evidence) states its own retention
   bound.

   Revocation for a known or suspected compromise is distinct from
   routine retirement: the issuer publishes the compromised key as
   revoked, or marks it with a compromise time, rather than only
   rotating it out.

   A compromised issuer signing key voids every guarantee the signature
   carries.  Holding issuer signing keys in non-exportable, HSM- or KMS-
   grade custody with dual-controlled generation reduces that risk.

   Segmenting keys by artifact class under distinct kid values within
   the one jwks_uri lets high-value, low-volume signing (long-lived
   evidence and portable artifacts) sit under stricter custody than
   high-volume token signing.  Verification is kid-indexed, so this
   needs no wire change.

17.  Privacy Considerations

   The privacy considerations of Section 13 of [RFC9396], Section 8 of
   [RFC9126], and Section 6 of [RFC9068] apply to this document, as do
   those of Section 5 of [RFC7662] for introspection and Section 6 of
   [RFC8693] for delegation.

   A Mission Identifier is a correlation handle.  A deployment limits
   exposure by:

   *  giving stable Mission Identifiers only to parties that enforce,
      audit, or observe that Mission;

   *  preferring audience-scoped projections of authority where
      possible; and

   *  minimizing status and introspection disclosures to authorized
      callers (Section 9.2.1).

17.1.  Mission Identifier Correlation

   This document carries a single canonical Mission Identifier on every
   derived token, and cross-domain projection
   ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-cross-domain]) carries it across
   trust domains unchanged.  Any party that observes credentials for the
   same Mission, whether a resource server, a Resource AS, or an auditor
   spanning audiences, can correlate that activity by the Mission
   Identifier, and mission.issuer further identifies the issuing AS.

   That stable anchor lets a resource server, a cross-domain Resource
   AS, and an auditor bind credentials and evidence to one approved
   Mission.  This document does not provide cross-audience unlinkability
   (Appendix C.2).

   Audience-pairwise (or request-pairwise) Mission references, in which
   the issuer projects a distinct opaque identifier per audience and
   resolves them server-side, are the fuller mechanism for
   unlinkability; they work against the stable anchor, and this document
   does not define them.  A deployment that carries the canonical
   Mission Identifier on the wire accepts this correlation.

17.2.  Token Payload Disclosure

   The carried constraints and a multi-resource Authority Set disclose
   the shape of the task and its business bounds (for example, an amount
   ceiling) to every holder and every audience of a derived token.
   Single-audience tokens, one per resource server, minimize this: each
   carries only the entries the consuming resource server needs, as
   Section 2.3 of [RFC9700] recommends (Section 7.3).

17.3.  Intent Retention and Anchor Disclosure

   The Mission Record's Intent members (goal, task_bounds) are personal-
   data sinks: they carry whatever task description the user supplied,
   and their retention and erasure follow Section 17.5.

   The integrity anchors are unsalted commitments: a party holding a
   candidate Intent can confirm it against intent_hash, and a candidate
   proposal against proposal_hash.  Over low-entropy or guessable
   content each anchor is therefore a disclosure channel, and
   deployments treat it as one when the Intent itself is sensitive.

17.4.  Third-Party Data Subjects

   A task can be about a person who holds no Mission role: in a
   background check, the employer's agent queries a registrar about a
   candidate who is neither Subject nor Approver nor resource owner.

   Mission approval records the accountable Approver's authorization of
   the undertaking (Section 6.1).  It is not, by itself, evidence of
   such a person's consent to disclosure or of any other legal basis a
   disclosure requires.  Whether a basis is required, and what satisfies
   it, is deployment and legal policy out of scope for this document.

   Where the resource domain requires data-subject consent or another
   basis, that domain's own lane (the resource server, a gateway, a
   policy decision point, or an authorization server acting for the
   domain) evaluates it through its own mechanisms, such as claims
   gathering or a resource-domain consent artifact.  That lane refuses
   access while required evidence is absent or invalid, and Mission
   authority does not override the refusal.

   Mission authority is not the data subject's consent either.  A
   Mission Record can retain a verified consent reference or facts as
   submission_evidence (Section 6.3); those facts are provenance and
   policy input only, which the resource domain validates independently
   under its current disclosure policy.

   Third-party personal data can enter through any Intent, proposal,
   authority, or recorded-evidence member:

   *  the prose members (goal, task_bounds) and purpose;

   *  target_resources and any explicit member a companion profile
      defines (for example, the metering companion's consumption bounds,
      [I-D.draft-mcguinness-mission-metering]);

   *  any type-owned member of a proposed entry, and the derived
      Authority Set entries that reach tokens (Section 7); and

   *  submission_evidence facts, including a consent reference.

   Whatever the member, it:

   *  persists on the Mission Record for its audit horizon
      (Section 6.3.4);

   *  concentrates at the AS with the record (Section 17.5); and

   *  if committed, is confirmable through the unsalted anchors by any
      party holding a candidate value (Section 17.3).

   Referencing a third party through resource-scoped or pseudonymous
   identifiers, rather than identifying prose, minimizes this exposure.
   An opaque identifier is minimization, not anonymity, and personal-
   data obligations follow it.

17.5.  Mission Record and Evidence Access

   The Mission Record concentrates the task, its authority, and its
   principals at the AS.  Every evidence artifact joins on the Mission
   Identifier, so the join is a correlation surface equal to the
   identifier itself.

   Tokens carry references and authority, not the record: this document
   puts no goal, task_bounds, or other Intent content in a credential.
   An Intent Submission Evidence artifact can carry personal data (an
   originator identity, a consent reference); PAR keeps it off the front
   channel, and the record retains the verified facts selected for
   recording under the same access governance as the rest of the
   Mission's evidence.

   Access to the record and to Mission evidence is policy-governed and
   auditable: reading a Mission's evidence is a privileged operation,
   not a byproduct of holding a Mission reference.  Retention and
   erasure are deployment policy, bounded below by the audit horizon
   (Section 6.3.4).

   Where approval-event evidence was registered under the audit
   transparency profile ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-mission-audit]), its
   erasure record and data-subject-request basis are the transparency-
   side mechanism.  That mechanism records an erasure but neither
   performs one nor overrides retention law, and it leaves the
   operational Mission Record and its audit-horizon retention floor
   untouched.

18.  Internationalization Considerations

   Mission Intent prose (goal, task_bounds) is human-readable
   disclosure. goal_lang (Section 4) declares the language of that prose
   as a BCP 47 language tag [RFC5646], so an approval surface can
   render, translate, or route it without guessing the language.

   Three rules apply to the declaration:

   *  goal_lang is a syntactic declaration: the AS checks only its well-
      formedness (Section 4), not that the prose is in the declared
      language, so the tag is not a verified property of the text.

   *  Rendering to the Approver follows the rendering rules of
      Section 6.1.4 unchanged: client prose stays inert text in any
      language and any script, including bidirectional text.  Where the
      approval surface presents a translation, the rendered disclosure
      is what the deployment's consent evidence records (Mission Consent
      Evidence binds one locale, one disclosure, one hash,
      [I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-consent-evidence]); goal_lang
      declares the source's language and is not a record of what was
      rendered.

   *  Authority Set entries carry machine-facing identifiers (URIs,
      action strings, structured constraints), not prose, and this
      document adds no language-tagged display fields to them.
      Localizing how authority is explained is the approval surface's
      duty under Section 6.1, not a property of the committed set.

19.  IANA Considerations

19.1.  OAuth Parameters Registration

   This document requests registration of the following in the "OAuth
   Parameters" registry:

   *  Parameter name: mission_intent

   *  Parameter usage location: authorization request

   *  Change controller: IETF

   *  Specification document(s): this document, Section 4.2

   *  Parameter name: mission_id

   *  Parameter usage location: token response

   *  Change controller: IETF

   *  Specification document(s): this document, Section 7.3.1

   *  Parameter name: mission_error

   *  Parameter usage location: token response

   *  Change controller: IETF

   *  Specification document(s): this document, Section 7.1

   *  Parameter name: mission_expires_at

   *  Parameter usage location: token response

   *  Change controller: IETF

   *  Specification document(s): this document, Section 7.3.1

   PAR [RFC9126] carries authorization-request parameters without a
   distinct usage location, so the pushed submission of mission_intent
   needs no separate registration.

   The mission_error member is carried in the token-endpoint error
   response, for which "token response" is the registry's applicable
   usage location.  It uses the error response's JSON extensibility
   rather than defining a new error code.

   The mission_denial attribute uses the extensible auth-param space of
   the WWW-Authenticate scheme ([RFC6750], Section 9.1), for which no
   IANA registry exists; no action is required for it.

19.2.  OAuth Extensions Error Registration

   This document requests registration of the following in the "OAuth
   Extensions Error" registry [RFC6749]:

   *  Error name: invalid_mission_intent_evidence

   *  Error usage location: authorization code grant error response,
      token error response

   *  Related protocol extension: Intent Submission Evidence
      (Section 4.4)

   *  Change controller: IETF

   *  Specification document(s): this document, Section 4.4

   A code distinct from invalid_request lets a client tell a malformed
   submission from missing or untrusted evidence, and remedy each
   differently.

19.3.  JSON Web Token Claims Registration

   This document requests registration of the following in the "JSON Web
   Token Claims" registry:

   *  Claim Name: mission

   *  Claim Description: Reference to the Mission a token was derived
      under.

   *  Change Controller: IETF

   *  Specification Document(s): this document, Section 7.8

   The mission claim (Section 7.8) references a Mission, the
   authorization object this document defines.  It is distinct from
   AAuth's mission_s256 claim ([I-D.draft-hardt-oauth-aauth-protocol]),
   the SHA-256 digest that identifies an AAuth mission, and from the
   agent's mission that [I-D.draft-ietf-wimse-aims] names, the purpose
   this document translates into authorization.  The two claim names do
   not collide in that registry.

19.4.  OAuth Token Introspection Response Registration

   This document requests registration of the following in the "OAuth
   Token Introspection Response" registry ([RFC7662]):

   *  Name: mission

   *  Description: The Mission a token was derived under, with its
      current lifecycle state when returned by the Mission's issuer
      (Section 9.2).

   *  Change Controller: IETF

   *  Specification Document(s): this document, Section 9.2

19.5.  OAuth Authorization Server Metadata Registration

   This document requests registration of the following in the "OAuth
   Authorization Server Metadata" registry ([RFC8414]):

   *  Metadata Name: mission_bound_authorization_supported

   *  Metadata Description: Boolean indicating that the authorization
      server supports the Mission Issuer core surfaces of this document.

   *  Change Controller: IETF

   *  Specification Document(s): this document, Section 12

19.6.  OAuth Protected Resource Metadata Registration

   This document requests registration of the following in the "OAuth
   Protected Resource Metadata" registry ([RFC9728]):

   *  Metadata Name: mission_bound_authorization_required

   *  Metadata Description: Boolean indicating that the protected
      resource accepts only Mission-bound tokens.

   *  Change Controller: IETF

   *  Specification Document(s): this document, Section 13

19.7.  Mission Lifecycle States Registry

   IANA is requested to create the "Mission Lifecycle States" registry.
   The registration policy is Specification Required [RFC8126].

   A Designated Expert reviews a submission for the discipline
   Section 10 requires:

   *  a Value matching ^[a-z][a-z0-9_]*$ not already registered;

   *  a Terminal value of no, consistent with the transitions the
      registrant's specification defines (a no state admits a further
      transition; terminated is the one terminal state, and a further
      way for a Mission to end is a termination reason, Section 19.8);
      and

   *  a Semantics sentence precise enough to distinguish the state from
      every registered state.

   Whether a Mission in any state is available for reliance is fixed by
   the governing rule below, never per row.

   Only the exact value active permits token derivation or continued
   reliance; a consumer treats every other value, including one it does
   not recognize, as non-active and never widens on it (Section 10).  A
   Designated Expert MUST reject a registration whose governing
   specification attempts to redefine this interaction rather than
   adding a new value bound by it.

   Each registration records:

   *  *Value*: the lifecycle state's string value.

   *  *Terminal*: yes if the state admits no further transition, no
      otherwise.

   *  *Semantics*: one sentence stating what the state means and, for a
      non-terminal state, what a Mission in that state cannot do.

   *  *Change Controller*: IETF, or the registrant for any other
      registration.

   *  *Reference*: the specification defining the state.

   This document seeds the registry with the states it defines:

   +============+==========+================+============+=============+
   | Value      | Terminal | Semantics      | Change     | Reference   |
   |            |          |                | Controller |             |
   +============+==========+================+============+=============+
   | active     | no       | The only state | IETF       | this        |
   |            |          | from which     |            | document,   |
   |            |          | tokens are     |            | Section     |
   |            |          | derived.       |            | 10          |
   +------------+----------+----------------+------------+-------------+
   | terminated | yes      | The Mission    | IETF       | this        |
   |            |          | has ended; its |            | document,   |
   |            |          | termination    |            | Section     |
   |            |          | gives the      |            | 10,         |
   |            |          | reason.        |            | Section     |
   |            |          |                |            | 10.1        |
   +------------+----------+----------------+------------+-------------+

        Table 7: Mission Lifecycle States registry: initial contents

   Each further document that defines a lifecycle state requests that
   state's registration in its own IANA considerations.  A Designated
   Expert MUST reject a further state registered as terminal: a further
   way for a Mission to end is a termination reason (Section 19.8).

19.8.  Mission Termination Reasons Registry

   IANA is requested to create the "Mission Termination Reasons"
   registry.  The registration policy is Specification Required
   [RFC8126].

   A Designated Expert reviews a submission for:

   *  a Value matching ^[a-z][a-z0-9_]*$ not already registered;

   *  a Semantics sentence precise enough to distinguish the reason from
      every registered reason; and

   *  a Members list naming each further termination member the reason
      defines (Section 10.1) with its presence (REQUIRED, CONDITIONAL,
      or OPTIONAL).

   A reason never makes a Mission available for reliance: every
   terminated Mission is non-active, whatever its reason (Section 10).

   Each registration records:

   *  *Value*: the termination reason's string value.

   *  *Semantics*: one sentence stating how the Mission ended.

   *  *Members*: the further termination members, or "none".

   *  *Change Controller*: IETF, or the registrant for any other
      registration.

   *  *Reference*: the specification defining the reason.

   This document seeds the registry with the reasons it defines:

   +=========+======================+=========+============+===========+
   | Value   | Semantics            | Members | Change     | Reference |
   |         |                      |         | Controller |           |
   +=========+======================+=========+============+===========+
   | revoked | An authorized        | none    | IETF       | this      |
   |         | party revoked the    |         |            | document, |
   |         | Mission, or the      |         |            | Section   |
   |         | deployment's         |         |            | 10.1      |
   |         | policy terminated    |         |            |           |
   |         | it (Section 10.2).   |         |            |           |
   +---------+----------------------+---------+------------+-----------+
   | expired | The Mission's        | none    | IETF       | this      |
   |         | expires_at passed;   |         |            | document, |
   |         | terminated_at        |         |            | Section   |
   |         | equals it.           |         |            | 10.1      |
   +---------+----------------------+---------+------------+-----------+

      Table 8: Mission Termination Reasons registry: initial contents

   Each further document that defines a termination reason requests that
   reason's registration in its own IANA considerations.

19.9.  Mission Intent Members Registry

   IANA is requested to create the "Mission Intent Members" registry.
   The registration policy is Specification Required [RFC8126].

   A Designated Expert reviews a submission for:

   *  a Name not already registered by a different owning specification;
      and

   *  a Semantics sentence naming what the member means and which
      specification defines its schema, production, and enforcement in
      full.

   The registry resolves ownership of a short top-level name, so that
   two independently implemented specifications cannot assign it
   incompatible schemas.  Registration defines no member semantics and
   confers no authority (Section 14).  A companion profile can instead
   use a collision-resistant name without registering it.

   Each registration records:

   *  *Name*: the member's top-level key in the Mission Intent.

   *  *Status*: stable or experimental.  An experimental entry's owning
      specification has not completed that specification's own promotion
      criteria for the member.  A Designated Expert MUST NOT register a
      member as stable without confirming that its owning
      specification's promotion criteria are met, and MUST NOT treat
      registration itself as a promotion event for an experimental
      member.

   *  *Semantics*: one sentence stating what the member means and
      pointing to the section that fully defines it.

   *  *Change Controller*: IETF, or the registrant for any other
      registration.

   *  *Reference*: the specification defining the member.

   This document seeds the registry with the members it defines itself:

    +================+======+====================+==========+=========+
    |Name            |Status|Semantics           |Change    |Reference|
    |                |      |                    |Controller|         |
    +================+======+====================+==========+=========+
    |goal            |stable|The Mission's plain-|IETF      |this     |
    |                |      |language objective. |          |document,|
    |                |      |                    |          |Section 4|
    +----------------+------+--------------------+----------+---------+
    |goal_lang       |stable|BCP 47 language tag |IETF      |this     |
    |                |      |for goal.           |          |document,|
    |                |      |                    |          |Section 4|
    +----------------+------+--------------------+----------+---------+
    |target_resources|stable|Client-requested    |IETF      |this     |
    |                |      |derivation ceiling  |          |document,|
    |                |      |and configured-     |          |Section 4|
    |                |      |mapping lookup key. |          |         |
    +----------------+------+--------------------+----------+---------+
    |task_bounds     |stable|Non-machine-readable|IETF      |this     |
    |                |      |prose bounds on the |          |document,|
    |                |      |task.               |          |Section 4|
    +----------------+------+--------------------+----------+---------+
    |purpose         |stable|URI identifying the |IETF      |this     |
    |                |      |task's purpose.     |          |document,|
    |                |      |                    |          |Section 4|
    +----------------+------+--------------------+----------+---------+
    |expires_at      |stable|Requested Mission   |IETF      |this     |
    |                |      |expiry ceiling.     |          |document,|
    |                |      |                    |          |Section 4|
    +----------------+------+--------------------+----------+---------+

                Table 9: Core-defined Mission Intent members

   Each further document that defines a Mission Intent member requests
   that member's registration in its own IANA considerations.

Appendix A.  End-to-End Example

   This appendix follows one Mission in a single trust domain through
   Mission creation, token issuance, resource server enforcement, a
   refusal, and revocation.  It is illustrative and adds no
   requirements.  The OAuth steps follow this document.  Identifiers are
   illustrative; the hash values are computed over the displayed objects
   (Section 6.4.1).

   The OAuth flow of Stages 1 to 4, and the revocation in Stage 5, use
   only this document and the mission_resource_access type
   ([I-D.draft-mcguinness-oauth-mission-resource-access]): the baseline
   issuance path, with enforcement from the token alone, bounded by
   token lifetime.

   Scenario: agent s6BhdRkqt3, acting for alice (user_3p2q8mN1a0kV7tR),
   reconciles Q3 invoices in the home ERP under Mission
   msn_8RfX2Lqv9TqMv4z7sA2bN1k0YpEdHc9-.

A.1.  Stage 1: Mission Creation

   The following example shows the Submission envelope the agent pushes
   through PAR (Section 4.2), carrying the Mission Intent and no
   evidence:

   =============== NOTE: '\' line wrapping per RFC 8792 ===============

   {
     "intent": {
       "goal": "Reconcile Q3 invoices and post adjustments of at most \
         USD 500 per journal entry.",
       "target_resources": ["https://erp.example.com"],
       "task_bounds": [
         "Read only invoices issued in 2026-Q3.",
         "Post journal entries of at most USD 500 each."
       ],
       "purpose": "urn:example:purpose:reconcile",
       "expires_at": "2026-12-31T23:59:59Z"
     }
   }

   In the same push, the agent proposes concrete authority on the
   authorization_details parameter (Section 4.3):

   [
     { "type": "mission_resource_access",
       "resource": "https://erp.example.com",
       "actions": ["invoices.*"],
       "constraints": {
         "resource_issued_after": "2026-07-01T00:00:00Z",
         "resource_issued_before": "2026-09-30T23:59:59Z"
       },
       "delegation": {
         "max_depth": 2,
         "allowed_delegates": [{ "sub_profile": "ai_agent" }]
       } },
     { "type": "mission_resource_access",
       "resource": "https://erp.example.com",
       "actions": ["journal-entries.write"],
       "constraints": {
         "max_amount": { "amount": "500.00", "currency": "USD" }
       } }
   ]

   Both are parameters of one pushed authorization request ([RFC9126]),
   with PKCE ([RFC7636]); client authentication is omitted, and extra
   line breaks are for display purposes only:

   POST /par HTTP/1.1
   Host: as.example.com
   Content-Type: application/x-www-form-urlencoded

   response_type=code
   &client_id=s6BhdRkqt3
   &redirect_uri=https%3A%2F%2Fagent.example.com%2Fcb
   &code_challenge=E9Melhoa2OwvFrEMTJguCHaoeK1t8URWbuGJSstw-cM
   &code_challenge_method=S256
   &mission_intent=%7B%22intent%22%3A%7B%22goal%22%3A%22Reconcile...
   &authorization_details=%5B%7B%22type%22%3A%22mission_resource_acc...

   The AS returns a request_uri:

   HTTP/1.1 201 Created
   Content-Type: application/json
   Cache-Control: no-store

   {
     "request_uri": "urn:ietf:params:oauth:request_uri:6esc_11ACC5bwc",
     "expires_in": 60
   }

   and the agent sends alice's user agent to the authorization endpoint
   with it:

   GET /authorize?client_id=s6BhdRkqt3&request_uri=urn%3Aietf%3Aparams
     %3Aoauth%3Arequest_uri%3A6esc_11ACC5bwc HTTP/1.1
   Host: as.example.com

   The AS (as.example.com) validates both, derives this Authority Set
   under a declared narrowing capability (each entry a same-type subset
   of a proposed entry, Section 4.3, Section 5.5.1), and renders it for
   alice's consent:

   [
     { "type": "mission_resource_access",
       "resource": "https://erp.example.com",
       "actions": ["invoices.read"],
       "constraints": {
         "resource_issued_after": "2026-07-01T00:00:00Z",
         "resource_issued_before": "2026-09-30T23:59:59Z"
       },
       "delegation": {
         "max_depth": 2,
         "allowed_delegates": [{ "sub_profile": "ai_agent" }]
       } },
     { "type": "mission_resource_access",
       "resource": "https://erp.example.com",
       "actions": ["journal-entries.write"],
       "constraints": {
         "max_amount": { "amount": "500.00", "currency": "USD" }
       } }
   ]

   alice approves at 08:00:00 UTC on 2026-11-02, and the AS records
   Mission msn_8RfX2Lqv9TqMv4z7sA2bN1k0YpEdHc9- in the active state with
   authority_hash sha-256:hjXlwWBY64-RPs1Z-E_gbyklNcViI5c9jiyMbE4KvFM,
   intent_hash sha-256:6T-7s_f-mNW9UpfrrZh3r0HRJmU4My_EsNAf1C1U4r0, and
   proposal_hash sha-256:p-s-EzdNQTn6RoPHXESkwVtGdk8yL1PxryNyMwroKGo.

A.2.  Stage 2: Mission-Bound Token Issuance

   At 08:00:05 the agent redeems the authorization code with its PKCE
   verifier and a DPoP proof ([RFC9449]):

   POST /token HTTP/1.1
   Host: as.example.com
   Content-Type: application/x-www-form-urlencoded
   DPoP: eyJ0eXAiOiJkcG9wK2p3dCIsImFsZyI6IkVTMjU2Iiwi...

   grant_type=authorization_code
   &code=SplxlOBeZQQYbYS6WxSbIA
   &redirect_uri=https%3A%2F%2Fagent.example.com%2Fcb
   &code_verifier=dBjftJeZ4CVP-mB92K27uhbUJU1p1r_wW1gFWFOEjXk

   The AS resolves the Mission from the grant (Section 6.5.1), checks
   that it is active (Section 7.1), and issues a Mission-bound access
   token for the ERP and a refresh token.  The token response carries
   the granted authorization_details echo (Section 7.3) and the
   mission_id and mission_expires_at response parameters
   (Section 7.3.1):

   {
     "access_token": "eyJhbGciOiJFUzI1NiIsInR5cCI6ImF0K2p3dCJ9...",
     "token_type": "DPoP",
     "expires_in": 300,
     "refresh_token": "rt_4mN8qV2xP7sL1tY9zB3k",
     "mission_id": "msn_8RfX2Lqv9TqMv4z7sA2bN1k0YpEdHc9-",
     "mission_expires_at": "2026-12-31T23:59:59Z",
     "authorization_details": [
       { "type": "mission_resource_access",
         "resource": "https://erp.example.com",
         "actions": ["invoices.read"],
         "constraints": {
           "resource_issued_after": "2026-07-01T00:00:00Z",
           "resource_issued_before": "2026-09-30T23:59:59Z"
         },
         "delegation": {
           "max_depth": 2,
           "allowed_delegates": [{ "sub_profile": "ai_agent" }]
         } },
       { "type": "mission_resource_access",
         "resource": "https://erp.example.com",
         "actions": ["journal-entries.write"],
         "constraints": {
           "max_amount": { "amount": "500.00", "currency": "USD" }
         } }
     ]
   }

   The following is an example of the decoded access token payload:

   {
     "iss": "https://as.example.com",
     "sub": "user_3p2q8mN1a0kV7tR",
     "aud": "https://erp.example.com",
     "client_id": "s6BhdRkqt3",
     "iat": 1793606405,
     "exp": 1793606705,
     "jti": "at_9Kp2vN7sR1tY8mZ3qX5b",
     "authorization_details": [
       { "type": "mission_resource_access",
         "resource": "https://erp.example.com",
         "actions": ["invoices.read"],
         "constraints": {
           "resource_issued_after": "2026-07-01T00:00:00Z",
           "resource_issued_before": "2026-09-30T23:59:59Z"
         },
         "delegation": {
           "max_depth": 2,
           "allowed_delegates": [{ "sub_profile": "ai_agent" }]
         } },
       { "type": "mission_resource_access",
         "resource": "https://erp.example.com",
         "actions": ["journal-entries.write"],
         "constraints": {
           "max_amount": { "amount": "500.00", "currency": "USD" }
         } }
     ],
     "cnf": { "jkt": "0ZcOCORZNYy-DWpqq30jZyJGHTN0d2HglBV3uiguA4I" },
     "mission": {
       "id": "msn_8RfX2Lqv9TqMv4z7sA2bN1k0YpEdHc9-",
       "issuer": "https://as.example.com"
     }
   }

   The token's authorization_details claim carries the same two entries
   as the token response's echo.  The token carries everything
   enforcement needs: the audience, the sender constraint (the cnf
   claim), the authority with its constraints, and the mission claim
   naming the Mission it was derived under.  Its 300-second lifetime
   ends well before the Mission's expires_at.  Revoking the Mission
   stops further derivation, but this token remains valid until its own
   exp (Section 10.2).

A.3.  Stage 3: Resource Server Enforcement

   The agent calls the ERP resource server (erp.example.com) with that
   token.  The resource server:

   *  validates the JWT and verifies the request's DPoP proof against
      the key identified by the token's cnf claim;

   *  enforces the authorization_details entries whose resource matches
      the operation target, https://erp.example.com, permitting
      invoices.read within the Q3 issuance window and journal-
      entries.write up to the max_amount ceiling of 500.00 USD
      (Section 9.1); and

   *  treats the mission claim as audit and correlation context, making
      no call to the AS.

   With enforcement from the token alone, the consequential journal-
   entries.write is bounded only by token lifetime and the carried
   constraints.

   At 08:14:00 the agent refreshes for a write-only token, narrowed to
   the journal-entries.write entry, as the refresh example in
   Section 7.3 narrows to the read entry.

A.4.  Stage 4: Resource Server Refusal

   The agent then asks the ERP to post a 780.00 USD adjustment with the
   same write-only token.  The amount exceeds the entry's max_amount of
   500.00 USD, so the ERP refuses it from the token alone as outside the
   carried authority (Section 9.1).  This deployment accepts the agent
   learning that more authority needs a new approval or an expansion
   (Section 16.3.2), so the challenge carries mission_denial:

   HTTP/1.1 403 Forbidden
   WWW-Authenticate: DPoP error="insufficient_scope",
     mission_denial="insufficient_authority"

   A fresh token from the same Mission does not help: every token it
   derives is a subset of the same Authority Set (Section 5.4).

A.5.  Stage 5: Revocation

   At 08:50:00 the agent refreshes for the read-only token of the
   refresh example in Section 7.3, which expires at 08:55:00.

   At 08:53:20 the Mission is revoked, and the AS commits terminated
   with reason revoked.  Revocation stops the Mission at the issuer
   (Section 10.2).  A later refresh is refused with the mission_error
   diagnostic (Section 7.1):

   HTTP/1.1 400 Bad Request
   Content-Type: application/json
   Cache-Control: no-store

   {
     "error": "invalid_grant",
     "mission_error": "revoked"
   }

   Introspection reports the 08:50:00 token inactive with the Mission's
   termination, although the token has not expired (Section 9.2.5,
   Section 9.2.2).

   Revocation does not recall the token already issued.  The ERP,
   enforcing from the token alone, can honor the read-only token until
   its exp at 08:55:00.  A resource server that introspects at the
   issuer on every request stops honoring it on its next request.

Appendix B.  Deployment Choices

   The following table lists the choices this document leaves to a
   deployment, who makes each, and, for each party that needs to learn
   it, the existing signal, if any, from which that party learns it.  A
   signal serves only the party entitled to read it: a client treats an
   access token as opaque (Section 6 of [RFC9068]), so a token's header
   and claims are resource-server signals.  Where no signal exists, the
   table says so, and the party relies on the deployment's documentation
   or configuration.  The cited sections govern.
